## Department_of_State_v_Legal_Assistance_for_Vietnamese_Asylum_Seekers_post_grant_remand_1996-10-21.md

**Case and dockets:** Department of State v. Legal Assistance for Vietnamese Asylum Seekers, Inc.; No. 95-1521
**Event and date:** Post-grant vacatur and remand; 1996-10-21; October Term 1996.
**Result:** The D.C. Circuit judgment is vacated and the case remanded for consideration of IIRIRA §633, unanimously.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and independently frozen modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event
Department of State v. Legal Assistance for Vietnamese Asylum Seekers, Inc., No. 95-1521. Argued October 15, 1996; decided October 21, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit. The decisions under review, 45 F.3d 469 and 74 F.3d 1308, concern the Department's refusal to process immigrant-visa applications in Hong Kong for Vietnamese applicants screened out of refugee status. The question is whether the policy violates 8 U.S.C. §1152(a)(1), and how the intervening enactment of IIRIRA §633 affects the statutory premise, live claims and available relief. The Government seeks vacatur or reversal; respondents seek affirmance or dismissal of the writ as improvidently granted. This is an argued post-grant disposition, not an asylum-eligibility proceeding.

## Opinion Topology
Justice Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Its common ground concerns an actual intervening enactment and the specific equities of this remand; it adopts no new rule for changed Executive advocacy. No separate writing issues.

**Reconciliation finding:** No supported historical departure is retained in judgment or disclosed-writing topology. The revisions rest on reweighing the existing lawful alternatives against the actual enacted development and remedy limits, with the historically reported procedural action as confirming evidence. They do not infer an undisclosed historical tally. No Justice now requires a separate writing; publication and assignment remain for assembly under its own rules.
### Assignment and final compatibility

The Chief belongs to the unanimous vacatur coalition and assigns the coordinated opinion to Stevens. Fit governs: the Associates' sufficient ground is already settled. Stevens's Landgraf/Rivers authorship directly demonstrates the central work of separating new consequences for completed conduct from future regulation; his Lawrence concurrence supports a case-specific equitable assessment rather than enactment-only relief. His reconciled commitment now accepts this bounded remand, and the opinion preserves the matured-claim and practical-danger concerns that made the temporal/remedial distinction necessary. Souter's joins in those cases and Stone's precise approved remand are strong alternatives, but Stevens has the closest demonstrated authorship on the difficult temporal boundary. His initial DIG preference is superseded and creates no present obstacle; authorship does not invent a new circulation conversion. No historical author is imported. The preceding-term assignment baseline reports 101 named Chief assignments, maximum 27 to O'Connor, so the consecutive-term ceiling bars no author. This earliest OT1996 Court opinion is Chief assignment 1 and Stevens's first; it counts once.

Final compatibility: all eight Associates retain the ready reconciliation's vacatur vote and bounded ground. Stevens and Ginsburg's prior DIG objections were resolved at reconciliation, not by exposure to Stone. The present opinion expressly weighs the asserted danger and preserves matured/reparative and independent claims, so their remaining boundaries are met. O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer receive probability plus independent equities, not enactment-only vacatur. Scalia and Thomas join the actual-enactment disposition and its case-specific sufficient ground without adopting Lawrence's disputed agency-advocacy extension or the Landgraf majority's general organizing method. The opinion references governing temporal law and reserves application; it does not require those broader joins. Nine judgment votes and nine direct joins support the stated holding. No Marks analysis or new reconciliation audit is required.


## California_v_Roy_summary_review_1996-11-04.md

**Case and dockets:** California v. Roy, No. 95-2025; October Term 1996, chunk 1.
**Event and date:** Summary merits disposition, 1996-11-04; certiorari granted, judgment vacated and remanded.
**Result:** Vacated and remanded, 6–3; Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer support; Stevens, Scalia and Ginsburg would affirm. Souter writes Parts I–III with the distinct joins specified below.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and frozen independent modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event
California v. Roy, No. 95-2025. Summary merits disposition, November 4, 1996. On petition for a writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, reviewing its en banc habeas judgment in Roy v. Gomez, 81 F.3d 863. No oral argument is held in this Court. The questions concern whether omission of an aider and abettor's purpose to facilitate robbery is structural or trial error, the harmlessness burden and permissible method on federal habeas, and the antecedent objection that the named petitioners were not parties below. California seeks vacatur or reversal; Roy seeks rejection of review or retention of the habeas-relief judgment.

The trial charge required knowledge of the perpetrator's unlawful purpose and affirmative assistance, excluding mere presence, but omitted Roy's purpose to facilitate the robbery. The jury returned Mannix robbery and first-degree felony-murder convictions and a knife-possession finding without personal knife use. It also convicted Roy of Clark second-degree murder with personal knife use and acquitted him of Clark robbery. The state court reversed robbery-murder and multiple-murder special circumstances and their sentence while otherwise affirming. This federal challenge concerns only the two Mannix convictions. The state and district courts found harmlessness through their functional-equivalence assessments; the en banc Ninth Circuit expressly reached the contrary Chapman conclusion before its separate collateral-standard analysis.

| Opinion of the Court, Part III | Souter | O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Vacatur and remand | Qualified actual-verdict method, application limits, preservation and remand directions |

| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm; joins Parts I and II | Record-specific failure of the State's Chapman showing without an exclusive-findings rule |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Ginsburg | Would affirm; both join Parts I and II | Actual-findings constraint for this omitted-purpose error and failure to establish its required connection |

Stone-Zsela agrees with rejection of the party-status objection without adopting its rationale. Five Justices directly join Part III; the judgment's sixth vote does not enlarge that opinion coalition.

## Judgment, opinion formation and assignment

The single final merits disposition concerning both Mannix convictions is vacatur/remand, six votes to three. The result does not mix reversal of one conviction with affirmance of another. All eight Associates' reconciled reach commitments remain intact, and Stone's fallback gives nine votes to reject the party objection and reach. Part I's explanation has eight direct joins; Part II has nine; Part III has five. Stone's method/remedy support is not silently counted as a sixth join in Part III. Stevens's distinct affirmance rationale is not combined with Scalia and Ginsburg to create an exclusive-findings majority. Every controlling rationale has five or more direct joins; no Marks construction is needed.

**Chief assignment:** Stone is in the six-Justice judgment majority and therefore assigns the single coordinated opinion even though he does not join all its reasoning. He assigns it to **Souter**. The common five-Associate method is settled with the express Yates qualification; that is a recorded join boundary, not a numerical or stylistic reason. Souter's own Yates authorship gives him the concrete advantage in explaining the connection between instructional focus, permissible evidence and the actual verdict. His current Brecht/O'Neal joins directly support the burden and remedy. O'Connor, author of current O'Neal, is the strongest alternative for the category and remedial aspects; Stone's approved position supplies the same broad method/remedy objective and fits their separation. Souter offers the stronger fit for the particular method qualification, the principal work distinguishing this case. No new accommodation is manufactured after reconciliation and no supposed draft exchange is narrated. Workload does not decide the assignment.

The preceding-term assignment baseline and mechanically copied actual assignment statements establish 101 named Chief assignments, largest recipients O'Connor 27 and Souter 23; no recipient exceeded half. Current-term LAVAS is Chief assignment 1 to Stevens. This is **Chief assignment 2**, **Souter's first** this term; the current total is Stevens 1, Souter 1, all others 0. No two-consecutive-term concentration ceiling applies. No historical per curiam authorship is imported. Supplied assignment statements, not future case processing, establish the count; Git existence is unverified under the no-Git instruction.

## Lopez_v_Monterey_County_merits_1996-11-06.md

**Case and dockets:** Lopez v. Monterey County; No. 95-1201
**Event and date:** Merits; 1996-11-06; October Term 1996.
**Result:** The district court judgment is reversed and the case remanded for enforcement of §5 and lawful interim proceedings, unanimously.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and frozen independent modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event
Lopez v. Monterey County, No. 95-1201. Argued October 8, 1996; decided November 6, 1996. On appeal from the three-judge United States District Court for the Northern District of California. The court authorized March 1996 municipal-court elections under a countywide, at-large system without preclearance of all operative changes and joined California for further litigation about the source of the system. Review concerns whether a covered County must preclear state-mandated changes despite having no policy discretion; the direct voting change, designation and prospective-laches defenses; and the limits on an enforcement court's use of an unprecleared governmental plan and a wholly court-developed temporary remedy. The plaintiffs seek reversal, prospective nonuse and lawful preclearance or interim proceedings.

## Opinion Topology
Justice Kennedy delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Its Parts I–IV correspond to the four controlling holdings below. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela files a concurrence, joined by no other Justice, adding his constitutional response to the designation objection and specifying his boundary for a wholly judicial plan.

### Assignment and final compatibility

The Chief joins each unanimous judgment component and assigns one coordinated opinion to Kennedy. Fit controls, with no unresolved necessary accommodation. Kennedy's own Presley opinion supplies the direct-voting/internal-governance distinction, and his Clark opinion supplies the precise nonuse, submission and remedial separation this case requires. His Morse private-association distinction is fully compatible with this governmental County; his Johnson disagreement is preserved as disagreement with governing law, not made a new defect in the temporary plan. Souter's Morse opinion and Stone's detailed approved construction are strong alternatives, but Kennedy has demonstrated authorship on both central established boundaries and can carry the new enact/administer construction within them. Historical Lopez authorship supplies no evidence. The prior-term baseline has no recipient over half, so no concentration ceiling excludes Kennedy. This is one Chief assignment to Kennedy; its chronological ordinal remains for parent integration after Roy. The concurrence adds no Court assignment.

Every Associate joins Parts I–IV exactly as limited above. The narrow public-government rule answers Scalia and Thomas's Morse objections without changing their private-association views. Preserving coverage, merits and remedial authority as separate questions meets Kennedy, O'Connor, Souter and Thomas's boundaries. Preserving the actual temporary plan as an option while withholding automatic validity answers Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer's concern and is supported by the governing Johnson rule. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas apply Johnson without joining its underlying constitutional rationale anew. Stone's complete County-duty, exact-submission, nonuse and independently supported temporary-remedy position fits the shared text. His additional facial-constitutional answer and exact material-policy formulation are recorded separately because the freeze does not establish eight Associate joins to those additional propositions. No silent Stone component needs fallback. All nine vote to reverse and remand; all nine directly join every Court holding. No Marks aggregation or post-reconciliation commitment change occurs.

**Case and dockets:** INS v. Yueh-Shaio Yang, No. 95-938; October Term 1996.

## Event

INS v. Yueh-Shaio Yang, No. 95-938. October Term 1996. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit. Argued October 15, 1996; decided November 13, 1996.

The Ninth Circuit's June 22, 1995 judgment vacated the Board of Immigration Appeals' discretionary waiver denial and required reconsideration excluding fraud it regarded as intertwined with entry; rehearing was denied October 5, 1995. The INS seeks reversal, and Yang seeks affirmance of the restriction on discretionary consideration. The question is whether former §1251(a)(1)(H) permits consideration of immigration fraud connected with and extending beyond the entry fraud that made the applicant deportable. The corrected 1996 immigration-review provisions also require a threshold determination of review authority.

## Ohio_v_Robinette_merits_1996-11-18.md

**Case and dockets:** Ohio v. Robinette; No. 95-891
**Event and date:** Merits; 1996-11-18; October Term 1996.
**Result:** The Ohio Supreme Court judgment is reversed and remanded, 7–2; Stone-Zsela would vacate and remand, and Stevens would affirm.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and frozen independent modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event
Ohio v. Robinette, No. 95-891. Argued October 8, 1996; decided November 18, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Ohio. Ohio seeks reversal of suppression and reinstatement of the conviction; Robinette seeks affirmance or a totality-based remand. The questions concern a categorical warning that a traffic detention has ended, the actual transition to a consensual encounter, and validity of consent. The Ohio judgment invoked the Fourth Amendment and Article I, §14 of the Ohio Constitution, held the detention unlawful and unattenuated, and separately imposed a release-warning rule. Both grounds are before the Court.

## Opinion Topology
| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Reversal and remand | Entire opinion: Parts I–IV |
| Partial joins in the Court opinion | Stone-Zsela | — | Would vacate and remand | Part II.B, objective continued-detention inquiry; Part III, no mandatory federal script and totality consent; Part IV's remand inquiries and limits, excluding reversal as the formal disposition |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Vacatur and remand | Preserves actual encounter analysis and his distinct formal remedy; adds no ground on Part I or Part II.A |
| Opinion concurring in the judgment | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Reversal and remand | Accepts review under Long and the federal no-script/totality correction; emphasizes independent Ohio law and reserves actual search validity |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Affirmance of suppression | Accepts review and rejects an indispensable federal script, but finds continued unlawful detention and no attenuation on this record |

Parts I and II.A have six direct joins. Part II.B, Part III and the identified remand framework in Part IV have seven direct joins. Ginsburg's judgment concurrence and Stevens's agreement on the absence of a federal script are not counted as whole-opinion joins. No controlling rationale depends on combining separate opinions.

### Assignment and final compatibility

The actual reversal/remand coalition consists of O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stone's approved formal judgment is vacatur/remand; Stevens would affirm. The Chief therefore does not assign the opinion for the reversal judgment. O'Connor, the most senior participating Associate in that coalition, assigns it to herself. Her Bostick authorship directly equips her to explain objective encounter classification without a categorical warning or seizure substitute; her Ornelas authorship provides demonstrated care separating supported facts and legal sufficiency, while the opinion respects Scalia's limit against converting Ornelas into a universal consent-review rule. Her Whren and Evans positions support the threshold and objective-motive correction. Kennedy's Evans opinion and Thomas's Whren opinion provide strong alternatives, but the core unresolved encounter problem particularly fits O'Connor's authored Bostick analysis. Ginsburg's separate state-law explanation does not require a new necessary accommodation to obtain the six direct whole-opinion joins. This is an Associate assignment, adds zero to the Chief's census, and does not impose the Chief's preferences on the assignor. Historical Robinette authorship and joins are not assignment premises.

The six whole-opinion Associates retain all reconciled limits: O'Connor requires the actual transition and trial findings; Scalia declines a universal Ornelas extension; Kennedy preserves Mimms's duration boundary; Souter requires a distinct causal inquiry; Thomas rejects both motive-based invalidity and an automatic end-of-seizure rule; Breyer preserves supported findings without conviction reinstatement. The draft satisfies all six without inventing a circulation revision. Ginsburg remains a judgment-only concurrence, accepts the identified legal correction and explains state-law freedom; she is not promoted to a Court-opinion join. Stevens's reconciled merits affirmance is preserved, with his account of the actual record and no-script agreement. Stone's formal vacatur is preserved as a separate remedy, and his direct joins are limited to approved rules and remand inquiries. The standing fallback supplies his exercise-of-review vote on the unaddressed Long component and his disposition-only agreement to set aside the unaddressed motive-based exit-order ground; it adds no explanation and does not convert his overall remedy to reversal. Public Projection preserves these exact limited agreements without exposing the private approval mechanism. Arithmetic: 7 reverse/remand, 1 vacate/remand, 1 affirm; 9 participate. Parts I/II.A have 6 direct joins; II.B/III and the remand framework have 7. No Marks analysis, new factual finding or changed non-Stone commitment occurs.

## Hopwood_v_Texas_merits_1996-11-25.md

**Case and dockets:** Hopwood v. Texas; Fifth Circuit No. 94-50664, 78 F.3d 932; no invented Supreme Court docket; OT1996, chunk 1, matter 6.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1996-11-25, supplied term-calendar scheduling premise; October Term 1996.
**Result:** Affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand; actual-process invalidity unanimous; diversity-direction vacatur 7–2; remedial-unit correction 8–1; remaining individual-remedy components unanimous with the opinion joins distinguished below.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and frozen independent modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event

**Case:** Hopwood v. Texas, October Term 1996; Fifth Circuit No. 94-50664, 78 F.3d 932, reviewing the judgment at 861 F. Supp. 551. Merits decision, November 25, 1996. On review of the Fifth Circuit’s judgment of March 18, 1996; rehearing denied April 4, 1996. No argument date or separate Supreme Court docket number is established in this account.

**Questions:** Whether racial and ethnic educational diversity can be a compelling interest in public-university admissions; whether race-conscious preferences may remedy past discrimination and what predicate and necessity must be shown; and whether the law school’s 1992 system was constitutional and what relief the affected applicants may obtain. The related intervention appeal, No. 94-50569, is outside the grant.

**Record and posture:** Four white Texas applicants—Cheryl Hopwood, Douglas Carvell, Kenneth Elliott and David Rogers—challenge the 1992 process in an individual, nonclass suit. They sought declaration, injunction, admission, compensatory damages and punitive damages under the Equal Protection Clause, §§1981 and 1983 and Title VI. The named defendants include State entities and officials in their official capacities. The District Court found the separate discretionary-review machinery unlawful, awarded one dollar nominal damages to each applicant, declaratory relief and fee-free reapplication, and refused compensation, punitive damages, admission and a broad injunction. The Fifth Circuit remanded individual causation under a school-borne preponderance burden, rejected diversity and the offered remedial grounds, retained the punitive and injunction denials, and left Eleventh Amendment immunity undecided.

In March 1992, resident presumptive-admit index thresholds were 199 for white and other nonpreferred applicants and 189 for Black and Mexican-American applicants; presumptive-deny thresholds were 192 and 179. Earlier preferred-group thresholds varied. Score categories admitted exceptions: some presumptive-admit files moved to discretionary review, and some low-score files could move upward. The preferred groups had separate discretionary review and waiting lists. Enrollment aspirations and observed percentages were not proved fixed reserved seats. How different waiting lists were compared for final places is not fully established. Hopwood’s 199 index led to discretionary review after an assessment of her educational background; the other applicants had indexes of 197. The later program changed the old arrangements, but its full operation is not before the Court.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing or part | Author | Joined by | Relationship and exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | O’Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Nine-Justice narrow actual-process invalidity and equal-competition injury; Adarand’s governing standard. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | O’Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Six-Justice affirmative educational-diversity rule and actual Bakke treatment. Kennedy concurs only in vacating the categorical direction. |

| Opinion of the Court, Parts IV-A, IV-B and IV-C | O’Connor | Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Eight-Justice causation/preponderance rule; Title VI intent; complete punitive standard and bounded denial. Stone-Zsela agrees with the respective dispositions only. |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts IV-D and V | O’Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Nine-Justice source-specific remedial reservations, retained broad-injunction refusal and limits on future-program directions. |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Kennedy | No other Justice | Reserves Part II’s affirmative constitutional question while supporting vacatur of the categorical rule; explains combined-system tailoring. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part, Section A | Scalia | Thomas | Opposes affirmative nonremedial diversity permission and vacatur of the corresponding categorical remand rule; joins the narrow actual-process and specified individual-remedy parts. |
| Same writing, Section B | Scalia | No other Justice | Does not join Part III’s affirmative institutional rule or additional inquiry; would resolve the offered defense on existing insufficiency. |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins Parts I–III, IV-D and V; disposition-only agreement on IV-A–C; preserves his additional operational and corrective conditions and individual causation ground. |

## Assignment and opinion formation

The Chief is in each prevailing judgment coalition, including the individual-remand components on which he does not join every rationale. He assigns the single coordinated opinion to O’Connor. The actual shared grounds are settled before author selection: narrow process invalidity; the six-Justice supported educational-interest rule; the eight-Justice bounded institutional correction; eight-Associate proof, statutory-intent and punitive reasoning; and the nine-Justice remedy reservations. No author is selected to induce an additional vote, and no non-Stone commitment changes.

Fit favors O’Connor’s own Croson opinion and Wygant concurrence: they perform the precise evidence, governmental-purpose, individual-burden, alternatives and remedial-fit work needed to distinguish a possibly important educational objective from an unconstitutional admissions implementation. Her Metro dissent distinguishes individualized higher-education diversity from a racial viewpoint proxy. Her present commitment supports every Court part without an unresolved accommodation. Souter is the strongest alternative for precedent scope and institutional remedy through his actual Adarand concurrence and Jenkins authorship. Stone is a serious alternative for practical operation and separating injury from remedy, but his additional operational/repair conditions and unadopted proof quantum would require more partial principal-opinion sections. O’Connor’s already shared scrutiny-and-fit formulation offers the concrete coordination advantage. This is fit, not an invented expansion history or a workload quota.

The assignment-only baseline and terminal preceding-term source statements establish 101 named Chief assignments in OT1995, maximum O’Connor 27 and Souter 23. ## Event

Tyler Murray v. Montrose County School District RE-1J. On writ of certiorari to the Tenth Circuit, 51 F.3d 921; lower-court Nos. 93-1466 and 93-1474. Argued October 7, 1996; decided December 2, 1996. The questions concern a neighborhood-school presumption under IDEA, use of another school to provide individualized specialized services, and evidence beyond the administrative record. The parents seek reversal and further consideration of aids, later progress and transfer appropriateness. The parents and team agreed on needs, goals and regular/special-class allocation, but disputed Olathe versus Northside. After the October 21, 1993 district judgment, the November 1993 IEP affirmatively placed Tyler at Olathe. The Tenth Circuit on April 4, 1995 affirmed for the district and dismissed its cross-appeal; rehearing was denied May 8, 1995. No separate Supreme Court companion or docket number is supplied.

## Event

Humane Society v. Babbitt. On writ of certiorari to the D.C. Circuit, 46 F.3d 93; D.C. Circuit No. 93-5339 and district-court No. 92-cv-0952. Review was granted October 7, 1996. Argued and decided December 9, 1996 under the expedited schedule. The three questions concern personal aesthetic injury from loss of access to a particular animal, causation and redressability when federal permission enables private action, and procedural standing without a separately established concrete interest. The Society challenges the August 1991 ESA amendment to Hawthorn's certificate and seeks suspension and a declaration against the commercial-activity interpretation. The November 1990 donation preceded that amendment; the original-transfer claim was abandoned on appeal, and the July 1991 CITES certificate was not challenged. The D.C. Circuit vacated the district court's statutory merits judgment and directed dismissal for lack of Article III jurisdiction. No Supreme Court docket number is supplied.

## In_re_Askin_merits_1996-12-09.md

**Case and dockets:** In re Askin; Fourth Circuit No. 94-5428, 47 F.3d 100; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied; October Term 1996.

**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and frozen independent modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event

In re Askin. October Term 1996. On granted merits review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit's February 2, 1995 affirmance of a civil-contempt judgment, reported at 47 F.3d 100, lower-court No. 94-5428. Argued October 8, 1996; decided December 9, 1996.

The questions are whether warrantless reception of the other participant's cordless-radio link violates the noncordless participant's Fourth Amendment rights; whether interception and use were prohibited by the Title III provisions governing the acquisition; and whether a witness may refuse criminal-trial questions directly derived from unlawful interception of his own communications, with what findings, remedial limits and just-cause consequences for civil contempt. The refusal occurred at the criminal trial after use immunity, not before a grand jury. The related criminal prosecution is not consolidated. The attorney-client and Sixth Amendment theories mentioned in the lower history are outside the granted questions.

## Caterpillar_v_Lewis_merits_1996-12-10.md

**Case and dockets:** Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis; No. 95-1263.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1996-12-10; October Term 1996, chunk 1.
**Result:** Reverse jurisdictional vacatur; restore district judgment against that ground and remand for five remaining appellate issues, 9–0.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record from preserved neutral and frozen independent modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commit are deferred under the express no-Git instruction.

## Event

Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis, No. 95-1263. On writ of certiorari to the Sixth Circuit. Argued November 12, 1996; decided December 10, 1996. The question is whether lack of complete diversity at removal requires vacating a federal judgment despite actual complete diversity before trial and through judgment where the plaintiff timely sought remand. Lewis sued Caterpillar and Whayne in Kentucky court; Liberty Mutual intervened. Removal occurred June 21, 1990, remand was denied September 24, 1990, and the Whayne/Liberty settlement occurred June 8, 1993. Whayne was dismissed before the November 15–22, 1993 trial. The jury returned a defense verdict. The Sixth Circuit vacated the federal judgment and directed state-court remand solely for the initial diversity defect, leaving five other appellate issues undecided.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope and relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–II | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Earlier preserved removal error and actual pretrial jurisdiction; unanimous reasoning |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III | O'Connor | Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reasons for returning the five undecided appellate issues; the Chief Justice agrees with that disposition only |

### Assignment and final compatibility

The Chief belongs to both final judgment components, including the remaining-issues remand by the approved fallback, and assigns the single coordinated opinion to O'Connor. His lack of a Part III rationale join does not remove him from that judgment coalition. Fit favors O'Connor's Quackenbush authorship on the actual source, stage and effects of remand and her Newman-Green majority join. Scalia and Kennedy require actual pretrial cure rather than appellate manufacture; her opinion can preserve that recorded distinction without expanding their commitments. Ginsburg is a strong statutory-remedy alternative through Things Remembered and Quackenbush; her historical authorship is not an assignment entitlement. Stone's approved separation of authority, procedural error and remedy is also a candidate strength, but his silence on the five appellate issues makes O'Connor better positioned to write the entire coordinated opinion. No expansion is needed once the frozen actual-cure qualification is retained. The preceding-term 101-assignment baseline triggers no concentration restriction. One named Chief assignment to O'Connor results; root integrates the chronological ordinal.

All eight Associates join C1–C3 exactly as frozen: no waiver, no efficiency-created jurisdiction, actual complete diversity before trial, no general harmlessness rule, and all five appellate issues returned. Scalia and Kennedy's Newman-Green dissent concern is answered by a trial-court cure already accomplished, not a new reason attributed to them. Stone joins C1–C2 at the approved level and supports the C3 disposition under the component-specific fallback; no C3 rationale is inferred. The final votes are nine for reversal/restoration against the jurisdictional ground and nine for remand of the independent appellate claims, with an eight-Justice C3 explanation. There is no changed Associate commitment, historical deviation or invented circulation sequence.

**Historical check:** The complete unanimous historical opinion and all fourteen numbered footnotes were read. It recognizes the preserved removal error, distinguishes *Finn* and *Grubbs* without pretending either decided this exact preserved-objection case, identifies actual diversity before trial and judgment, rejects interlocutory-review waiver, and remands rather than deciding unaddressed appellate merits. These premises remain intact and strongly confirm the independent eight commitments. The historical author does not determine simulated assignment.

## Ogilvie_v_United_States_merits_1996-12-10.md

## Event

O'Gilvie v. United States, Nos. 95-966 and 95-977; October Term 1996, chunk 2. Merits decision, December 10, 1996. Argued October 9, 1996. On writs of certiorari to the Tenth Circuit, 66 F.3d 1550, which reversed the taxpayers' favorable District Court judgments. The consolidated cases concern the former personal-injury tax exclusion, the children's erroneous-refund limitation defense and the timeliness of the Government's appeal in the father's refund action. The wrongful-death verdict awarded $1,525,000 in actual damages and $10 million in punitive damages; only the punitive proceeds are at issue here.

## Opinion Topology

## Greene_v_Georgia_summary_review_1996-12-16.md

**Case and dockets:** Greene v. Georgia; No. 96-5369
**Event and date:** 1996-12-16; October Term 1996, chunk 2.
**Result:** Certiorari granted; Georgia judgment reversed and remanded by eight Associates; Stone would vacate and remand.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record; supersedes none. Operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

Greene v. Georgia, No. 96-5369; October Term 1996, chunk 2. Summary review decision, December 16, 1996; no oral argument. On petition for certiorari to the Supreme Court of Georgia, Greene v. State, 266 Ga. 439, 469 S.E.2d 129 (1996). The question is whether federal habeas deference under Wainwright v. Witt controls state direct appellate review of capital-juror exclusions. Five prospective jurors were excused for cause over objection after expressing reservations about capital punishment. Georgia treated Witt as controlling its appellate deference as well as the substantive qualification rule.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope and judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Per curiam | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Long/Evans reviewability explanation; Stone-Zsela agrees to reach the source question without joining this explanation. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Source-of-review holding and limits; Georgia's lawful deference choices remain open. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III | Per curiam | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Formal reversal and remand. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins Part II and the reconsideration inquiry; would vacate and remand instead of reverse. |

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the source of a review rule matters. Witt permits excluding a capital juror whose views prevent or substantially impair performance under the instructions and oath. It also addresses former §2254(d)'s presumption for a federal court reviewing a state judgment on habeas. The Georgia court treated Witt as controlling its direct-review deference as well as the qualification rule. The federal habeas statute does not assign Georgia's own appellate responsibility.
### Assignment and final joins

The eight-Associate formal reversal coalition excludes the Chief. Stevens is the most senior participating Associate in that coalition and has assignment authority if a named Court opinion is used. The settled, narrow source correction instead issues as an institutional per curiam with no named author or invented assignment. The Chief's agreement with Part II does not put him in the reversal coalition. No named Chief assignment is added.

All eight Associates join Parts I–III exactly as reconciled. Their respective Witt, Morgan, Thompson and Robinette boundaries remain in the holding: no mandatory de novo review, qualification finding or automatic new trial. Stone joins Part II's source rule and remand inquiry, reaches that question without a Part I jurisdictional join, and preserves his separate vacatur remedy. This requires no circulation change or imported historical roll call. The nine source-rule joins, eight jurisdictional-rationale joins and eight formal-reversal votes are intentionally distinct.

## MLB_v_SLJ_merits_1996-12-16.md

**Case and dockets:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J.; No. 95-853
**Event and date:** 1996-12-16; October Term 1996, chunk 2.
**Result:** Mississippi cost-bar judgment reversed and remanded, 7–2; six-Justice Court opinion; Kennedy judgment-only due process.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record; supersedes none. Operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

M.L.B. v. S.L.J., No. 95-853; October Term 1996, chunk 2. Merits decision, December 16, 1996. Argued October 7, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Mississippi. The question is whether Mississippi may deny an indigent parent its provided first appeal from permanent termination because she cannot prepay the necessary record. The father and stepmother filed the termination/adoption suit in November 1993. The December 14, 1994 decree terminated rights to two children and approved adoption. M.L.B. timely appealed in January 1995 and paid the $100 filing fee, but could not pay the $2,352.36 record-preparation and transmission charge. Mississippi denied indigent appellate status in August 1995 and dismissed the appeal for nonpayment. The federal objection was preserved in the chancery court and state supreme court.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope and judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I and mandate | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Bounded access rule, due-process/equality rationale, adequate-record qualification and reversal/remand. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | O'Connor | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Prospective burden on the State to establish a sufficient alternative after a colorable need for a full transcript; Stone-Zsela does not join this additional proposition. |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | No other Justice | Same operative access protection and remedy on due process; no join to the Court's opinion. |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm; adequate prior hearing and nonextension of the criminal-access exception to this civil appeal. |

### Assignment and final joins

The Chief belongs to the seven-Justice reversal coalition and assigns the coordinated opinion to O'Connor. Six already share the bounded convergence rationale and necessary-record remedy; five Associates independently share the prospective Mayer allocation. Kennedy's due-process-only ground is fixed. No expansion is needed to obtain Court authority. Fit favors O'Connor's own Bearden opinion, which directly performs the context-specific work of connecting indigency, fairness and equality without making poverty a suspect class. Her actual A. St. P. C. and Baby Richard dissents distinguish complete permanent termination from a surviving parental status or an unestablished new liberty predicate. Those authored distinctions supply concrete legal tools for expressing the precise permanent-termination boundary here while carrying both Court parts.

Stone is a strong alternative for Part I through his own two family-process opinions and approved precise access rule; his supplement does not, however, support authoring Part II's additional prospective burden allocation as his own rule. Ginsburg is a strong whole-opinion alternative through her family-process joins and the frozen integration of Griffin/Mayer, Boddie and Santosky. O'Connor's demonstrated authorship on indigency convergence and the distinction her own dissents already draw supplies the concrete fit advantage. The choice neither imports historical authorship nor invents an accommodation or workload quota. This is one named Chief assignment to O'Connor.

Kennedy's separate Boddie/Lassiter/Santosky/Mathews ground remains judgment-only without speculative partial joins. Thomas writes the shared dissent because his actual Lewis concurrence supplies the bounded access-versus-assistance distinction, with Scalia joining the common nonextension grounds. Their historical broader attack on Griffin is not imported. Part I and mandate have six direct joins; Part II has five; Kennedy adds the seventh reversal vote. Stone's limited join does not change the five Associates' burdens proposition. No Associate changes position or adds a ground after reconciliation.

## United_States_v_Sahhar_merits_1996-12-16.md

**Case and dockets:** United States v. Sahhar; Ninth Circuit Nos. 94-50186 and 94-50356; separate Supreme Court docket unassigned
**Event and date:** 1996-12-16; October Term 1996, chunk 2.
**Result:** Ninth Circuit judgment affirmed, 9–0, with current-predicate and state-placement remand; dismissal undisturbed.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record; supersedes none. Operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

United States v. Sahhar; Ninth Circuit Nos. 94-50186 and 94-50356; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned. October Term 1996, chunk 2. Merits decision, December 16, 1996. Argued October 9, 1996. On review of 56 F.3d 1026, decided May 26, 1995, after argument and submission September 14, 1994. The questions concern §4246 civil custody beyond the hypothetical five-year sentence for threatening the President, continued safeguards after dismissal and the state-placement duty. Sahhar was found incompetent and unlikely to regain competence. The District Court dismissed the indictment and ordered release despite then-existing findings of illness and danger; release was stayed pending the Government's appeal. The Government did not appeal dismissal. The Ninth Circuit reversed the release order and remanded for state-placement inquiry.

## DeAngelis_v_El_Paso_Association_merits_1997-01-06.md

**Case and dockets:** DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers' Association; Fifth Circuit No. 93-8700, 51 F.3d 591; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-01-06; October Term 1996, chunk 2, matter 17.
**Result:** Affirm the judgment for the Association: hostile environment, 7–2; retaliation, 9–0. No damages restored.
**Version / lineage:** Initial canonical record; supersedes none. Repository verification and commitment remain pending with the operator; no Git operation was performed in this assembly.

## Event

DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers' Association; Fifth Circuit No. 93-8700. Argued October 15, 1996; decided January 6, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Fifth Circuit, which reversed the jury's Title VII verdict and rendered judgment for the Association, 51 F.3d 591. The questions concern hostile-environment sufficiency, the preserved First Amendment defense to publication-based liability, and retaliation through references to DeAngelis's discrimination complaint.

The challenged newsletter reached at least seven hundred officers. The ten reproduced columns, including the April 1990 item, included derogatory comments about women generally and references connected to DeAngelis. She described humiliation, reduced confidence, promotion hesitation, mocking and impaired supervisory authority, including two insubordination incidents. Her reprimands were upheld, her ratings remained good, and management and Association leaders criticized the columns. Her causal account and reasonable favorable inferences are credited for sufficiency review; the lower court's contrary evaluation is not an independent fact. Coverage as a labor organization was resolved against the Association. The First Amendment defense was preserved but not decided below.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship and exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Statutory hostile-environment insufficiency; six direct joins |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Retaliation insufficiency; unanimous |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No joins | Joins Part II; rejects Part I and affirms hostile-environment judgment solely on the separate expressive ground |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Ginsburg | Joins Part II; would reverse and remand the hostile-environment portion without restoring liability or damages |

**Controlling explanation:** The Court distinguishes opposition to a discrimination complaint from a proved retaliatory consequence. The publications reveal disparagement and contemplated conditional litigation, but do not establish lost pay, promotion, assignment, authority, membership, representation or a comparable imposed disadvantage caused by the complaint. No actual retaliatory lawsuit or unreported sanction is found. The same record can raise distinct hostile-environment and retaliation questions; disagreement on the former does not supply the missing consequence for the latter.
### Assignment authority and fit

The Chief joins the actual seven-Justice hostile-environment affirmance and unanimous retaliation judgment, and assigns the coordinated Court opinion to O'Connor. The six statutory affirmers already share sufficient ground, so fit rather than expansion governs. Her Harris authorship directly equips her to preserve both the contextual objective boundary and the prohibition on compulsory injury or work-loss factors. Souter's Harris join and Hurley authorship provide a strong alternative; Stone's separation of statutory and constitutional questions is another candidate strength. But Stone rejects the governing statutory application, while O'Connor can state the existing six-Justice ground and unanimous retaliation component without converting a reservation into a speech holding. No authorship accommodation is needed to create or change a join. This is one named Chief assignment to O'Connor.

The frozen preceding-term baseline establishes 101 named Chief assignments, with the largest recipient totals O'Connor 27 and Souter 23. No author exceeded half, so the two-consecutive-term concentration rule excludes no author. The current chunk-1 baseline is eight named Chief assignments: Stevens one, Souter three, Kennedy one, O'Connor two, Scalia one. The operator integrates the chronological chunk-2 census; no count is used as a quota or as a substitute for fit.

## Watts_and_Putra_summary_review_1997-01-06.md

**Case and dockets:** United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra; No. 95-1906, one Rule 12.4 petition concerning two Ninth Circuit judgments
**Event and date:** Summary review, 1997-01-06; October Term 1996, chunk 2, matter 18.
**Result:** Grant the petition, summarily reverse each Ninth Circuit judgment and remand each case separately, 7–2 on immediate reversal and remand.
**Version / lineage:** Initial canonical record; supersedes none. Repository verification and commitment remain pending with the operator; no Git operation was performed in this assembly.

## Event

United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra, No. 95-1906. Submitted without oral argument on a single Rule 12.4 petition; decided January 6, 1997. The petition seeks review of separate Ninth Circuit judgments vacating the defendants' sentences, 67 F.3d 790 and 78 F.3d 1386. The question is whether a general acquittal categorically bars consideration of the underlying conduct in mandatory Guidelines sentencing, including the distinct statutory, double-jeopardy, due-process and jury-allocation objections.

Watts was convicted of possessing cocaine base with intent to distribute and acquitted of using a firearm in relation to a drug offense. Cocaine base was found in a kitchen cabinet and loaded guns and ammunition in a bedroom closet. The judge found firearm possession connected to the drug offense by a preponderance and applied the two-level §2D1.1(b)(1) adjustment. Putra was convicted for involvement in a one-ounce cocaine transaction on May 8, 1992, and acquitted of the corresponding charge involving five ounces on May 9. Both were videotaped. The judge found her involvement in the second transaction by a preponderance and aggregated the quantities under §1B1.3. Each court of appeals judgment rested on a categorical prohibition against the challenged acquitted-conduct use. Each preserved sentencing objection and each judgment receives separate treatment.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship and exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Seven Justices join the complete bounded ordinary-use rule, constitutional analysis and separate remands |
| Concurrence | Scalia | No joins | Joins the Court; disputes Commission authority to prohibit otherwise lawful acquitted-conduct consideration |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No joins | Joins the Court; regards Commission exclusion of acquitted conduct from mandatory enhancement as a permissible policy change |
| Dissent | Stevens | No joins | Opposes summary treatment and would affirm on his independent statutory-authorization and proof ground |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No joins | Opposes summary merits disposition; would grant plenary review without presently deciding the merits |

**Justice Stevens, dissenting; no joins.** He seeks plenary briefing and argument and would, if the merits are decided now, affirm both sentence-vacatur judgments. Section 3661 permits receiving information and using it in lawful sentencing discretion; it does not itself assign mandatory weight or authorize a binding-range increase on a preponderance finding of additional criminal conduct. Section 1B1.4 addresses selection within the range or departure, while §1B1.3 determines the range through relevant conduct. Section 994(l)'s instruction about incremental punishment for multiple convictions reinforces his narrower authorization reading; the Court's contrary view treats that instruction as a minimum rather than an exclusive limit.
### Assignment authority and fit

The seven-Justice immediate-reversal coalition includes the Chief. The already settled narrow categorical-bar correction issues as an institutional per curiam, with no named author and no named Chief assignment. The form does not conceal the distinct procedural dissents or the separate statutory Commission disagreement. No historical author is imported. The common opinion preserves Scalia and Thomas's express refusal of a universal statutory-ceiling characterization; it does not require them to join the broader proposition they withheld in Witte. Because the frozen scope already accommodates that distinction, no post-reconciliation expansion or changed commitment is used.

The frozen preceding-term baseline establishes 101 named Chief assignments, with the largest recipient totals O'Connor 27 and Souter 23. No author exceeded half, so the two-consecutive-term concentration rule excludes no author. The current chunk-1 baseline is eight named Chief assignments: Stevens one, Souter three, Kennedy one, O'Connor two, Scalia one. The operator integrates the chronological chunk-2 census; no count is used as a quota or as a substitute for fit.

**Separate Commission positions.** Breyer maintains that the Commission may change the relevant-conduct policy to exclude acquitted conduct from mandatory enhancement, even though present Guidelines contain no exception. The compatibility of judge and jury findings under different burdens is not the only permissible policy consideration; the institutional role of acquittals can support a different rule. Scalia disagrees: §994(b)(1) requires consistency with pertinent Title 18 provisions, and §3661 forbids a Commission or judicial restriction on otherwise lawful receipt and consideration of sentencing information. In his view the Commission may recommend congressional change but cannot itself prohibit otherwise lawful enhancement or impose an unsupported higher proof barrier solely because the conduct was acquitted. Neither view is silently assigned to the other six Associates. These opposed statutory positions are not part of a shared six-Associate constitutional rationale. Their substantive disagreement warrants separate-position treatment if a summary merits disposition is assembled; authorship and exact publication joins remain for the authorized next stage.

## Old_Chief_v_United_States_merits_1997-01-07.md

**Case and dockets:** Old Chief v. United States; No. 95-6556
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-01-07; October Term 1996, chunk 2, matter 19.
**Result:** Reverse the Ninth Circuit judgment, 6–3 on Rule 403 error and reversal; five Justices direct a harmlessness remand, while Stone-Zsela would vacate the conviction and order a new trial.
**Version / lineage:** Initial canonical record; supersedes none. Repository verification and commitment remain pending with the operator; no Git operation was performed in this assembly.

## Event

Old Chief v. United States, No. 95-6556. Argued October 16, 1996; decided January 7, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, which affirmed the federal conviction. The question is whether Rule 403 permitted the Government to identify the name and nature of a prior assault conviction solely to prove felon status when Old Chief offered to admit the complete qualifying status element.

The charges arising from a 1993 confrontation included assault with a dangerous weapon, use of a firearm in a crime of violence and possession of a firearm by a prohibited person under §922(g)(1). The prior judgment described a December 18, 1988 assault causing serious bodily injury and a five-year sentence. Old Chief offered an admission, moved to exclude the name and nature, and renewed his objection when the judgment was introduced. The prosecutor refused the stipulation, and the judge accepted that refusal on the prosecution's general freedom to choose evidence. The jury convicted; the Ninth Circuit upheld the evidentiary ruling without deciding harmlessness. The preserved judgment-record objection is distinct from the requested and later withdrawn credibility instruction. The proposed status instruction required clarification of legal adequacy, but that was not the trial court's ground for refusing the offer.

## Opinion Topology

|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Rule 403 error, adequate-status-proof limits and reversal of the no-error appellate judgment; six direct joins |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Ginsburg | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Harmlessness remand and its conditional trial consequences; five direct joins |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No joins | Joins Part I and reversal; dissents from Part II's open harmlessness inquiry and would vacate the conviction for a new trial |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm for no Rule 403 abuse; accepts ordinary harmlessness review conditionally if the Court's contrary error conclusion governs |

### Assignment authority and fit

For Part I's common appellate reversal and Rule 403 error, the Chief belongs to the actual six-Justice judgment coalition: his approved formal disposition expressly calls for reversal, although he demands a different downstream remedy. He assigns Part I to Ginsburg. Stevens, the senior Associate in the distinct five-Justice harmlessness-remand coalition, assigns Part II to Ginsburg as well. Neither assignor acts from a coalition he does not join. The coordinated opinion counts once as a named Chief assignment to Ginsburg because the Chief assigns one part.

Fit governs already settled grounds. Ginsburg's Williamson writing expressly distinguishes a sufficient account for deciding evidentiary error from the whole trial record required for prejudice, the central coordination task here. It also demonstrates attention to context-specific probative force rather than categorical evidence labels. Souter's Williamson join and Stone's complete comparative-value formulation are strong alternatives. Stone is fully eligible for Part I, and his formulation expresses the doctrinal development precisely, but his fixed adverse harmlessness conclusion prevents him from carrying the coordinated five-Justice Part II. Ginsburg offers the demonstrated advantage of stating both the shared error holding and the independent remedial boundary without diluting Stone's separate position. Stevens's selection for Part II rests on that same supported division, consistent with his own Williamson join. No historical Old Chief author or joinder supplies this choice, and no new coalition accommodation is needed.

No author exceeded half, so the two-consecutive-term concentration rule excludes no author. The current chunk-1 baseline is eight named Chief assignments: Stevens one, Souter three, Kennedy one, O'Connor two, Scalia one. The operator integrates the chronological chunk-2 census; no count is used as a quota or as a substitute for fit.

## Walmer_v_Department_of_Defense_merits_1997-01-13.md

**Case and dockets:** Walmer v. Department of Defense; Tenth Circuit No. 93-3377, 52 F.3d 851; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-01-13; October Term 1996, chunk 2, matter 20.
**Result:** Affirm denial of preliminary relief: equal protection, 5–4; Bill of Attainder claim, 9–0 on the legislative-action predicate alone.
**Version / lineage:** Initial canonical record; supersedes none. Repository verification and commitment remain pending with the operator; no Git operation was performed in this assembly.

## Event

Walmer v. Department of Defense; Tenth Circuit No. 93-3377. Argued October 16, 1996; decided January 13, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Tenth Circuit, which affirmed denial of a preliminary injunction and dissolved its appellate stay on April 4, 1995, 52 F.3d 851. The questions concern equal protection under the Fifth Amendment, the role of military deference, and the Bill of Attainder Clause in separation under the earlier executive Army Regulation 635-100.

Major Joyce Walmer admitted the past homosexual acts on which the Board of Inquiry proceeded and expressly stipulated that she did not qualify for the regulation's retention exceptions under paragraphs 5-51a(1)–(5) and 5-56. Other initial allegations, including a same-sex marriage, marijuana use and conduct unbecoming, were excluded from the Board proceeding and supply no alternative basis here. The Board recommended honorable discharge; the Board of Review and Secretary adopted that recommendation. The question is preliminary relief against the challenged separation, not a final declaration of policy validity or adjudication under later legislation.

The district court decided likelihood of success without finding irreparable injury, comparative harm or public interest. The court of appeals assumed those other factors only for its modified likelihood inquiry, under which serious, substantial, difficult and doubtful merits questions could warrant deliberate investigation if the remaining factors were satisfied. That assumption was not a finding that they were satisfied. The live preliminary-relief controversy is before the Court on this January 13 calendar; no continuing historical stay, completed discharge or current service-status change is established.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship and exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Ordinary rational-basis application and affirmance of the equality-based preliminary denial; five direct joins |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Executive regulation lacks the legislative predicate for attainder; unanimous, punishment reserved |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Joins both Court parts; adds the distinct Bowers and permissible conduct-morality ground |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg | Joins Part II; would vacate the equality denial under ordinary rational basis without a new Government proof burden |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No joins | Joins Part II; Part A independently requires Government justification and equality vacatur/remand; Part B separately proposes intermediate scrutiny |

**Controlling explanation:** Beach permits conceivable legitimate reasons without formal findings or empirical certainty. Heller requires a relation to the specific disadvantage and also preserves relevant classwide generalizations. Romer applies those limits to a blanket civil disability; its ordinary-review rule neither assigns an orientation scrutiny tier nor makes every conduct-based personnel classification invalid. The Court examines this military rule's connection to discipline, morale and workable service relationships instead of accepting the military label as an answer.
### Assignment authority and fit

The Chief does not belong to the five-Justice equal-protection affirmance, so O'Connor, the senior participating Associate in that actual judgment majority, assigns Part I to herself. Her Harris and Heller/Romer work preserves contextual review without an invented heightened tier, while her Goldman dissent supplies a specific basis for separating real constitutional review from mechanical military deference without transferring religious-exercise burdens. Kennedy's Heller family-participation authorship offers the strongest alternative on relevant predictive generalizations; O'Connor offers the combined fit across ordinary equality review, military deference and the confined preliminary disposition. No expansion is needed because the five already support the same specific military relationship.

The Chief belongs to the unanimous attainder judgment and assigns Part II to O'Connor as part of the coordinated opinion. Stone is eligible to write that component and states its institutional ground precisely; Stevens's Ambassador Books authorship is also a strong alternative. The branch-specific threshold is settled among all nine, and O'Connor can state it with its punishment reservation alongside the independently assigned equality component without changing any join. Coordinated authorship here preserves the actual component distinctions rather than giving the Chief authority over Part I. This counts once as a named Chief assignment to O'Connor because he assigns Part II. No historical Supreme Court author exists for the assigned review, and no later-career evidence is used.

The frozen preceding-term baseline establishes 101 named Chief assignments, with the largest recipient totals O'Connor 27 and Souter 23. No author exceeded half, so the two-consecutive-term concentration rule excludes no author. The current chunk-1 baseline is eight named Chief assignments: Stevens one, Souter three, Kennedy one, O'Connor two, Scalia one. ## Abebe_Jira_v_Negewo_merits_1997-01-14.md

**Case and dockets:** Abebe-Jira v. Negewo, No. 95-8873; Eleventh Circuit No. 93-9133.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-01-14; October Term 1996, chunk 2.
