**Version / lineage:** Initial record; supersedes none. Operator Git verification and commit pending; no Git operation is represented as completed.

## Event

Merits decision

Abebe-Jira v. Negewo, No. 95-8873. Submitted for merits consideration January 14, 1997; decided January 14, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit, No. 93-9133, 72 F.3d 844, which affirmed a bench-trial damages judgment. Review concerns whether §1350 supports judicial recognition of this civil action, whether compensatory and punitive remedies are authorized, and whether the political-question doctrine bars individual claims for official torture abroad. No earlier oral argument is stated.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirmance of both damages components | Parts I–V in full |
| Same opinion | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Affirmance of both damages components | Parts I–III and V; Part IV's punitive authority and deliberate-personal-torture application, excluding its equivalently reckless aggravation formulation |
| Same opinion | Stevens | Scalia and Thomas | Agreement on distinct jurisdiction and political-question conclusions; disagreement with damages judgment | Parts I and V only |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse the damages judgment and remand | Rejects Part II's action authority and consequently Parts III–IV's relief in this action; concurs in Parts I and V |

Parts I and V have nine joins. Parts II and III have seven. Part IV's complete culpability formulation has six, with seven joining its deliberate-torture ground and application. No narrower-ground aggregation is needed.

## Judgment coalitions, assignment and compatibility

Before assignment, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer form the seven-Justice coalition to affirm both compensation and punishment for each plaintiff. Scalia and Thomas would reverse both through the sufficient source-of-action objection and remand. All nine separately support the forum and no-categorical-political-question propositions. The affirmative seven share the received-action and deliberate-torture remedy grounds; six also support the broader punitive aggravation wording.

The Chief assigns the coordinated opinion to Stevens. Fit governs: his Central Bank authorship directly separates bounded development of a received action from invention of unrestricted liability, the central task here. His Franklin join and punitive positions support keeping action, compensation, punishment and amount constraints separate. Stone is the strongest alternative for that same authority/action/remedy structure through his explicit current supplement; Stevens's already demonstrated authorship of the received-action rule gives him the concrete advantage in explaining the analogy without converting it into a universal permission. Kennedy's Musick, Peeler authorship supplies a further supported alternative but focuses on incidents of an already established action. No expansion is needed: the frozen affirmative commitments already share the relevant basis, and assigning either dissenter cannot answer their substantive source objection. Historical Supreme Court merits authorship does not exist here and is not imported.

The [assignment-only source statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK2_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) and [preceding-term baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK1_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md) establish that no prior recipient exceeded half of 101 named Chief assignments. No concentration ceiling excludes an author. This single coordinated opinion adds one Chief assignment to Stevens; root integrates the chronological census. No workload quota or later-career evidence determines authorship. Scalia authors the source-based separate opinion with Thomas joining, reflecting the frozen writing plan and their recorded legal ground.

Every Associate retains the final frozen component commitment. The six affirmative Justices join the same source-specific rationale, compensation and independently grounded punitive authority. Scalia and Thomas join only Parts I and V and dissent from the judgment. No agreement on forum or political question is converted into damages support. O'Connor's statutory-source concern is answered by the existing frozen primary-personal-tort formulation, not a new circulation concession. Every Justice's amount, personal-responsibility, immunity and unpleaded-theory limits remain intact. No material post-reconciliation change or invented draft exchange occurs.


## Atherton_v_FDIC_merits_1997-01-14.md

**Case and dockets:** Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, No. 95-928.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-01-14; October Term 1996, chunk 2.
**Result:** Vacate the Third Circuit judgment and remand, 9–0; eight join the procedural explanation and all nine join the source-of-law, statutory and remedial holdings.
**Version / lineage:** Initial record; supersedes none. Operator Git verification and commit pending; no Git operation is represented as completed.

## Event

Merits decision

Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, No. 95-928. Argued November 4, 1996; decided January 14, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Third Circuit, reviewing its interlocutory §1292(b) judgment, 57 F.3d 1231. The questions concern §1821(k)'s effect on the receiver's claims, its coverage of federally chartered institutions and the disputed source of the ordinary duty. The Third Circuit restored federal-common-law theories while reserving their precise standard; no liability judgment is under review.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Vacatur and remand | Parts I–IV |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stone-Zsela | Vacatur and remand; agrees to reach the merits | Parts II–IV; does not join Part I's procedural explanation |

No separate opinion is filed.

## Assignment and final opinion formation

The unanimous vacatur/remand judgment coalition is established before assignment. The eight Associates share Parts I–IV; Stone shares Parts II–IV and the decision to reach the merits. The Chief therefore assigns the single coordinated opinion to Scalia. Fit governs: Scalia's O'Melveny authorship directly supplies the source-of-duty, specified-conflict and no-automatic-relief analysis; his Lebron authorship equips him to distinguish an included source argument from a separate question. A wholly textual savings-clause explanation satisfies the actual O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas join conditions without accommodation-dependent expansion. Souter is the strongest Associate alternative through United States National Bank's governing-law analysis. Stone is a genuine alternative through his exact state-law/federal-floor formulation, but Scalia's authored work addresses both the governing rule and the separate review-scope issue, which Stone's supplement does not explain. No historical authorship supplies this choice.

The [preceding-term assignment baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK1_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md) establishes 101 named Chief assignments and a maximum of 27 to one author; nobody exceeded half, so the concentration bar excludes nobody. [Assignment-only statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK2_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) supply the current-term source review. This coordinated opinion adds one Chief assignment to Scalia; root supplies the completed chronological census. No quotas or later-career capability evidence are used.

All final Associate joins are those frozen in the final reconciliation. The opinion adds no legislative-history ground, liability finding, conclusive New Jersey rule, automatic state-claim revival or retroactivity ruling. O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas thus join the actual text-based opinion fully. No changed Associate commitment or invented circulation dialogue occurs.

**A3 — Section 1821(k)'s authorization and savings sentence.** The provision covers insured depository institutions whether federally or state chartered. In a civil action by, on behalf of, or at the request or direction of the Corporation, prosecuted wholly or partly for its benefit, its authorization applies when the Corporation acts as receiver or conservator; acts on a claim purchased from, assigned by, or otherwise conveyed by such receiver or conservator; or acts on a claim purchased from, assigned by, or otherwise conveyed wholly or partly by an insured institution or its affiliate in connection with §1823 assistance. It permits personal monetary liability for gross negligence, similar conduct, or conduct demonstrating a greater disregard of duty, including intentional tortious conduct, with the culpability terms defined under applicable state law. When applicable it prevents a more protective state liability shield from defeating that authorization. It does not itself find breach, causation, absence of defenses or an amount due.
**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1821(k), when applicable, permits personal monetary liability of a director or officer of an insured depository institution, whether federally or state chartered, for gross negligence, similar conduct or conduct demonstrating a greater disregard of duty, including intentional tortious conduct, with those culpability terms defined under applicable state law; the action must be by, on behalf of, or at the request or direction of the Corporation, prosecuted wholly or partly for its benefit, with the Corporation acting as receiver or conservator, on a claim purchased from, assigned by or otherwise conveyed by such a receiver or conservator, or on a claim purchased from, assigned by or otherwise conveyed wholly or partly by an insured institution or its affiliate in connection with §1823 assistance. That authorization defeats a more protective state liability shield but preserves rights actually available under other applicable law, including a state duty permitting recovery for less culpable conduct: gross negligence is a federal floor, not a ceiling.

## Castano_v_American_Tobacco_Co_merits_1997-01-14.md

**Case and dockets:** Castano v. American Tobacco Co.; Fifth Circuit No. 95-30725; 84 F.3d 734; interlocutory appeal under 28 U.S.C. §1292(b).
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-01-14 — October Term 1996, chunk 3.
**Result:** Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 9–0 on each component; the existing class remains uncertified.
**Version / lineage:** Initial adjudication from the approved chunk 3 brief and separately preserved January 14 modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Filesystem-saved; operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

**Castano v. American Tobacco Co.**, Fifth Circuit No. 95-30725, 84 F.3d 734. October Term 1996. Merits decision, January 14, 1997; merits hearing January 14, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, following its May 23, 1996 decision on an accepted interlocutory appeal under 28 U.S.C. §1292(b). The Court reviews the accepted interlocutory appeal, rather than a mandamus proceeding.

The nationwide tobacco-addiction class covers smokers and specified derivative or representative claimants since 1943. The District Court certified core-liability and punitive questions while excluding injury, causation, reliance, defenses and individual compensatory damages. The Court of Appeals reversed and directed dismissal of the class complaint. The class seeks reinstatement; manufacturers seek decertification. The questions concern predominance and superiority; the rigorous analysis required for a novel mass-tort theory; material state-law variations; and management, individual proof and settlement pressure. The separate denial of Rule 23(b)(2) equitable and medical-monitoring certification was not cross-appealed.

## Opinion Topology

Justice Ginsburg delivers the Opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV, joined throughout by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer.

## Assignment and final compatibility

All nine tentatively support both decertification components and removal of the categorical tort-maturity dismissal instruction. They already share the actual-law-and-proof ground and the possibility, not entitlement, of a supported narrower proposal. The Chief assigns the coordinated Opinion of the Court to Ginsburg. Her own Walsh treatment performs this case's exact work: credible state-law analysis, meaningful groupings and proof, while avoiding an automatic bar based on diversity of state law. This is fit, not expansion or a manufactured accommodation. O'Connor's Matsushita opinion makes her a strong alternative on representative authority, but the central defect here is prospective certification across state-law regimes, the task most directly addressed in Walsh. Stone's approved distinction between an unsupported present class and a lawful future proposal supports self-authorship, but Ginsburg's direct formulation offers the stronger case-specific fit. Historical authorship and generic reputation play no role.

The [assignment baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md) and [actual bounded assignment statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) were read. OT1995 has 101 named Chief assignments, with a maximum recipient count of 27; no recipient exceeded half. The start of chunk 3 has 17 named Chief assignments. The three January 14 additions are counted in record-preparation order for bookkeeping only: Reich to Kennedy, Castano to Ginsburg, Deuel to O'Connor. This opinion is **named Chief assignment 19, Ginsburg's 2 in OT1996**, counting this coordinated opinion once. The preceding-term concentration condition bars no candidate. No separate writing adds an assignment. Git existence and commitment order are not certified.

## Chamber_of_Commerce_v_Reich_merits_1997-01-14.md

**Case and dockets:** Chamber of Commerce v. Reich; D.C. Circuit No. 95-5242; 74 F.3d 1322; no Supreme Court docket number assigned.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-01-14 — October Term 1996, chunk 3.
**Result:** Affirmed and remanded, 9–0, for appropriate prospective relief against the implementing Secretary.
**Version / lineage:** Initial adjudication from the approved chunk 3 brief and separately preserved January 14 modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Filesystem-saved; operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

**Chamber of Commerce v. Reich**, D.C. Circuit No. 95-5242, 74 F.3d 1322. October Term 1996. Merits decision, January 14, 1997; merits hearing January 14, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, which on February 2, 1996 reversed the District Court's dismissal and rejected the challenged procurement policy. No Supreme Court docket number is assigned.

The employer organizations seek declaratory and injunctive relief against the Secretary of Labor's implementation of Executive Order 12954. Their complaint was not amended to plead an APA challenge to the later regulations. The questions are whether traditional equity permits this subordinate-officer challenge despite the absence of a pleaded APA action; whether any statute precludes it; and whether the Procurement Act authorizes the replacement-based contracting restriction consistently with the NLRA. Presidential APA status remains an open question.

## In_re_Deuel_merits_1997-01-14.md

**Case and dockets:** In re Deuel; Federal Circuit No. 94-1202; 51 F.3d 1552; application 07/542,232; no Supreme Court docket number assigned.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-01-14 — October Term 1996, chunk 3.
**Result:** Affirmed, 9–0 for each claim group, reversing the existing §103 rejection of claims 4–7; no patent issuance ordered.
**Version / lineage:** Initial adjudication from the approved chunk 3 brief and separately preserved January 14 modeling/reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Filesystem-saved; operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

**In re Deuel**, Federal Circuit No. 94-1202, 51 F.3d 1552; application Serial No. 07/542,232, Heparin-Binding Growth Factor. October Term 1996. Merits decision, January 14, 1997; merits hearing January 14, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit at the government's request. The petition was held through July 1, 1996; limited review was granted October 7, 1996. No Supreme Court docket number is assigned.

The Board of Patent Appeals and Interferences affirmed the examiner's §103 rejection of claims 4–7 on November 30, 1993. The Federal Circuit reversed on March 28, 1995. Claims 5 and 7 recite particular human placental and bovine uterine cDNA sequences; claims 4 and 6 cover broader DNA genera encoding the identified proteins. The questions concern whether prior protein information and known isolation methods can establish obviousness without prior disclosure of the claimed nucleotide sequence, and when a predictably effective method is sufficient despite uncertainty about precise molecular structure. The actual prior protein disclosure contains only nineteen amino acids, not the complete sequence. The controversy concerns reversal of existing application rejections, not infringement or an order to issue a patent.

## Opinion Topology

Justice O'Connor delivers the Opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV, joined throughout by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

## Walters_v_Metropolitan_Educational_Enterprises_merits_1997-01-14.md

**Case and dockets:** Walters v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc., No. 95-259; Equal Employment Opportunity Commission v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc., No. 95-779.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-01-14; October Term 1996, chunk 2.
**Result:** Retaliation judgment reversed and remanded, 9–0, with the 1990 numerical objection resolved; promotion dismissal left undisturbed, 9–0, with eight direct nonreview-reasoning joins and Stone disposition only.
**Version / lineage:** Initial record; supersedes none. Operator Git verification and commit pending; no Git operation is represented as completed.

## Event

Merits decision

Walters v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc., No. 95-259, consolidated with Equal Employment Opportunity Commission v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc., No. 95-779. Argued November 6, 1996; decided January 14, 1997. On writs of certiorari to the Seventh Circuit, 60 F.3d 1225, which affirmed dismissal under a daily-compensation counting method. The question is whether Title VII counts a genuine employment relationship on each working day or requires actual work or payment on that day. The EEOC sued over Walters's discharge; she intervened and added a promotion claim. The lower courts described their numerical dismissal as jurisdictional; the Court does not decide that classification.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Retaliation reversal/remand and undisturbed promotion dismissal | Parts I–IV in full |
| Same opinion | Souter | Stone-Zsela | Same component dispositions | Parts I–II and Part IV's retaliation reversal/remand ground; disposition-only agreement with Part III and Part IV's promotion component |

No separate opinion is filed.

## Coalitions, assignment and final compatibility

Before assignment, all nine support reversal/remand of the retaliation dispute and leaving the promotion dismissal undisturbed. The eight Associates share every final reconciled reason. Stone shares the relationship rule and supported minimum application but supplies disposition alone on the newly presented promotion component. The Chief therefore assigns the coordinated opinion to Souter, counting it once. Fit favors Souter's Darden authorship, which directly distinguishes an actual employment relationship from an unhelpful statutory definition and a single indicium, together with United States National Bank's careful treatment of governing-law review without a universal duty to decide omitted issues. Ginsburg is the strongest Associate alternative through Town & Country's actual-control and compatible-service analysis. Stone is a serious alternative on the statutory text and practical operation, but his supplement is silent on the separate preservation rationale that the coordinated opinion must explain. Souter can state both supported grounds with their actual limits. No expansion-dependent join or historical authorship supplies this choice.

The [preceding-term baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK1_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md) reports 101 named Chief assignments and a maximum recipient total of 27; no concentration ceiling is triggered. [Assignment-only source statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK2_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) establish the prior source review. This opinion adds one Chief assignment to Souter; root integrates the chronological census. No quota, capacity premise or later-career evidence is used.

All Associates retain R1–R4 exactly as reconciled. No worker-status uncertainty, unsupported exact count, 1988 finding, intentional waiver, automatic promotion reopening, liability result or jurisdictional characterization is introduced. The repair's original model supported returning the promotion component; the final reconciliation superseded that remedial choice before Stone entered. Assembly does not silently re-adopt it. No Associate change requires a new audit, and no circulation history is invented.

**Version / lineage:** Original adjudication from the approved chunk 3 brief and frozen January 21 reconciled commitments; supersedes no completed event. Repository verification and commitment are pending the operator's permitted checks.

## Event

Babbitt v. Youpee, No. 95-1595. Merits decision, January 21, 1997. Argued December 2, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, whose September 5, 1995 judgment, 67 F.3d 194, affirmed declaratory and injunctive relief against amended §207 of the Indian Land Consolidation Act. The question is whether the 1984 amendments cured the uncompensated taking identified in Hodel v. Irving.

William Youpee died on October 19, 1990. His will devised each disputed fractional trust-land interest to one descendant, preserving each existing fraction. The descendants did not already own interests in the relevant allotments. Interior applied the escheat provision; its appeals board declined to decide the constitutional challenge. The Government seeks reversal; the devisees seek to preserve the protection against uncompensated escheat.

## Opinion Topology

Justice O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Justice Stevens files a dissent. No partial joins or judgment-only concurrences occur.

## Final judgment, assignment, and joins

Affirmance is 8–1: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer support it; Stevens would reverse and remand on the presented takings ground. The same eight support every controlling proposition. No compensation amount, code mandate, new hearing, or probate distribution is ordered.

**Chief assignment:** The Chief belongs to the settled eight-Justice affirmance coalition and assigns the single opinion of the Court to O'Connor. Her own Irving opinion performed the precise contextual succession analysis needed here; she is best placed to explain the real amendments without treating every inheritance restriction as confiscation. Stone's approved formulation is compatible, but O'Connor offers a concrete subject-specific advantage. Kennedy's contextual approach and Souter's record discipline are suitable alternatives; neither supplies an unresolved accommodation that requires expansion assignment. Fit therefore controls, with no manufactured circulation history. Stevens authors his own dissent.

The [assignment baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md), [bounded earlier statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md), and [actual January 14 statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_JAN14_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) establish 20 named Chief assignments before this event, including six to O'Connor. This is **named Chief assignment 21, O'Connor's seventh in OT1996**, counting the coordinated opinion once. OT1995 contains 101 named Chief assignments, with a maximum recipient count of 27; no preceding-term recipient exceeded half, so the consecutive-term concentration condition bars no candidate. January 21 accounting order does not create intervening substantive law. Git existence and commitment sequence remain unverified.

## Curtis_v_School_Committee_of_Falmouth_merits_1997-01-21.md

**Case and dockets:** Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth; historical petition identifier No. 95-617; no separate merits docket assigned; OT1996.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-01-21; argued 1996-11-04.
**Result:** Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court affirmed on the granted constitutional questions, 9–0; no injunction or evidentiary remand.
**Version / lineage:** Original adjudication from the approved chunk 3 brief and frozen January 21 reconciled commitments; supersedes no completed event. Repository verification and commitment are pending the operator's permitted checks.

## Event

Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth. Merits decision, January 21, 1997. Argued November 4, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts, which on July 17, 1995, affirmed summary judgment for the school defendants, 420 Mass. 749, 652 N.E.2d 580. The questions concern parental liberty, free exercise, and whether the Constitution requires parental exclusion or notification when this school offers condoms without compelling participation.

The program covers grades seven through twelve. Junior-high students may request free condoms from the nurse and must receive counseling before receipt; the nurse supplies disease-information pamphlets. High-school students may obtain free condoms from the nurse or pay 75 cents at restroom machines; counseling is available on request and pamphlets are available in the nurse's office. The program emphasizes abstinence as the only certain means of avoiding sexually transmitted disease, directs staff to withhold personal opinions, and contains no parental exclusion or notification provision. Program entry is optional and abstention has no grade or disciplinary consequence. Parents and students seek an injunction unless exclusion and notice provisions are added.

## Opinion Topology

Justice Souter delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. There are no separate writings or partial joins.

## ProCD_v_Zeidenberg_merits_1997-01-27.md

**Case and dockets:** ProCD, Inc. v. Zeidenberg; Seventh Circuit No. 96-1139, 86 F.3d 1447; District Court judgment, 908 F. Supp. 640. No separate Supreme Court docket number assigned.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-01-27; October Term 1996, chunk 3, inventory entry 30.
**Result:** Affirmed, 9-0; the noncommercial-use covenant is enforceable on this record and its enforcement is not preempted by 17 U.S.C. §301.
**Version / lineage:** Initial decision, superseding no completed adjudication. Validated and durably preserved in the authorized filesystem workflow on October 12, 2026; no Git commitment is claimed or verified.

## Event

ProCD, Inc. v. Zeidenberg. Merits decision, January 27, 1997. On review of the Seventh Circuit's judgment at 86 F.3d 1447, appellate No. 96-1139, reversing the Western District of Wisconsin, 908 F. Supp. 640, and remanding for entry of judgment for ProCD. The Seventh Circuit heard argument May 23, 1996, and decided the appeal June 20, 1996.

The granted questions are whether packaged-software terms disclosed after payment may be enforceable when the buyer can review, return and obtain a refund; whether continued use after notice manifests acceptance under applicable commercial law; and whether 17 U.S.C. §301 preempts this covenant restricting commercial use or redistribution of factual information not protected by copyright. Wisconsin law governs formation. No companion matter is presented.

## Opinion Topology

Justice Ginsburg delivers the Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. The same unanimous opinion decides formation, §301 preemption and the mandate. No separate writing is filed.

**Case and dockets:** ProCD, Inc. v. Zeidenberg; Seventh Circuit No. 96-1139, 86 F.3d 1447; District Court judgment, 908 F. Supp. 640. No separate Supreme Court docket number assigned.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-01-27; October Term 1996, chunk 3, inventory entry 30.
**Result:** Affirmed, 9-0; the noncommercial-use covenant is enforceable on this record and its enforcement is not preempted by 17 U.S.C. §301.
**Version / lineage:** Initial decision, superseding no completed adjudication. Validated and durably preserved in the authorized filesystem workflow on October 12, 2026; no Git commitment is claimed or verified.
## Judgment, writings and assignment

The judgment of the Seventh Circuit is affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The appellate reversal of the District Court and its remand for entry of judgment enforcing the noncommercial-use covenant against Zeidenberg and Silken Mountain remain in force. A genuinely preserved, generally applicable contract defense not foreclosed below remains available, but the record identifies no developed defense requiring a new defense proceeding. The Court fixes no monetary amount, orders no injunction beyond the proved undertaking, and adjudicates no rights against nonassenting persons.

All nine support both formation and nonpreemption as stated, and the same mandate. Ginsburg writes the Opinion of the Court, joined in full by the other eight. No concurrence, dissent, partial join or judgment-only vote is published. The holdings are direct unanimous holdings; Marks has no work to do.

The Chief belongs to the settled judgment coalition on every component and assigns the single opinion to Ginsburg. Fit governs: all eight reconciled Associate commitments and Stone already share the actual-undertaking and clause-specific equivalence grounds. Ginsburg's Wolens opinion performs the central work of identifying the actual promise under ordinary law while separating it from external regulation and independently construing the federal clause. O'Connor's Feist opinion makes her the strongest alternative on copyright's public-domain boundary; that boundary remains intact, while the present opinion's distinct task is the relationship between a proved bargain and federal displacement. Stone is a supported candidate because his approved notice and remedy conditions frame the same distinction, but Ginsburg's direct prior treatment offers the more specific authorship advantage. No expansion accommodation, invented circulation history, historical authorship or workload inference supplies the choice.

The [assignment baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md), [bounded actual assignment statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) and [January 14 statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_JAN14_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) were read. OT1995 has 101 named Chief assignments and no recipient above half (maximum 27). The [actual January 21 statements](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_JAN21_ASSIGNMENT_STATEMENTS.md) add Youpee to O'Connor and Curtis to Souter, bringing the total before this case to 22: O'Connor seven; Souter six; Stevens three; Scalia two; Kennedy two; Ginsburg two. This is **named Chief assignment 23, Ginsburg's third in OT1996**, counting the single opinion once. The preceding-term concentration condition bars no candidate. These counts use actual assignment statements, without claiming Git existence or commitment order.


## Bibles_v_Oregon_Natural_Desert_Association_summary_review_1997-02-18.md

**Case and dockets:** Bibles v. Oregon Natural Desert Association; No. 96-713; OT1996, chunk 3, matter 31.
**Event and date:** Reasoned summary merits disposition; 1997-02-18.
**Result:** Certiorari granted; Ninth Circuit judgment vacated and remanded. Stone-Zsela joins the controlling reasons and remand but would reverse rather than vacate; the institutional action publishes no aggregate poll.
**Version / lineage:** Version 1.0, original assembly from preserved neutral, commitment and reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Filesystem-preserved handoffs are used under the no-Git instruction; no Git commitment or hash is certified.

## Event

Bibles v. Oregon Natural Desert Association, No. 96-713; October Term 1996. Reasoned summary merits disposition, February 18, 1997. On petition for a writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 83 F.3d 1168. The question is whether obtaining the Bureau of Land Management's newsletter mailing list to send recipients competing information supplies a cognizable public interest under FOIA Exemption 6. Organizational names and addresses have already been released; private individuals' information remains in dispute. The District of Oregon ordered disclosure, and the Ninth Circuit affirmed. No oral-argument date is reported.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Unsigned | No roll call announced; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela expressly joins the controlling explanation | Supports vacatur and remand | Exclusion of advocacy access and preservation of the actual Exemption 6 balance; Stone-Zsela does not join vacatur |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Chief Justice Stone-Zsela | No other Justice joins this writing | Agrees with the explanation and remand; would reverse rather than vacate | No separate withholding rule or additional merits ground |

## Public action, judgment, opinion form and assignment

The internal B1/B2 rule and actual-balance coalition is all nine. On B3, all eight Associates support grant, summary vacatur and remand; Stone supports grant, summary reversal and the same remand. The eight-Justice vacatur coalition is Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stone is the sole supporter of reversal instead of vacatur. The remand and no-final-withholding limits receive all nine. This paragraph is internal arithmetic, not an authorization to publish the petition poll or an otherwise undisclosed institutional roll call.

The settled correction issues as an institutional per curiam without an attributed author. Stevens is the senior Associate in the vacatur judgment coalition; the Chief's membership in the shared legal-correction and remand coalition does not authorize assigning from the vacatur coalition he rejects. No named author or named Chief assignment is inferred. The [actual assignment baseline](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK3_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md) expressly excludes unattributed institutional opinions from named-recipient accounting. Stone's limited separate writing adds no assignment. No draft exchanges, persuasion, new objections or changed Associate commitments are invented; final compatibility with the written operative rule suffices.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court applies Department of Justice v. Reporters Committee for Freedom of the Press, which limits the ordinary third-party public-interest inquiry to understanding government activity, and Department of Defense v. FLRA, which retains that inquiry when applying Exemption 6. A useful private project does not become information about government merely because a government list would help accomplish it. The Ninth Circuit recognized requester neutrality but then treated access to the Bureau's audience for competing communications as a substantial public benefit. That reasoning makes the asserted future use do work the statute assigns to the information disclosed.

## California_Division_of_Labor_Standards_Enforcement_v_Dillingham_merits_1997-02-18.md

**Case and dockets:** California Division of Labor Standards Enforcement v. Dillingham Construction, N.A., Inc.; No. 95-789; OT1996, chunk 3, matter 32.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-02-18; argued 1996-11-05.
**Result:** Ninth Circuit ERISA-preemption judgment reversed and remanded, 9-0, on a unanimous opinion by Souter; independently unresolved claims and remedies retain their proper postures.
**Version / lineage:** Version 1.0, original assembly from preserved neutral, commitment and reconciliation handoffs; supersedes no completed adjudication. Filesystem-preserved handoffs are used under the no-Git instruction; no Git commitment or hash is certified.

## Event

California Division of Labor Standards Enforcement v. Dillingham Construction, N.A., Inc., No. 95-789; October Term 1996. Argued November 5, 1996; decided February 18, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 57 F.3d 712. The question is whether ERISA §514(a) preempts the application of California's public-works wage rule permitting lower apprentice wages only for apprentices enrolled in state-approved programs. The District Court granted summary judgment to the state defendants; the Ninth Circuit reversed on ERISA grounds, leaving independent NLRA and contingent damages issues unresolved.

## Opinion Topology

Justice Souter writes the opinion of the Court, Parts I-III, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The complete opinion uses the concrete plan-command and economic-effects inquiry stated in Holdings. No separate opinion is filed.

## Event

Ehrlich v. City of Culver City, California Supreme Court No. S033642. Dismissal of the writ of certiorari as improvidently granted, February 18, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the California Supreme Court, reviewing its March 5, 1996 judgment, 12 Cal.4th 854, 911 P.2d 429, No. S033642. No separate argument date is stated. The questions concern applying nexus and rough proportionality to an individualized monetary development condition, the justification for the recreation charge, and the distinction between that charge and general regulatory requirements, including the art program. The state judgment reversed the Court of Appeal and directed further proceedings before the City to determine whether a recreation fee is justified and, if so, its extent; it sustained the art condition.

## Event

Federal Election Commission v. Legi-Tech, Inc.; D.C. Circuit Nos. 94-5379 and 95-5085, 75 F.3d 704. Merits decision, February 18, 1997. On review of the D.C. Circuit's February 16, 1996 judgment reversing dismissal without prejudice of the Commission's enforcement action. The questions concern whether actual reconsideration by the lawfully constituted Commission can cure the earlier structural defect, whether all investigation and conciliation steps must be repeated, and whether retroactive constitutional law requires dismissal despite a valid cure.

## Event

General Motors Corp. v. Tracy, No. 95-1232. Argued October 7, 1996; decided February 18, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Ohio, reviewing 73 Ohio St. 3d 29, 652 N.E.2d 188 (1995), which sustained the use-tax assessment. Questions: purchaser standing, the Commerce Clause comparison between regulated local distribution companies and independent gas marketers, and the separate Equal Protection Clause challenge. The purchases occurred October 1, 1986 through June 30, 1990. The state court relied on equal taxation of local and interstate nonqualifying marketers and a seller-only standing objection; its equal-protection discussion was submerged in that Commerce Clause analysis. The independent state-law classification and penalty-remission issues are outside the federal questions reviewed.

| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins Part I; would reverse the competitive-purchase application and remand; reserves equal protection |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Joins Part I; would vacate for comparison and conditional justification; reserves equal protection |

That case-specific fit serves the confined rationale she can join. Kennedy is a strong alternative through his Carbone opinion, but Souter's direct treatment of the public-service distinction gives him the clearer fit for this opinion. No policy attributed privately to Stone supplies O'Connor's choice.

The unanimous standing holding is a predicate within this single affirmance judgment, not a separate judgment granting relief. It does not transfer assignment authority to the Chief. This adds no named Chief assignment. No expansion accommodation is needed, and no draft exchange is invented. Stone and Stevens join Part I only; each of the seven affirmance Justices joins Parts I–III. Their distinct equal-protection and compensatory limits are preserved; no Marks synthesis is necessary.

## Event

Robinson v. Shell Oil Co., No. 95-1376. Argued November 6, 1996; decided February 18, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Fourth Circuit, reviewing its en banc judgment, 70 F.3d 325 (1995), No. 93-1562. The question is whether employees in Title VII §704(a) includes former employees alleging retaliatory postemployment conduct by a former employer. Shell discharged Robinson in 1991; while his EEOC racial-discharge charge was pending, a prospective employer sought a reference. His complaint alleges false information and negative references in retaliation. The claim was dismissed under Rule 12(b)(6) solely because he was no longer Shell's employee. The vacated panel disposition is not the judgment under review.

Breyer is a strong alternative through Town & Country's statutory-context reasoning, but Souter's work with Title VII's actual time units offers the closer fit. Fit selects Breyer: his Johnson opinion and conditional Behrens joins directly protect favorable factual assumptions while separating the purely legal question, and his published Pinder dissent requires precise explanation of why the present institutional attribution differs. Thomas fully supports the same result and is a capable alternative, but Breyer supplies the closer combined fit for favorable-record discipline and the contrary Pinder analogy, with the lower count. No expansion or invented change of view is necessary. The single signed Court opinion adds one to Breyer; Stone's concurrence adds none.

## Event

United States v. Brockamp and United States v. Scott, No. 95-1225. Argued December 3, 1996; decided February 18, 1997. One petition brings up the Ninth Circuit judgments of October 5, 1995, reported at 67 F.3d 260 and 70 F.3d 120. Brockamp's case concerns summary judgment and a disputed dementia claim; Scott's incapacity was found after trial.

## Auer_v_Robbins_merits_1997-02-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Auer v. Robbins; No. 95-897.

**Result:** Affirm, 9–0, subject to actual reimbursement and a promise of future compliance before the affected salary-basis claim is disposed of on a corrected-exemption ground; eight Justices retain controlling own-regulation interpretation, while Stone-Zsela proposes a different interpretive rule.
**Version / lineage:** Original assembly Record; supersedes no completed adjudication. Operator Git verification and commitment pending.

## Event

Auer v. Robbins, No. 95-897. Argued December 10, 1996; decided February 19, 1997. On certiorari to the Eighth Circuit. St. Louis police sergeants and a lieutenant challenge their treatment as exempt executive or administrative employees under the Fair Labor Standards Act. The questions concern the salary-basis rule's validity for public employees, practical exposure to impermissible disciplinary deductions and correction of an improper deduction. The Board also asserts that it is an arm of Missouri. Salary amount and the appellate duties determination are uncontested predicates; the latter is not independently reconsidered.

## Opinion Topology

|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I and III | Kennedy | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Non-arm status and governing controlling-weight interpretation; the Chief Justice does not join these grounds |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts II, IV and V | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Validity, practical-exposure construction/application, correction and conditional affirmance |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins Parts II, IV and V; agrees only with Part I's threshold resolution; proposes independent interpretation in place of Part III's controlling-weight rule |

## Form, assignment and final topology

Form first: one signed opinion after plenary argument addresses five linked components—Part I, non-arm status; Part II, validity; Part III, governing own-regulation interpretation; Part IV, practical exposure and supported application; Part V, correction eligibility and enforceable conditional affirmance. Distinct legal work, not accounting, determines these parts. One author can carry all five. Stone's separate concurrence states an approved, materially different interpretive rule; no separate opinion is manufactured for his silent threshold vote.

A fresh count from the actual source opinion tables, including the published new February 18 Records, yields 31 signed plenary leads before this assignment. ## Lynce_v_Mathis_threshold_1997-02-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Lynce v. Mathis, No. 95-7452; October Term 1996, chunk 4, inventory No. 41.
**Event and date:** Signed threshold decision, vacatur and remand; 1997-02-19.
**Result:** Vacate the reviewed certificate-denial disposition and remand through the Court of Appeals for exhaustion-first proceedings, 9–0; eight Associates join Thomas's threshold opinion. Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment with a noncontrolling conditional merits view.
**Version / lineage:** Initial completed Record following the stopped Run identified as 88d34e4. Stone's Version 2 approval is identified as 9d5688a and the prospective §8 revision as e0abdfd. The final frozen reconciliation controls over the provisional merits-reach handoff; no completed Lynce adjudication is superseded. These identifiers are supplied provenance; no Git verification is claimed.

## Event

Lynce v. Mathis, No. 95-7452. Argued November 4, 1996; decided February 19, 1997. Threshold decision on writ of certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit, reviewing its October 16, 1995 denial of a certificate of probable cause. The District Court dismissed the habeas petition on May 10, 1995 after adopting the view that the credits' administrative overcrowding purpose defeated the Ex Post Facto claim. The appellate certificate order stated no merits reasoning. The granted question asks whether retrospective cancellation of release credits already awarded and used to release Lynce increased the punishment attached to his offense. The Court first addresses exhaustion.

Lynce's offense occurred October 27, 1985; he received a 22-year sentence on April 14, 1986. The 1,860 provisional credit days helped produce release on October 1, 1992. A May 17, 1993 retaking order preceded his return to custody on June 8, 1993. The separate later cancellation of 335 administrative days is not challenged here.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports threshold-only vacatur and remand | Entire threshold opinion, including the exact concession, intervening-law and remedial limits; no Ex Post Facto merits holding |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Agrees with exhaustion-first vacatur and remand | Does not join the Court's opinion; states a conditional Ex Post Facto merits view and conditional remedy, neither controlling |

## Form, judgment coalitions and assignment

**Form first:** The case receives a signed, reasoned threshold opinion following plenary briefing and argument. The legal work is a claim-specific exhaustion determination explaining why the actual concession, maintained objection and governing law require a focused return; a bare remand would obscure those limits. The opinion resolves no Ex Post Facto merits question and orders no favorable habeas relief. One common threshold opinion and Stone's expressly approved concurrence preserve the supported structure. This form and proposition scope were settled before considering counts or authors.

Stone agrees with the judgment and the priority of exhaustion but does not join the complete rationale. ## Maryland_v_Wilson_merits_1997-02-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Maryland v. Wilson; No. 95-1268; OT1996, chunk 4.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-02-19; argued 1996-12-11.
**Result:** Reverse and remand, 6–3.
**Version / lineage:** Original Canonical Decision Record; supersedes no adjudication. Written durably October 13, 2026; Git verification and Git commitment remain unperformed under the user’s no-Git instruction.

## Event

Maryland v. Wilson, No. 95-1268. Argued December 11, 1996; decided February 19, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Maryland Court of Special Appeals, which affirmed suppression of cocaine that fell when Wilson obeyed an order to leave a lawfully stopped vehicle. The question is whether Mimms's categorical driver-exit authority extends to passengers. The State presents that categorical theory; the delayed stop, passenger movements and nervousness are not a preserved independent ground for the order.

## Opinion Topology

Justice Thomas delivers the Opinion of the Court, joined throughout by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Justice Kennedy files a dissent, joined by Stevens. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela files a separate dissent, joined by no other Justice. No dissenting writing supplies a join to the Court's rule.

## Opinion form and assignment

One signed coordinated opinion is appropriate because the argued grant presents a new passenger application of the driver safety rule and a bounded remedial consequence. No per curiam or divided lead opinion is warranted. This form and scope were fixed before counts or author selection. The six full-proposition eligible authors are O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. O'Connor assigns as senior Associate in the reversal coalition; the Chief is outside it. A fresh recount from the actual current-term Records' Public Projection opinion tables, preserved in [the source receipt](../tmp/RECOUNT_Maryland_v_Wilson_merits_1997-02-19.json), identifies 32 qualifying signed plenary leads. Each author delivering any part counts once per opinion, including Associate assignments; unsigned/summary/procedural actions, admitted sources, separate writings and stopped matters are excluded. LAVAS is included because its established October 15 argument made it a plenary event. The last counted event is Auer v. Robbins, February 19, 1997. Same-day ordering affects accounting only.

| Justice | Signed plenary leads before assignment |
|---|---:|
| Stone-Zsela | 0 |
| Stevens | 3 |
| O'Connor | 9 |
| Scalia | 3 |
| Kennedy | 3 |
| Souter | 8 |
| Thomas | 1 |
| Ginsburg | 3 |
| Breyer | 2 |

O'Connor assigns the single Opinion of the Court to Thomas. The exact eligible set is O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. For transparent accounting the eligible minimum is one and the minimum-plus-one pool is Thomas and Breyer; the Chief's balance rule does not bind this Associate assignment. O'Connor's case-specific choice rests on Thomas's Whren opinion, which she joined: it carefully separates objective initial-stop authority from the scope and duration of ensuing intrusions. The present opinion must add one confined safety command without making Whren a source of automatic custody, frisk or search power. Thomas's reconciled full commitment preserves those boundaries and the no-unpreserved-alternative limit. O'Connor herself is a strong alternative through Bostick and Robinette, and Breyer can carry the complete opinion; Thomas's already authored distinction provides the closer continuity for explaining precisely what this extension adds. No private Chief preference, historical authorship or additional join is used. The resulting signed lead adds one to Thomas. Kennedy writes the Associates' shared dissent because the recorded common ground concerns reasoned, accountable justification for this added passenger restraint; Stevens joins only that fully shared account. Stone writes his own approved rule and remedy separately. Neither separate writing counts.

The final public topology below preserves two distinct dissent accounts preserve the common Associate objection and Stone's more specifically approved modest-objective-reason formulation; no mutual join beyond frozen support is invented. Separate-writing authorship is not a counted lead assignment.

## Event

Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York, No. 95-1065. Argued October 16, 1996; decided February 19, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit's en banc judgment, 67 F.3d 377, affirming challenged preliminary-injunction provisions as modified to make verbal or nonverbal refusal explicit. Schenck and Saunders challenge fixed and moving fifteen-foot demonstration zones and the linked counseling condition under the First Amendment. The original injunction issued February 14, 1992; its preliminary relief remains subject to lawful further proceedings.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing or portion | Author | Joined by | Relationship and exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court in full | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Ginsburg | Full five-Justice coalition: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; all controlling portions |
| Court opinion's responsible-party, notice and actual-concert coverage boundary | Stone-Zsela | Breyer, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas additionally | All nine join this boundary; agreement does not resolve whether the fixed or mobile restrictions are sufficiently tailored |
| Court opinion's fixed-access and in-zone counseling holdings | Stone-Zsela | Breyer additionally | Six direct joins sustaining those components |
| Court opinion's moving-person, moving-vehicle and dependent-removal holdings | Stone-Zsela | Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas additionally | Eight direct joins invalidating mobile relief and removing the dependent distance |
| Concurrence | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins the Court; separately describes the precisely limited post-refusal provision he would permit on remand |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Justices Kennedy and Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join the responsible-party/notice/actual-concert boundary, moving insufficiency and its remedy but would invalidate fixed relief as well |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | No other Justice | Joins fixed relief; opposes present moving invalidation on first-instance interpretation and modification grounds |

Each controlling component has a direct rationale majority. No combination of distinct separate opinions supplies an additional rule.

## Form and assignment

One signed coordinated lead opinion is legally warranted for the several interdependent portions of a single preliminary decree. The opinion separates fixed access relief, the fixed-zone exception, moving-person and moving-vehicle relief, and dependent remedy because their legal fit differs, not to manipulate eligibility. Full-proposition authors are Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Ginsburg. Each supports all controlling portions. Breyer supports fixed relief but opposes present moving invalidation; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join moving insufficiency but oppose fixed relief. There is no reason to divide lead authorship when five eligible authors can carry the whole opinion. The Chief joins each prevailing judgment component and assigns. A fresh recount from the actual current-term public opinion tables, preserved in [the source receipt](../tmp/RECOUNT_Schenck_v_Pro_Choice_Network_merits_1997-02-19.json), yields 34 signed plenary lead opinions. Each delivering Justice counts once per opinion, including Associate assignments and argued LAVAS; unsigned/summary/procedural actions, admitted sources, separate writings and stopped matters do not count. The last counted event is Regents of the University of California v. Doe, February 19, 1997. Same-date alphabetical order supplies no substantive dependency.

## Event

Brown v. Hot, Sexy & Safer Productions, Inc., 68 F.3d 525. Argued November 6, 1996; decided February 24, 1997. On review of the First Circuit's October 23, 1995 judgment affirming dismissal of the federal claims. The granted questions concern parents' substantive educational liberty, students' privacy and bodily integrity, and Title IX. Two fifteen-year-old students allege that they were required to attend a ninety-minute AIDS-awareness presentation on April 8, 1992, featuring graphic language, simulated sexual conduct and suggestive demonstrations involving other selected students. They also allege weeks of coarse imitation afterward. The case is decided on the complaint and favorable inferences, without trial findings.

## Event

Dunn v. Commodity Futures Trading Commission, No. 95-1181. Argued November 13, 1996; decided February 25, 1997. On certiorari to the Second Circuit's judgment affirming appointment of a temporary equity receiver. The question is whether the Treasury Amendment excludes the off-exchange foreign-currency options from Commodity Exchange Act coverage. Investors supplied funds for trades in defendants' names; options or participations were not sold directly to those investors. The reviewed proceedings have not finally adjudicated fraud liability.

## Event

Andrade v. Mayfair Management, Inc.; Fourth Circuit No. 95-2476; judgment below reported at 88 F.3d 258. Argued and decided February 26, 1997, on writ of certiorari to the Fourth Circuit under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1). The questions concern responsibility under Title VII for harassment aided by delegated supervisory authority without higher-management notice, the distinction between agency attribution and negligence, and the required connection between workplace authority and misconduct. The jury awarded $25,000; the District Court entered renewed judgment as a matter of law for the employer, and the Court of Appeals affirmed. Constructive discharge is outside the grant.

## Event

United States v. Wells, No. 95-1228. Argued November 4, 1996; decided February 26, 1997. On certiorari to the Eighth Circuit's judgment vacating Wells's and Steele's conspiracy and bank-false-statement convictions and ordering a new trial. The questions are whether the Government may present its statutory argument despite its earlier treatment of materiality, and whether 18 U.S.C. §1014 makes materiality an element. Two other substantive counts ended in acquittals and are outside this review's remedial reach.

## Opinion Topology

| Court opinion, Part III: acquittals and independent-issue limits | Thomas | All nine Justices | Stevens joins these limits while retaining his different new-trial route |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Stevens alone | Joins Parts I and III; retains materiality and would affirm the new-trial judgment |

## Form, component joins and assignment

One signed Court opinion can carry the common reviewability rule, the eight-Justice statutory holding and vacatur, and the common remedial limits. Part I resolves reviewability, with all nine direct joins. Part II holds materiality absent and removes the appellate new-trial judgment's statutory premise, with Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Part III preserves the two acquittals and the proper posture of independent issues, with all nine direct joins to those limits. Stevens's agreement with Part III does not join vacatur or displace his proposed affirmance of the new-trial judgment under his retained materiality element.

The Chief Justice joins the judgment and assigns this single Court opinion. Before assignment, a fresh recount read the exact source opinion topologies in `../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK4_ASSIGNMENT_BASELINE.md` and the published Legi-Tech, Uhlrig, Brockamp, Auer, Wilson, Regents, Schenck, Brown and Dunn topologies. Events were ordered by effective date and same-date case name. LAVAS's signed post-argument lead counts under its separately supplied chronology; Tyler Murray and Humane Society also count. Each author is counted once per opinion, including Associate assignments and opinions with partial joins. Separate writings, unsigned or summary actions, admitted noncase sources and stopped Ehrlich/Lynce are excluded. No tally in a prior assignment statement supplies the count.

| Ginsburg | 4 |

Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer are eligible to carry every Court proposition, including Parts I and III's full common limits. Stevens supports those parts but rejects Part II, so he is not a complete-opinion author. The eligible minimum is two; the minimum-plus-one balance pool is Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer. O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Ginsburg remain fully eligible but outside this count-defined pool.

The Chief assigns Thomas. The common Court rationale is settled; fit governs rather than an invented accommodation or effort to obtain Stevens's statutory vote. Thomas's authored Staples analysis and reconciled X-Citement and Aguilar positions directly support distinguishing required culpability from an additional objective condition. That is the central legal work here: enforce every actual fault and coverage requirement while explaining why materiality is not another element, then preserve the jury and independent-trial-issue limits. Stone is the strongest alternative because his approved position precisely separates reviewability, statutory meaning and remedy; Thomas offers the more directly demonstrated criminal-elements fit at the same lowest count. Breyer also supplies offense-specific purpose analysis, while Kennedy's supported purpose/objective-capacity distinction fits at one higher count. All pool members can write the opinion. ## Event

United States v. Gonzales, No. 95-1605. Argued December 11, 1996; decided March 3, 1997, on writ of certiorari to the Tenth Circuit, 65 F.3d 814. The question is whether a federal court may make a §924(c) firearm term concurrent with an already imposed state term. Respondents received state terms arising from a drug sting and later federal drug and firearm convictions. The District Court made the drug terms concurrent with state imprisonment and the five-year firearm portions consecutive. The Court of Appeals accepted concurrency with state terms.

## Event

Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., No. 95-728. Argued October 15, 1996; decided March 3, 1997, on writ of certiorari to the Federal Circuit. The en banc court affirmed a jury verdict of nonliteral patent infringement and a permanent injunction. The questions concern survival of the doctrine of equivalents after the 1952 Act, claim-element limits and prosecution-history estoppel. Hilton Davis's amended claim covers approximately pH 6.0 to 9.0; Warner-Jenkinson uses pH 5.0, and Hilton Davis concedes no literal infringement. The reason for the lower pH boundary was not established.

| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Reserves intent-based infringement; joins the rest of the Court's opinion and the remand. |

## Form and balanced assignment

Balance pool: Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The Chief assigns Ginsburg. She supplies the precise old-prosecution accommodation on which her and Kennedy's full joins depend: consider whether a further opportunity is proper without eliminating the patentee burden or requiring automatic reopening. Her authorship offers a concrete advantage in stating that qualification faithfully with the enforceable claim boundary. Breyer, at count 3, is the strongest eligible alternative because his frozen practical reservation also supports fair consideration of an old prosecution record. Ginsburg's recorded objection defines the exact accommodation and supports the rebuttable expansion preference within the pool despite her one additional prior lead. **Controlling explanation.** Markman assigns legally operative claim meaning to the judge, including genuine technical-usage and credibility disputes embedded in construction under its controlling eight-Justice holding. It also preserves external infringement facts and does not permit a jury to erase an element of the construed claim. The unexplained amendment presents a legal gate to the equivalence claim that the Federal Circuit did not fully resolve. Once the court determines the permitted boundary, any remaining properly factual infringement work retains its ordinary place. Neither this remand nor Markman answers the complete judge-jury allocation for every equivalents issue or every appellate review standard. The Court orders neither patent invalidation nor an automatic new trial and makes no final comparison of the accused process with a legally available equivalent.

## Wright_v_MetroHealth_Medical_Center_merits_1997-03-03.md

**Case and dockets:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center; Sixth Circuit No. 94-3548; 58 F.3d 1130.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-03-03; OT1996, chunk 5, inventory 54.
**Result:** Affirmed on the granted federal questions, 9-0; ungranted Ohio-law dispositions left undisturbed.
**Version / lineage:** Initial adjudication; supersedes no completed event. Repository verification and commit are reserved to the operator under the no-Git instruction.

## Event

Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center; Sixth Circuit No. 94-3548; 58 F.3d 1130. Argued November 12, 1996; decided March 3, 1997, on writ of certiorari to the Sixth Circuit. The questions concern whether an anti-nepotism assignment policy directly and substantially burdens marriage, the resulting level of scrutiny and the justification supplied by emergency-flight operations. Martha Wright worked as a MetroHealth nurse and John Wright as a PHI helicopter pilot in the same LifeFlight unit. The Sixth Circuit affirmed federal summary judgment and Ohio-law dismissals under Rule 12(b)(6); the grant reaches the federal marriage and equal-protection questions. PHI settled during that appeal and ceased to be a party to it.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg | Both federal holdings, corrected record application and limited affirmance. |

## Form and balanced assignment

Before considering counts or authorship, the legal form selected was one signed plenary federal opinion with two common holdings and a bounded affirmance. No opinion division, new claim or separate state merits opinion is used to affect the counts.

**Balanced-assignment count category:** Counted signed plenary lead; one credit to Breyer.

The actual opinion tables of earlier-effective Records, including Records already written in this Run, were recounted immediately before assignment; [recount receipt](../tmp/COUNT_BEFORE_WRIGHT.json). Counts: Stone-Zsela 3; Stevens 4; O'Connor 9; Scalia 4; Kennedy 4; Souter 8; Thomas 4; Ginsburg 5; Breyer 3. Last counted event: Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co.; No. 95-728. (1997-03-03).

Eligible authors, each supporting every governing proposition in full: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Minimum eligible count: 3. Balance pool: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. The Chief joins the judgment coalition and assigns Breyer. Breyer's frozen practical-operation ground expressly coordinates the qualified policy promise, the actual comparable offer, the threatened geographic move and the later interim arrangement. Those distinctions are the necessary legal work of the record-specific burden rule. Stone is the strongest pool alternative at the same lowest count because his approved core equally preserves the actual comparable-transfer limit and separate ordinary employment law. Breyer can express that objective with the repaired sequence and all eight other joins intact; assigning him distributes the leads without altering the common rule. No unresolved formulation objection remains and no expansion rationale or special capability exclusion is invented.

**Balanced-assignment count category:** Counted signed plenary lead; one credit to Breyer.
### The actual assignment burden does not require heightened scrutiny

**Holding and operative rule.** An employment rule does not require heightened constitutional scrutiny merely because marriage triggers an assignment consequence; its actual operation must directly and substantially interfere with the decision to marry. On this record, the close-interaction assignment restriction, considered with the actual same-grade-and-pay hospital-transfer alternative and the entire threatened-transfer sequence, imposes an incidental employment consequence rather than that substantial marriage barrier.

**Controlling explanation.** Loving and Zablocki protect marriage as a fundamental liberty; Jobst distinguishes substantial interference with marrying from incidental marital-status consequences. The fact that the Wrights married does not alone defeat their challenge, and the absence of a completed discharge is not a universal safe harbor. Paragraph C allows affected employees to choose who moves, gives thirty calendar days after notice before the less senior hospital employee moves by default, and requires all reasonable attempts to provide comparable responsibility and pay. That effort obligation is not a guarantee. Separately, MetroHealth actually offered Martha a transfer to any available same-grade-and-pay position in another unit; she declined. John worked for contractor PHI, so the general policy does not establish that both spouses were hospital employees. After their marriage, MetroHealth sought John's transfer and PHI announced relocation to Louisiana effective January 19, 1993. That threatened geographic disruption was substantial enough to require consideration, but no completed move, discharge or demonstrated pay reduction occurred, and the reported hospital-transfer alternative distinguishes an unavoidable marriage barrier. After suit, the defendants agreed that John would remain and Martha would move; during trial the spouses stayed in LifeFlight on separate flight schedules. Those accommodations neither erase the original threat nor promise permanent protection. The Court does not find an unreported vacancy or approve future loss of livelihood, unavoidable separation or materially inferior work.

**Holding and operative rule.** An employment rule does not require heightened constitutional scrutiny merely because marriage triggers an assignment consequence; its actual operation must directly and substantially interfere with the decision to marry. On this record, the close-interaction assignment restriction, considered with the actual same-grade-and-pay hospital-transfer alternative and the entire threatened-transfer sequence, imposes an incidental employment consequence rather than that substantial marriage barrier.
**Holding and operative rule.** Under ordinary rational-basis review, restricting spouses' simultaneous assignments in a close-interaction emergency-flight unit is constitutionally permissible when the restriction rationally relates to conflicts, supervision, safety and effective teamwork. The rule need not be the least restrictive arrangement or rest on proof that every married pair will impair operations, but it may not rest on bare harm or irrational prejudice unrelated to the disadvantage imposed.
**Common rule and corrected application:** Marriage is a fundamental liberty. A public-employment rule does not require heightened scrutiny merely because marriage triggers an assignment consequence; the asserted interference must directly and substantially burden the decision to marry. Actual operation matters. Completing the marriage, avoiding a completed discharge or obtaining temporary litigation protection is not by itself dispositive of the constitutional claim.
**Ordinary-review holding:** The close-interaction assignment restriction rationally relates to conflicts, supervision, safety and effective emergency teamwork. The spouses' work in the same operational unit matters despite their nominally different employers. Rational-basis review allows reasonable predictions and imperfect classifications without demanding an accident, proof that every married pair is unsafe or adoption of the least restrictive schedule. The successful interim scheduling alternative does not itself establish irrationality. Heller and Romer still require a relationship to the actual disadvantage and do not permit bare harm or irrational prejudice; Tracy illustrates a supported operational distinction without establishing a new rule about marriage.
**Precedent and scope:** Loving and Zablocki remain controlling protections for the decision to marry; Jobst supplies the distinction between substantial marriage interference and incidental marital-status consequences. No general immunity for anti-nepotism rules, suspect marital-status classification, right to a particular joint assignment, compulsory individualized exception, mandatory safety study or constitutional approval of livelihood loss follows. No new First Amendment association, career-liberty, sex-discrimination or Ohio-law holding is entered.

## Event

Nabozny v. Podlesny; Seventh Circuit No. 95-3634, 92 F.3d 446. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit. Decided March 10, 1997, following plenary merits review.

The Seventh Circuit’s July 31, 1996 judgment reinstated sex and sexual-orientation equal-protection claims, rejected the preserved due-process theories and denied individual qualified immunity on the supported equality account. The questions concern intentional discriminatory denial of school protection, the asserted due-process duty arising from attendance and known abuse, and individual immunity. The preserved due-process theories allege increased risk through failure to punish and a policy encouraging a harmful environment; the generalized special-relationship argument was expressly disavowed below and is not revived.

Nabozny’s favorable account describes repeated anti-gay abuse, assaults, a classroom mock assault and a beating causing internal bleeding, together with complaints, promises, allegedly discriminatory responses and departures from ordinary enforcement. Defendants dispute that account. The whole record is available on equality because the District Court ventured beyond the moving grounds; the Court assumes its availability for due process without resolving that separate local-rule issue.

## Brown_v_Polk_County_merits_1997-03-17.md

**Case and dockets:** Brown v. Polk County; Eighth Circuit No. 93-3313, 61 F.3d 650 (en banc); no separate Supreme Court merits docket supplied.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-03-17; OT1996, chunk 5.

**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record for this event; supersedes no completed adjudication. Written under the user-authorized no-Git workflow; no Git commitment is claimed.

## Event

Brown v. Polk County; Eighth Circuit No. 93-3313, 61 F.3d 650 (en banc). Argued November 13, 1996; decided March 17, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit. Its en banc judgment issued July 31, 1995, and rehearing was denied August 21, 1995.

After a five-day bench trial, the District Court ruled for the County and its officials. The en banc court reversed the statutory religious-discharge judgment and the judgment on the two actually argued constitutional restrictions, remanding for appropriate relief; it affirmed the separate statutory race judgment. Review concerns religious accommodation, the breadth of the religion-specific directives, public-employer authority and the asserted operational and Establishment justifications.

Isaiah Brown directed the County’s information-services department. The record separately identifies secretarial typing of Bible-study notes, prework office prayer, voluntary and irregular department-meeting prayers, and a single religious work-ethic reference. The County ordered an end to activities characterized as witnessing, proselytizing or counseling and removal of religious items, including a desk Bible. Statutory claims challenge the later discharge; the constitutional claims actually argued challenge the reprimand and removal order, not termination.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Affirms reviewed judgment | Parts I–IV |
