| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins the stated Court portions and the full judgment | Additional speech and attribution analysis |

**Opinion form established before assignment:** One signed Opinion of the Court: Part I on the two religion-specific directives; Part II on the four actual activity categories and accommodation; Part III on the original mixed-motive rule, completed-record same-decision failure and closed statutory liability; Part IV on distinct remedial tracks and limits. Eight Associates directly support every part. Stone joins Parts I, II and IV and supports Part III’s disposition through the approved standing fallback without adding its reasons. His additional attribution and employee-speech account receives a separate concurrence. These divisions reflect actual issues and a real partial join, not author-count considerations.
**Balanced-assignment count category:** Counted signed plenary lead opinion. The single lead is counted once for its author; separate writings are not counted.

His full support for the other parts does not authorize division when an eligible Associate can carry the complete opinion. **Balance pool:** Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, counts four or five, at most one above the eligible minimum of four. O’Connor and Souter remain eligible but outside the pool.

**Author chosen:** Stevens. Fit governs because the precise limited opinion already has the necessary direct joins. Its central work is to enforce Beadle’s supported-burden inquiry without repeating the lower court’s actual-disruption-only language, distinguish statutory accommodation from religion-specific constitutional excess, and preserve the existing mixed-motive liability/remedy allocation. Stevens’s Beadle and NTEU opinions and his Price Waterhouse participation demonstrate the relevant work across those specific questions. His stronger establishment concerns remain within the frozen scope: the opinion does not declare all supervisor-led prayer private or constitutional. Breyer is the strongest eligible pool alternative because his Andrade and Wright opinions carefully separate actual authority, burdens, proof and remedial posture. Stevens has the more direct demonstrated formulation experience with the operative accommodation standard and prospective speech burden; neither count nor authorship creates a new join. Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg remain fully capable and eligible, with no identified necessary accommodation that makes one of them the stronger choice. Stone is not compared as an eligible alternative because his Part III reason is absent, not because of subject-matter ability, office or prior count.

**Final compatibility:** Each Associate joins all four parts at the reconciled scope. Stone joins Parts I, II and IV, concurs in Part III’s disposition through fallback, and adds only his separate attribution/speech account. Statutory liability remains closed, the relief-only tracks remain distinct and no constitutional termination claim is added. No Associate commitment changes in assembly and no reassignment is needed.

## Commissioner_v_Estate_of_Hubert_merits_1997-03-18.md

**Case and dockets:** Commissioner v. Estate of Hubert; No. 95-1402.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-03-18; OT1996, chunk 5.
**Result:** Affirm the Eleventh Circuit and the reviewed Tax Court result, 7–2; Scalia and Breyer dissent and would reverse and remand for calculation of a required reduction.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record for this event; supersedes no completed adjudication. Written under the user-authorized no-Git workflow; no Git commitment is claimed.

## Event

Commissioner v. Estate of Hubert, No. 95-1402. Argued November 12, 1996; decided March 18, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit, which affirmed the Tax Court in Estate of Hubert v. Commissioner, 63 F.3d 1083, affirming 101 T.C. 314.

The question is whether administration expenses paid from postmortem income attributable to marital and charitable residuary bequests require reduction of their estate-tax deductions. Nearly $2 million in expenses was paid from an estate exceeding $30 million: approximately $500,000 from principal and the remainder from income. The estate reduced deductions for principal-funded expenses. The Commissioner challenges the treatment of the income-funded amount. Qualification of the trusts was stipulated; the dispute is valuation.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Affirms both deductions | Whole confined opinion; material limitation and case-specific burden, without a valuation-date-only rule |
| Dissent | Scalia | Breyer | Would reverse and remand for calculation | Reduction principle; method and amount reserved, with Breyer’s additional economic qualification stated below |

**Opinion form established before assignment:** One signed Opinion of the Court affirms on the confined material-limitation construction and the Commissioner’s failure to maintain a quantitative basis for reversal. A single substantive dissent states Scalia and Breyer’s common reduction/remand position and preserves Breyer’s distinct concern about timing and economic equivalence. Seven direct joins establish a common Court rationale; no Marks aggregation or broad valuation-date holding is adopted. Form and legal scope precede all counts.
**Balanced-assignment count category:** Counted signed plenary lead opinion. The single lead is counted once for its author; separate writings are not counted.
## Assignment

**Assignment authority:** The Chief joins the affirming judgment coalition and its full confined ground and assigns the single signed lead. The seven-Justice form and limited tax rationale were established before the recount; the broader valuation theory was excluded on the merits of the frozen join barriers, not to alter counts.

## Young_v_Harper_merits_1997-03-18.md

**Case and dockets:** Young v. Harper; No. 95-1598.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-03-18; OT1996, chunk 5.
**Result:** Affirm the Tenth Circuit’s judgment, 9–0; eight Associates adopt review-timing rationale, all nine adopt protected-liberty and revocation-process holdings.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record for this event; supersedes no completed adjudication. Written under the user-authorized no-Git workflow; no Git commitment is claimed.

## Event

Young v. Harper, No. 95-1598. Argued December 9, 1996; decided March 18, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, which reversed the denial of federal habeas relief. The question is whether termination of Oklahoma preparole community release requires the procedures governing revocation of parole.

Harper was released in October 1990 to live at home, work and report to a parole officer under parole-like conditions. After five apparently uneventful months, the Governor denied formal parole; on March 14, 1991, an officer telephoned and directed him to return immediately to prison without the required revocation process. An August 1991 procedure later addressing parole denial postdated that return. The original procedure was silent on the effect of parole denial, and Harper’s acknowledgment tied community status to compliance with release conditions.

The appellate rehearing petition was two days late, but the Tenth Circuit granted leave, treated it as timely and withheld the mandate until denial. Certiorari was sought within 90 days of that denial.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports timely review and affirmance | Parts I–IV in full |
| Same opinion, merits and remedy | Breyer | Stone-Zsela | Supports affirmance | Parts II–IV; agrees with the timely-review resolution without joining Part I’s rationale |

**Opinion form established before assignment:** One signed lead opinion, Part I on accepted rehearing and timely Supreme Court review, Part II on existing conditional community liberty and the original/later-rule distinction, Part III on Morrissey procedures with their complete qualifications, and Part IV on the bounded affirmance and remedy. Eight Associates directly support all parts; Stone supports Parts II–IV expressly and review on the standing fallback only. A single eligible Associate can carry the entire opinion, so no split authorship or separate opinion is needed. Parts correspond to actual distinct questions and join scope, not balance.
**Balanced-assignment count category:** Counted signed plenary lead opinion. The single lead is counted once for its author; separate writings are not counted.

O’Connor and Souter remain eligible but outside the pool.

**Author chosen:** Breyer. Fit governs: no unresolved accommodation is needed to preserve the eight full joins. The opinion must distinguish existing community liberty from an unreceived parole benefit and from the nonexclusive state-entitlement route, then preserve Morrissey’s practical administrative stages and the exact original/later-rule sequence. Breyer’s controlling Sandin opinion directly formulated the preserved route on which this opinion relies, and his current frozen commitment keeps that distinction without an unnecessary program-entitlement remand or new process test. Thomas is the strongest lower-count pool alternative, given his Lynce opinion’s distinction between an actual habeas threshold and an unsupported defect; he too can write the complete timing and merits opinion. Breyer’s demonstrated formulation of the direct-liberty/state-entitlement boundary supplies the stronger case-specific fit at equal counts. Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg are not excluded by experience, historical votes or prior authorship; their full eligibility is retained, and no necessary differing formulation identifies one of them instead. Stone is excluded only by his absent timing rationale, not by capability or office. Historical Young authorship plays no role.

There is no new Associate position, complete-hearing-before-arrest rule, emergency-only detention condition, numerical deadline or automatic release. ## Bennett_v_Spear_merits_1997-03-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Bennett v. Spear; No. 95-813.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-03-19; OT1996, chunk 5.
**Result:** Reverse dismissal of the reviewed ESA/APA claims and remand, 9–0; leave the unchallenged NEPA dismissal undisturbed.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record for this event; supersedes no completed adjudication. Written under the user-authorized no-Git workflow; no Git commitment is claimed.

## Event

Bennett v. Spear, No. 95-813. Argued November 13, 1996; decided March 19, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, which affirmed dismissal in Bennett v. Plenert, 63 F.3d 915.

The Bureau of Reclamation consulted the Fish and Wildlife Service about the Klamath Irrigation Project and two endangered fish species. The Service’s Biological Opinion found likely jeopardy and identified minimum reservoir levels as reasonable and prudent alternatives; the Bureau stated it would comply. Irrigation districts and ranch operators allege injury from reduced water and challenge the Opinion under the Endangered Species Act and Administrative Procedure Act. The questions are constitutional standing, the scope of the statutory review channels, the provision-specific zone of interests and final agency action. A fourth claim alleging an unassessed de facto habitat designation under NEPA was dismissed below and that dismissal is unchallenged.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Reverses reviewed dismissal and remands | Whole opinion; NEPA dismissal outside review |

**Opinion form established before assignment:** One signed unanimous Opinion of the Court separates Article III, citizen-suit scope, APA availability/provision-specific interests and completed legally consequential action. The NEPA dismissal remains outside the reviewed components. No separate opinion or alternative biological merits holding is needed. Form, scope and common full grounds precede counts.
**Balanced-assignment count category:** Counted signed plenary lead opinion. The single lead is counted once for its author; separate writings are not counted.

O’Connor and Souter remain eligible but outside the pool. One author can carry the complete opinion; no division is warranted.

**Author chosen:** Scalia. Fit governs because no recorded disagreement requires an accommodation to secure or retain the nine full joins. The opinion must enforce concrete personal injury and litigation-stage proof while recognizing that the alleged water loss supplies the personal causal link absent from an abstract procedural grievance; it must then distinguish that constitutional inquiry from each enacted review channel and the provision-specific APA inquiry. Scalia’s own Lujan reasoning demonstrates the relevant separation of personal interest, procedural right and proof stage, and his frozen Bennett commitment applies that separation while giving the citizen-suit text its breadth only within its actual channels. That combination supplies the strongest case-specific fit for the principal standing dismissal and its alternative reviewability grounds.

The Chief is the strongest pool alternative at the same count. His Franklin opinion and his approved Bennett supplement demonstrate a concrete strength in completed agency action, practical legal consequences and separation of reviewability from merits and remedy. He can faithfully carry the entire opinion. Scalia’s more directly demonstrated articulation of the constitutional standing boundary, paired here with his express channel-specific commitment, provides the meaningful advantage for this case; the Chief’s approved objective is fully preserved without a new formulation or selective doctrinal development requiring his authorship. Scalia’s earlier Franklin disagreement is not a barrier: his frozen position accepts this statement’s distinct conditional taking consequences and applies Franklin at its actual controlling scope. Thomas has the same count and full support, but the record identifies no corresponding advantage over the author of the concrete-interest analysis the opinion applies. Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer remain fully capable pool members; no different accommodation or lower count selects them. No historical Bennett authorship, general stylistic reputation or assumed inability supplies the choice.

**Final compatibility:** All nine retain direct joins to pleaded Article III sufficiency, the distinct citizen-suit channels, provision-specific APA review and completed agency action with conditional taking consequences. The actual Franklin majority and Dalton preclusion holding govern; noncontrolling predecessor rationales are not converted into law. Notice and other statutory prerequisites remain unadjudicated, the designation and biological merits remain open, and the unchallenged NEPA dismissal remains undisturbed. No fallback, changed Associate commitment, invented circulation history or separate writing is needed.


**Assignment authority:** The Chief directly supports the reviewed reversal and remand and assigns the lead. The single signed opinion, its four distinct reviewability propositions, all nine full joins and the preserved NEPA boundary were established before counting or choosing an author.

Section 1533 economic-impact consideration and discretionary exclusion with the extinction limit remain distinct; consultation under section 1536 is not wholesale economic balancing. The incidental-take statement's statutory predicates and section 1536(o)(2)'s compliance condition are not unconditional immunity. Each citizen-suit subsection retains its own notice/enforcement restrictions, including the distinct emergency exception for the Secretary-duty channel, as copied in Appendix B. APA sections 702/704 retain their independent limits. None of these unlitigated conditions is found satisfied or waived. A common signed opinion may carry the eight direct joins; no separate Associate writing is necessary. Assignment remains for assembly.

## Ingebretsen_v_Jackson_Public_School_District_merits_1997-03-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District; court-of-appeals No. 94-60631.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-03-19; OT1996, chunk 6.
**Result:** Standing affirmed 9–0; injunction affirmed in part, vacated in part and remanded 7–2; intervention as of right denial affirmed 8–1; permissive intervention denial affirmed 9–0.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record for this event; supersedes no completed adjudication. Durable written handoff under the user-authorized no-Git workflow; repository commitment and provenance verification remain with the operator.

## Event

**Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District**, court-of-appeals No. 94-60631, 88 F.3d 274. Merits decision, March 19, 1997. On review of the Fifth Circuit's affirmance of a preliminary injunction against Mississippi's school-prayer statute, its graduation exception and denials of student intervention. Merits hearing and decision: March 19, 1997 under the established Court calendar; no separate argument date is supplied.

The questions concern preimplementation standing; official versus private student prayer, including school pressure and a statutory disclaimer; lawful reach and severability of interim relief; the graduation exception; and intervention under Rule 24.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Breyer throughout; other joins below | Controlling portions supporting each judgment component | I standing; II sponsorship; III school pressure; IV relief; V-A intervention as of right; V-B permissive intervention |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela | Supports standing and injunction judgment; dissents from V-A judgment; agrees only with V-B judgment | I, II and IV; no III or V reasons |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Ginsburg | Kennedy | Supports every judgment component | I, III, IV and V; no II |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Ginsburg | Scalia, Thomas | Supports standing and intervention; dissents from retained injunction and graduation extension | I, V, and IV's independently stated protection of private speech and lawful provisions; no II, III or retained-restraint application |
| Concurrence in part | Kennedy | — | Explains withheld sponsorship-only join | Pressure rationale and private-expression boundary |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Would vacate the presently retained restraint | Predicted official participation is insufficient at this preliminary stage |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | — | Would remand intervention as of right for a specific adequacy determination | Sponsorship ground and Rule 24(a)(2) disagreement; adds no ground on permissive intervention |

**Research cutoff:** 1997-03-19. All nine Justices participate; no case-specific exclusion is established. The established merits schedule and grant control; no distinct historical argument date is invented. The entering-law slice and validated neutral supplement apply the actual Lee sponsorship and pressure majorities separately, Pinette, Chabad, Rosenberger, Good News, Brown, Lujan and Rule 24. Same-day Bennett is assignment evidence only. This is interlocutory review of a hearing-supported preliminary injunction, not final universal invalidation of student religious expression.
**Opinion form established before assignment:** One signed plenary lead opinion separates standing (I), sponsorship (II), school pressure (III), provision-sensitive relief (IV), intervention as of right (V-A), and permissive intervention (V-B). A common author can state all controlling portions. Partial joins and three substantive separate writings preserve Kennedy's pressure ground, Scalia and Thomas's remedial disagreement, and the Chief's intervention disagreement. Form and proposition scope rest on the legal differences, not counts. This is one counted lead.
## Assignment

The Chief assigns the controlling portions supporting the standing and injunction judgment. Stevens, the senior Associate in the eight-Justice intervention majority, assigns V-A. The shared lead can be assigned to one Justice who supports all its propositions; dividing authorship is unnecessary. Both assign Ginsburg. The Chief's balanced pool governs his portions and the common author.

O'Connor nine and Souter eight are outside the pool. Stone's lower count does not cure his withheld pressure and intervention joins, and his fallback supplies no authorship eligibility. He could carry some parts but a whole-opinion author exists, so the no-unnecessary-division rule controls.

**Author chosen:** Ginsburg. Fit, not expansion, governs the settled shared grounds. Her Chabad and Good News positions and Brown join furnish a concrete basis for distinguishing contextual government attribution from protection of private expression while tailoring relief to actual state action. Her frozen commitment also treats the Attorney General's actual representation rather than public identity as dispositive. Stevens is the strongest pool alternative, with direct joins to both Lee grounds and the same count; his Pinette and Good News positions equally support the official-worship boundary. Accepts that genuinely law-compelled worship is prohibited and that government prescription is legally distinct from permitting private speech. | Souter | J: reach. O: preserve restraint of official program worship/content selection and the independent pressure application. G: vacate exemption. R: narrow and remand. A/B: affirm under individualized adequacy, not government identity. | Joined both Lee grounds and authored its broader neutrality concurrence; authored Chabad's contextual rule and Good News Part II; joined Lujan's operative standing portions. | No forum label, student choice or disclaimer automatically removes official religious affiliation. G: would preserve the student-initiated graduation allowance at this record stage, without treating it as an unconditional exemption from all coercion claims. R: vacate the blanket restraint and remand without the majority's present restraint. A/B: affirm both denials. | Joined Scalia's Lee dissent and Pinette concurrence; joined Kennedy's Good News private-expression concurrence, the Court's access and remedy portions, Rosenberger's actual program holding and Lujan's concrete-injury holdings. | Does not join contextual endorsement, a new categorical attribution of student speech, or presumed official authorship contrary to Section 2. Legal coercion and actual government prescription remain distinct; no later nonincorporation theory or later school-prayer opinion is imported. Joins Scalia's proposed separate position at these grounds. |
| Ginsburg | J: reach. O: preserve restraint on official religious adoption/supervision and on school-directed participation pressure. G: vacate exemption. R: narrow and remand. A/B: affirm both denials on actual adequacy and discretion. | Joined Chabad Part III and Good News Part II; her Pinette dissent distinguishes the adequacy of actual relief from an offered safeguard; joined Kiryas Joel's actual neutrality holdings and Brown v. Polk County's control/private-expression distinction. | The decree must identify what is restrained and what remains private. No mere assurance or disclaimer establishes an effective private-use safeguard, but uncertainty cannot justify suppressing every independent religious practice. Joins sponsorship and bounded pressure grounds; no adoption of a new comprehensive Establishment Clause test. |
| Breyer | J: reach. O: preserve supported restraint of official worship and controlled participation pressure. G: vacate exemption. R: narrow and remand. A/B: affirm on this record. | Joined Pinette's contextual explanation and Souter's narrower attribution concurrence; joined Chabad Part III and Good News Part II; joined Brown v. Polk County's practical distinction between permissible control and overbroad religious suppression. | The record's school control, attendance and administration matter together. Does not transform every safeguard into a mandatory checklist, make every prayer on school property official, or require completed injury before interim relief. Supports the separate effect of Section 2 and practical remedial tailoring. |

## Turner_Broadcasting_System_Inc_v_FCC_merits_1997-03-31.md

**Case and dockets:** Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. Federal Communications Commission; No. 95-992.
**Event and date:** Merits decision; 1997-03-31; OT1996, chunk 6.
**Result:** Affirm reviewed full-power must-carry core, 5–4; all nine decline separate low-power and market-modification classifications.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record for this event; supersedes no completed adjudication. Durable written handoff under the user-authorized no-Git workflow; repository commitment and provenance verification remain with the operator.

## Event

**Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. Federal Communications Commission**, No. 95-992. Merits decision, March 31, 1997. Argued October 7, 1996. On direct appeal from the three-judge District Court for the District of Columbia, 910 F. Supp. 734, after the 1994 remand. The granted question asks whether the developed record establishes advancement and fit under the intermediate scrutiny prescribed for the general full-power must-carry core.

**Statutory setting relevant to fit:** Under 47 U.S.C.§534, systems with 12 or fewer usable activated channels must carry at least three local commercial signals; systems also having 300 or fewer subscribers are exempt from that section only while they delete no broadcast signal. Systems with more than 12 channels carry eligible local commercial signals up to one-third of usable activated capacity. This is not a combined commercial-and-educational ceiling. Substantially duplicative commercial signals already represented in carriage and additional same-network affiliates need not be carried; voluntary duplicates and multiple affiliates count toward the obligation. When eligible stations exceed the required number, the operator selects among them, but cannot substitute low power for full power and must choose the elected network's affiliate with the closest city-of-license reference point to the principal headend.

Section535 adds separate educational duties. Systems with 12 or fewer channels carry one qualified local educational signal and may carry more. Systems with 13–36 carry at least one and need not carry more than three, subject to continued carriage; they may carry more. If no qualified local station is present, the former category must import one qualified educational signal and the latter at least one. For that importation, the smallest systems need not remove programming actually provided on March 29, 1990 but must use the first available channel. All qualified local educational stations carried on that date retain carriage unless operator and station give written waiver consent. Systems above36 channels, including those expanding beyond36, carry requesting qualified local educational stations; a system required to carry three need not add stations substantially duplicating another qualified local educational station requesting carriage. This is not a general three-station cap. A13–36-channel system already carrying a State-network educational affiliate need not add another same-network affiliate whose programming substantially duplicates the carried affiliate.

Commercial distant signals require indemnification of increased copyright liability. A commercial station below the specified headend levels (−45dBm UHF; −49dBm VHF) is excluded if it does not agree to bear the costs of delivering a good-quality or baseband signal. Educational carriage is not required without delivery of a good-quality or baseband signal; its distant-signal indemnity exception concerns adding a signal and expressly does not erase the continued-carriage rule. These are actual programming obligations, including primary video, audio and captions. Commercial carriage includes technically feasible program-related blanking-interval or subcarrier material; educational carriage includes technically feasible program-related ancillary material necessary for disability access, education or language purposes. Other ancillary material remains discretionary. Positioning and availability duties also constrain operation; the law does not merely reserve empty channels. No new dispute over ancillary material, positioning, payment, notice or enforcement is decided.

The reserved low-power duty depends on an insufficient number of full-power local commercial stations: systems with35 or fewer channels carry one qualified low-power station, and larger systems two. Its disputed programming criterion concerns local needs inadequately served because of full-power stations' geographic distance. The separate market-modification factor asks whether another eligible station supplies the community news, sports or events; it is not that requirement imposed on the requesting station. These provisions' independent classifications remain outside the granted question.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Supports full-power affirmance | I scope; II preservation/diversity advancement; III fit and summary disposition |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg | Agree with scope; dissent from core judgment | I only; no II or III |
| Concurrence | Breyer | — | Joins the common Court opinion | Competing speech interests; no independent economic-predation justification |
| Concurrence | Stevens | — | Joins the common Court opinion | Sufficiency of congressional predictive evidence without endorsing the former plurality's whole insufficiency account |
| Dissent in part | O'Connor | Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg | Would reverse core approval; joins scope portion | Independent intermediate-fit failure and retained classification objection |

The controlling rationale is directly shared preservation and diversity, with five joins. No independently sufficient anticompetitive application is adopted, and no combination of different rationales is used to make a majority.

**Opinion form established before assignment:** One signed lead states a five-Justice common preservation/diversity justification, advancement and fit, with an all-Justice scope portion. A separate four-Justice dissent preserves the intermediate-fit and classification objections. Breyer explains the competing speech interests in a concurrence joining the common opinion. Stevens explains his more deferential view of the already available congressional evidence while also joining. The common lead does not adopt an independent economic-predation/fair-competition application: only four support that separate ground, while the common preservation/diversity ground has five direct joins. This choice reflects actual substantive support before any count or author selection, requires no divided authorship or Marks inference, and counts as one signed plenary lead.

## Event

Opuku-Boateng v. California. Merits decision, April 7, 1997, on review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 95 F.3d 1461, No. 94-16542. The Ninth Circuit reversed the District Court's trial judgment for California and remanded for appropriate relief. The questions are whether Title VII protects a public-employment applicant's known religious conflict on the same terms as an employee's, and whether California established undue hardship from the supported scheduling alternatives under the governing accommodation standard. The case concerns refusal to complete a permanent plant-quarantine inspector appointment at the continuously operating Yermo station because of Friday-sundown-to-Saturday-sundown observance.

## Event

Chandler v. Miller, No. 96-126. Argued January 14, 1997; decided April 15, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit, which affirmed final judgment for Georgia on December 19, 1995. The question is whether Georgia's requirement that candidates for designated legislative, executive and judicial offices present a negative drug-test certificate within thirty days before qualifying is a reasonable special-needs search under the Fourth Amendment. The action began in May 1994. Chandler's uncontested intention to run again preserves the prospective claim despite the completed 1994 election.

## Event

Blessing v. Freestone, No. 95-1441. Argued January 6, 1997; decided April 21, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, which reversed summary judgment for Arizona on October 10, 1995 and recognized a broad individual right to substantial compliance with Title IV-D. Five mothers alleged failures of paternity establishment, parent location, support orders and collections caused by staffing shortages, backlogs and structural defects; Madrid separately alleged failure to distribute collected support. Review concerns identification of individual rights enforceable through §1983 and whether Congress displaced that channel through federal oversight. The request for programwide relief is not a finding of any violation.

His actual Livadas opinion supplies this case's central authoring task?separating an individual statutory right from ?1983, congressional foreclosure and the lawful remedy?and his Suter position distinguishes a claimed individual protection from useful administrative machinery. That combination is a concrete advantage for rejecting generalized staffing/computer claims without converting their failure into chapterwide foreclosure or prematurely recognizing Madrid's entitlement. It is not an automatic entitlement arising from writing Livadas. Stone, at the same count, is the strongest alternative; his approved position makes the same distinctions and could carry the opinion, but adds no necessary accommodation or unique coordination advantage over Stevens's demonstrated treatment of the exact enforcement structure. Thomas, at five, also qualifies fully and can complete the entire opinion; the lower count keeps him in the pool but does not displace the particular fit advantage for this lead. The separate concurrence is assigned to Scalia, joined by Kennedy. This does not count as another lead opinion. Its actual legal work is to state the original-meaning distinction between a right secured by law and third-party enforcement of a governmental funding contract while expressly applying existing precedents in the present case. Scalia's reconciled formulation directly articulates that additional distinction; Kennedy's full concurrence join supplies the same reservation. Selection preserves the substantive two-Justice position, not a historical author entitlement. No additional Justice is assigned that reservation, and it changes neither eligibility for the common lead nor the judgment.

**Counts before assignment:** Stone-Zsela 6; Stevens 6; O'Connor 9; Scalia 6; Kennedy 6; Souter 8; Thomas 5; Ginsburg 6; Breyer 6. **Last counted event:** Chandler v. Miller, 1997-04-15. The fresh recount reads actual current-term Record opinion tables, including earlier same-day assignments for counting only. Receipt: `freeze/OT_1996CHUNK6_70_ASSIGNMENT.json`; bounded source extract: `runtime/assembly/OT_1996CHUNK6_70_ASSIGNMENT.md`.
**Controlling explanation:** The substantial-compliance measure evaluates aggregate performance for federal oversight and funding consequences. A State may meet that benchmark while failing one family; aggregate success cannot extinguish an independently existing personal right. Section 654(3) directs a single, separate organizational unit meeting prescribed staffing requirements, but identifies no beneficiary's right to a particular staff complement, caseload or assigned worker. Former and amended §654(16), their associated requirements and §654a's management and performance functions similarly organize program administration. Their technical detail does not make the system itself an individual entitlement. Mandatory duties important to effective collections therefore do not sustain the general claim recognized below. Alleged shortages, backlogs and accounting defects may still bear on a proved violation or fitted remedy, but create no right by themselves. No separately pleaded claim to each standardized computer requirement, confidentiality protection or individual transmission deadline is established here. The Court adjudicates no individual clause of 45 C.F.R. §§303.20 or 307.10 and announces no categorical rule against regulatory elaboration of an established statutory right. A genuinely distinct claim must receive its own provision-specific inquiry, rather than automatic approval or rejection because it appears in an administrative section.
**Stage:** Fresh, narrowly scoped non-Stone modeling clarification. This supplemental handoff preserves `OT_1996CHUNK6_70_COMMITMENTS.md` without rewriting it. It supplies no opinion assignment, final authorship, Stone position, historical reconciliation or completed adjudication.
**Event / research cutoff:** April 21, 1997; No. 95-1441.
**Authorized inputs:** Engine §§1, 3, 5–7 and 14; the original frozen matter-70 commitments; matter-70 neutral packet and entering-law slice; the Blessing portion of the Neutral B supplement; `runtime/OT_1996CHUNK6_BLESSING_FRESH_NEUTRAL_FACTS.md`; official historical §§654 (1994 and 1996), 657 (1994), 1320a-2 and 1320a-10 (1996); and the subsequently supplied official 1996 §654a text. No current-matter Supreme Court opinion, comparator, reconciled handoff, private Stone material, raw Decision Record or exposure receipt was opened. No Git command or external research was used.
**Agency organization and staffing — §654(3).** Both supplied Code versions require a single and separate organizational unit within the State to administer the plan, meeting staffing and organizational requirements prescribed by the Secretary. This directs the establishment and administration of the agency. It does not specify a beneficiary's entitlement to a particular staff complement, caseload, assigned employee or staffing-level remedy. Reading this organizational direction as a general personal right to adequate staffing would repeat B2's substitution of effective program administration for an identified individual right. The lack of an identified personal entitlement is sufficient here; no universal rule that qualitative standards are judicially unenforceable is necessary.

## Event

**Case:** Kadic v. Karadzic; Doe v. Karadzic. Consolidated review of Second Circuit Nos. 94-9035 and 94-9069; 70 F.3d 232 and 74 F.3d 377.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, April 21, 1997; October Term 1996, chunk 7, inventory 73.
**Route and chronology:** On writs of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Second Circuit; granted October 7, 1996 and submitted on the merits. The judgments below reversed the district court's blanket jurisdictional dismissal and remanded.
**Questions:** Whether §1350 reaches the alleged private or nonstate genocide and war crimes; which asserted norms require official action; and whether political-question or related foreign-affairs grounds require dismissal. The included domestic-source question is distinct from jurisdiction. Ultimate liability and amount of relief are not presented.
**Posture:** Karadzic seeks restoration of dismissal. The two plaintiff groups seek continuation on allegations of killing, rape, forced prostitution and impregnation, torture, and a personally planned or directed campaign against protected ethnic or religious groups. Those allegations are not findings. The consolidated review does not merge their distinct service circumstances.

Disposition only on Part VII's particular service application and Part VIII's supplemental-claim component. |
| Same opinion | Ginsburg | Scalia, Thomas | Concurrence in the common threshold and surviving-action remand; disagreement with new received actions | Parts I and IV–VII; Part VIII's ordinary proof, relief and supplemental-claim principles, excluding its authorization of the additional Part II–III actions. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm in part, reverse in part, and remand | Rejects the domestic source of the additional genocide and war-crime actions; preserves the common rulings and existing official-torture and enacted TVPA routes. |

Parts II–III have seven direct joins; no fractured-opinion synthesis is required. Agreement with the service and supplemental-claim disposition does not supply Stone-Zsela's join to those additional grounds.

## Metropolitan_Life_v_Robertson_Ceco_merits_1997-04-21.md

**Case and dockets:** Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Robertson-Ceco Corp.; Second Circuit No. 95-7370; companion petition identifiers 96-306 and 96-472.

## Event

**Case:** Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Robertson-Ceco Corp.; Second Circuit No. 95-7370; companion petition identifiers 96-306 and 96-472 concerning one underlying action.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, April 21, 1997; October Term 1996, chunk 7, inventory 72.
**Route and posture:** On writ of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Second Circuit, 84 F.3d 560; submitted for merits decision. The court below affirmed a Rule 12(b)(2) dismissal for constitutional unreasonableness despite finding general contacts sufficient. Met Life seeks restoration of its Vermont action; Robertson also defends dismissal on insufficient contacts.
**Questions:** Whether constitutional reasonableness remains a separate inquiry after adequate general contacts, how the absence of a connection between the forum and dispute affects that inquiry, and the period and nature of contacts establishing general jurisdiction.
**Record:** The action filed August 31, 1993 concerns alleged defective curtain-wall design, manufacture, and installation at a Miami building, with conduct in Missouri, Texas, and Florida and no claim-generating Vermont activity. Met Life is incorporated and headquartered in New York; Robertson is incorporated in Delaware and headquartered in Pennsylvania. Extensive jurisdictional discovery preceded a prima-facie determination without an evidentiary hearing. United Dominion's March 14, 1995 separate summary judgment completed the underlying disposition; it is outside this appeal.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter | Reversal and remand | Parts I–IV in full |
| Same opinion | Thomas | Scalia | Reversal and remand | Parts I, II, III-B and IV; reserves Part III-A's invariably required separate inquiry |
| Same opinion | Thomas | Stone-Zsela | Agrees that constitutional dismissal must be removed and case remanded; would vacate rather than reverse | Part I's reasonable-period rule, Parts II–III in full, Part IV's proof/defense/remand limits; judgment only on the discovery order; does not join formal reversal |
| Same opinion | Thomas | Ginsburg, Breyer | Agree with preserved discovery order; would affirm dismissal | Part I's reasonable-period and discovery-limit propositions only |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Reserves the mandatory separate inquiry while agreeing that this objection fails even assuming its applicability |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would vacate and remand | Adopts the sufficient-contacts and fairness grounds; preserves the prima-facie stage and seeks vacatur rather than formal reversal |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Breyer | Would affirm on the alternative insufficient-contacts ground | Agrees with Part I; rejects Part II's sufficiency conclusion and does not reach the separate fairness issue |

The reasonable-period proposition has nine direct supporters; the separate discovery-limit rationale has eight, with Stone-Zsela agreeing in disposition only. The contacts and record-specific fairness holdings have seven. The independent-fairness requirement has six. The formal reversal has six. No vote for one proposition or remedial form supplies a join to another.

## Form, proposition coalitions and final compatibility before assignment

Before the count or author selection, the case's legal form was fixed as one signed plenary opinion resolving the reasonable-period and discovery issue, the aggregate contacts, independent fairness framework, record-specific fairness failure, and formal remand disposition. These are distinct legal questions and grounds, not parts created to change counts. One author can carry the whole opinion. Partial joins preserve the existing disagreements rather than divide authorship: Scalia reserves III-A; Stone supports vacatur rather than reversal and adds no discovery rationale; Ginsburg and Breyer agree with I but reject II and reserve fairness as unnecessary. Separate writings explain those real disagreements. No historical Supreme Court author exists for this granted merits event.

The final compatibility test changes no reconciled Associate commitment. Stevens and O'Connor retain the meaningful independent fairness inquiry they supported in Asahi, without limiting it to foreign defendants. Kennedy, Souter and Thomas retain both the independent inquiry and its bounded application, without treating Quill as an all-purpose-jurisdiction holding. Scalia joins the same record-specific failure even assuming that inquiry applies and is not counted in III-A. Ginsburg and Breyer retain their alternative insufficient-contacts affirmance, without joining a fairness-only affirmance or a new headquarters-only rule. All eight retain the approved discovery limit and prima-facie procedural limits. The six formal reversals are preserved exactly; Stone's vacatur is neither converted to reversal nor counted as an affirmance. Seven support the contacts and insufficient-fairness propositions; six directly support the independent-inquiry rule; no Marks aggregation is used. No post-reconciliation changed premise or invented circulation history requires a new audit.

## Assignment statement

The opinion announces the six-Justice **reversal** and remand, and separately preserves the challenged discovery limit. Stone's requested vacatur is a different formal disposition, so he is outside the reversal coalition. **Stevens, the senior participating Associate in that coalition, assigns the reversal component; the Chief, within the unanimous discovery-disposition coalition, assigns that portion.** The frozen commitments decide the reasonable discovery limit as a component; it is not merely an unreviewed procedural fact. Stone's fallback supplies assignment authority from his disposition but no authorship eligibility on its rationale. The opinion's coordinated form and propositions were fixed before counting. Because the Chief assigns part of this one lead, the balanced-assignment rule governs the coordinated opinion. Shared agreement with removing dismissal does not authorize attributing reversal to the Chief, and no component is removed or opinion split to avoid the rule.

## Event

New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A. Argued December 3, 1996; decided April 21, 1997. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, 69 F.3d 1081, No. 94-2160. The September 23, 1983 foster-care decree permits termination after twelve consecutive months of substantial and continuous compliance. The District Court terminated it on June 30, 1994, adopting reports from June 1989 and April 30, 1993; the Tenth Circuit vacated and remanded on November 9, 1995 and denied rehearing December 14, 1995. Review concerns the decree's release standard, the adequacy of Rule 52 findings and methodology, and the distinction between performance-based termination and Rufo modification. The separate 1994 monitoring-fee judgment is outside the grant.

Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, No. 95-1100. Reargument order, April 28, 1997. Argued November 5, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, 67 F.3d 1174.

## Event

Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno; review of Third Circuit No. 95-7137, 76 F.3d 1294 (February 13, 1996). Argued December 4, 1996; decided April 28, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1). Pic-A-State seeks declaratory and prospective injunctive relief against the Interstate Wagering Amendment to 18 U.S.C. §1301. The questions are whether the stopped procurement business presents a concrete pre-enforcement controversy, whether Congress may regulate its interstate procurement transmissions although the tickets remain in another State, and what effect the express interstate-agreement exception has on the dormant Commerce Clause objection.

That court affirmed the conviction after denial of suppression and no-contest pleas. The questions are whether the Fourth Amendment permits its categorical announcement exception for warrants seeking evidence of felonious drug delivery or possession with intent to deliver, and whether the actual entry is sustainable on a narrower ground. Its interwoven federal and state reasoning supplies no independently sufficient state ground barring review; genuinely independent state protection remains distinct.

Strate v. A-1 Contractors, No. 95-1872. Argued January 7, 1997; decided April 28, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eighth Circuit, 76 F.3d 930 (1996) (en banc). The federal §1331 action challenges the outer federal limit on the Tribal Court of the Three Affiliated Tribes' subject-matter jurisdiction over Gisela Fredericks's personal-injury claim against A-1 Contractors and Lyle Stockert, arising from a November 9, 1990 collision on State Highway 8 within the Fort Berthold Reservation. The en banc court reversed the District Court's judgment sustaining jurisdiction; the earlier panel judgment was vacated. Tribal exhaustion is complete. Personal jurisdiction, the adult sons' unresolved consortium claims and the dismissed insurer are outside this appeal.

## Timmons_v_Twin_Cities_Area_New_Party_merits_1997-04-28.md

**Case and dockets:** Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party; No. 95-1608.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-04-28; October Term 1996.
**Result:** Reversed and remanded, 5–4; old restriction sustained as applied, subject to the enacted transition.
**Version / lineage:** Original Canonical Decision Record; supersedes no adjudication. Written during this Run; no Git command or Git commitment is represented.

## Event

Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party, No. 95-1608. Argued December 4, 1996; decided April 28, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eighth Circuit, Twin Cities Area New Party v. McKenna, 73 F.3d 196 (January 5, 1996). The court below reversed summary judgment for state officials and held Minnesota's old prohibition unconstitutional as applied to the New Party's nomination of Andy Dawkins, a consenting, supported candidate already seeking the Democratic-Farmer-Labor nomination. The DFL did not object. The grant concerns that minor/major-party combination under the First and Fourteenth Amendments, not the separate two-major-party prohibition. Minnesota Laws 1996, chapter 419, conditionally changed the statute and expressly makes reversal consequential.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Breyer; Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg in I and III | Reversal and remand | Five join merits Part II; all eight Associates join live-controversy Part I and statutory-transition Part III; Stone-Zsela agrees to reach the merits without an additional statutory rationale |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg throughout; Souter in I–II | Would affirm the as-applied constitutional judgment | I–II: nomination interest and inadequate fit; III: broader objection to preserving the established parties' competitive position |
| Separate dissent | Souter | No other Justice | Would affirm | Joins Stevens I–II; reserves a demonstrated stability justification connected to effective governing |
| Dissent | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would affirm on contextual, nonsevere-burden review | Substantial but not severe burden; inadequate fit; prospective and format/consent limits |

## Form and final joins before assignment

One signed plenary opinion is fixed: I, live controversy after the conditional enactment; II, contextual merits sustaining the application; III, the exact conditional statutory consequence and remand. Eight Associates join I and III. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer join II and reverse. Stone agrees the merits are reached but supplies no chapter-specific ground. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Stone would affirm the as-applied constitutional judgment. Stevens's dissent states the genuine nomination interest and inadequate fit in I–II; Ginsburg joins throughout, Souter joins I–II but not the broader stability discussion in III and writes separately. Stone's separate dissent states his precise nonsevere burden and inadequate-fit framework. No automatic strict scrutiny, actual-confusion finding or categorical validity proposition enters the majority.

Every Associate vote and join boundary is retained from the freeze, including Souter's reservation of a demonstrated stability justification tied to effective governing. No circulation change or new premise is inferred. The five merits joins directly control; the eight threshold and statutory-transition joins directly control those components. No Marks synthesis or aggregation of dissenters into a merits majority is used.

## Assignment statement

The Chief assigns the live-controversy component from the coalition reaching the merits. O'Connor, senior participating Associate in the five-Justice reversal coalition, assigns the merits; the Chief supplies no reversal vote. The coordinated opinion is consequently governed by §8 balance. Fresh actual-topology counts: Stone-Zsela 6; Stevens 7; O'Connor 9; Scalia 7; Kennedy 7; Souter 8; Thomas 7; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 7. Last counted event: Strate v. A-1 Contractors, April 28, 1997; 65 preceding counted leads. Receipt: `freeze/OT_1996CHUNK7_78_ASSIGNMENT_RECOUNT.json`.

Whole-opinion eligible authors: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer. The eligible minimum is seven; pool: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer, each seven. **The Chief and O'Connor assign their respective portions to Thomas for a single coordinated opinion.** Fit governs. Thomas's actual Burdick and Colorado Republican joins, applied in his frozen present ground, distinguish the official selection instrument from the party's own protected campaign expression without erasing genuine association or immunizing all election rules. Kennedy is the strongest alternative because his own Burdick dissent protects actual candidate choice while rejecting an arbitrary-message entitlement, and his present position explains why this available candidate differs. Thomas can carry the settled common ground without making that distinction turn on an asserted earlier personal fusion vote or an actual-confusion finding. Counts are equal; the precise complete-system/ballot-designation account supplies fit. O'Connor's relevant Colorado Republican authorship does not overcome her exclusion from the pool. Stone opposes the controlling merits and is not eligible for the whole. No division is needed. One signed lead credit goes to Thomas.

| Justice | Judgment and sufficient ground | Own writing or join; strongest alternative and join boundary |
|---|---|---|
| Stevens | **Affirm the old-law as-applied invalidation; remand only for prospective relief consistent with the statutory development.** Adopt Path B. The State has prevented the party from nominating its genuinely preferred consenting candidate without showing an adequate connection between this total prohibition and the dangers invoked. The choice is central association; the case can be decided without declaring every nominal limitation severe. | Joined Kennedy's Burdick dissent, which accepted calibration but refused to treat theoretical access as an answer to the actual electoral choice; joined Norman's protected authorized association. His Colorado Republican dissent treats an official party as a distinctive nominating institution, a premise supporting the importance of nomination here while allowing contribution-channel regulation there. He accepts Path A's preventive-regulation premise but rejects using it to insulate a categorical ban unrelated to supported consent and actual ballot access. Will join an adequate-fit as-applied rule; will not join mere endorsement as an equivalent substitute, a categorical two-party-entrenchment interest, or a constitutional right to separate party vote totals. |
| O'Connor | **Reverse; remand for disposition consistent with sustaining the old prohibition in this application and chapter 419's express consequence.** Adopt Path A. Remaining candidate and party access make the burden reasonable, and preserving ballot integrity, distinct nomination choices and stable election machinery supplies sufficient regulatory justification. | Joined Burdick's complete-access holding and Norman; authored Colorado Republican Part I and its narrower Parts II–III. Those positions distinguish meaningful access from an entitlement to a preferred institutional mechanism and distinguish genuinely independent speech from regulated electoral channels. Path B correctly identifies a real burden, but consent alone does not require the State to alter its nomination structure for this alliance. Will not join a presumption that every anti-fusion law is valid or that political competition itself is an evil. No automatic strict scrutiny from Colorado Republican. |
| Scalia | **Reverse on Path A; same bounded remand.** The official ballot may require each eligible candidate to enter through a defined nomination channel; the Constitution protects advocacy without requiring recognition of every party affiliation on that ballot. | Joined Burdick's Court opinion, including complete-access and arbitrary-message holdings; joined Kennedy's Colorado Republican concurrence protecting the party's own campaign expression even when coordinated. The latter protects a speaker's message and does not erase the distinction between campaign speech and the State's candidate-selection instrument. Path B's nomination interest is genuine, but it cannot carry an unlimited constitutional right to party labels. He can join the contextual lesser-burden holding and ordinary preventive interests, not an assertion that all consensual party cooperation may be suppressed or that the expenditure concurrence has been abandoned. |
| Kennedy | **Reverse on Path A; same bounded remand.** This party and its supporters may support and elect the actual candidate; the restriction on placing an additional nominating identity on the official ballot does not have the severity of excluding an actual electoral option. Reasonable nomination-system regulation supplies sufficient grounds here. | Authored Burdick's dissent, which protected the ability to choose actual candidates but rejected an independent tally for arbitrary messages; authored Colorado Republican's party-expression concurrence; joined Norman. The strongest contrary analogy is Norman's refusal to erase authorized party identity. It is not dispositive: Norman excluded otherwise qualified candidacies for unrelated geography and another component's failure; this rule organizes overlapping nominations of the same available candidate. He will not equate this real nomination with a protest message, deny the party's independent expressive identity, or approve excluding an actual alternative candidate merely to preserve the two major parties. His lesser-burden conclusion concerns this complete system and the available candidate. |
| Souter | **Affirm on Path B; prospective-remedy treatment as Stevens.** The asserted general regulatory interests do not justify preventing this supported, consensual party nomination. A conclusion that the burden is less than severe does not end Anderson's fit inquiry. | Authored Norman and joined Burdick's Court opinion; joined Colorado Republican Part I and O'Connor's narrower Parts II–III. Those anchors support regulation with an actual connection to its object, not a universal deregulatory rule. His Burdick join preserved primary finality and protection against evasive candidacies within adequate access. Here the willing nominee is the primary winner, not a defeated candidate seeking reentry; consent undermines the party-conflict justification. Will join a confined inadequate-fit holding without a categorical severe-burden rule. Refuses the proposition that stability permits insulation from minor-party influence as such. He reserves whether a demonstrated threat to effective governing, connected to fusion, could justify a restriction; he does not join a categorical rejection of every stability justification. |
| Thomas | **Reverse on Path A; same bounded remand.** The complete nomination system imposes a permissible limitation on ballot designation while leaving protected party advocacy and meaningful electoral routes. | Joined Burdick's Court opinion and Kennedy's Colorado Republican concurrence. Those joins supply different treatment of official selection machinery and the party's own campaign expression. The best contrary claim is that nomination communicates association in a constitutionally significant way; he accepts that fact without concluding the State must represent every alliance on its ballot. Will join the reasonable-burden and general administrative interests rationale, not a rule forbidding political coalition speech or an unqualified incumbent-protection interest. His position adopts no universal validity rule for stricter access systems. |
| Ginsburg | **Affirm on Path B; prospective-remedy treatment as Stevens.** The State's legitimate administrative interests do not adequately justify the exclusion of this party's actual willing nominee when qualification and consent are present. | Joined Stevens's Colorado Republican dissent, which emphasizes the distinctive continuing relationship of the official nominating party and its candidates. Applied here, that institutional understanding makes nomination materially more than outside endorsement. This is an inference from her supported writing, not an invented earlier fusion vote. Burdick remains law; she accepts preventive rules without proof of past misconduct but requires a connection between this prohibition and the asserted risk. Will join Souter's less-than-severe-but-insufficient-fit formulation; does not require an electoral subsidy, duplicate votes or a particular party-status counting system. |
| Breyer | **Reverse on Path A; same bounded remand.** The Constitution permits this reasonable limitation as part of an administrable nomination system, notwithstanding the party's real preference for cross-nomination. Ballot integrity and stable party identities may be protected prospectively; this holding does not rest on voter confusion. | Joined Colorado Republican Part I and O'Connor's narrower Parts II–III, preserving contextual examination and realistic preventive regulation while rejecting party status as a substitute for an actually required connection. Burdick's complete-system rule supplies controlling law; no nonexistent personal vote in that case is attributed to him. Path B correctly insists on precise interests, but the actual remaining access and system-wide effects make a simple nomination rule proportionate. Will not join pure rational-basis immunity for ballot rules, automatic deference to unsupported claims of past fraud, or an interest in suppressing competition merely to protect incumbents. |

## Doe_v_SEPTA_merits_1997-05-05.md

**Case and dockets:** Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority; Third Circuit No. 95-1559; no separate Supreme Court merits docket supplied.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-05-05; October Term 1996.
**Result:** Affirmed, 5–4, on an expressly assumed-right and confined no-violation ground.
**Version / lineage:** Original Canonical Decision Record; supersedes no adjudication. Written during this Run; no Git command or Git commitment is represented.

## Event

Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority; review of Third Circuit No. 95-1559, 72 F.3d 1133 (December 28, 1995). Argued January 13, 1997; decided May 5, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Third Circuit under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1). That court reversed Doe's $125,000 emotional-distress judgment and directed judgment for SEPTA and Judith Pierce under Rule 50. The three granted questions concern constitutional confidentiality of prescription information, governmental benefits administration using identified information, and limited internal disclosure. Distinct personal-immunity, municipal-attribution, damages-sufficiency and Rule 59 questions are not granted. The parties' relevant policymaker agreement is preserved, and the adverse failure-to-train judgment was not appealed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Affirmance | Entire expressly assumed-right, record-specific no-violation holding |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Affirmance | Joins Court opinion in full; additionally rejects an adequate constitutional source for the proposed internal-use extension |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Would vacate and remand | Limited deliberate-medical-inquiry extension, with damages and genuinely remaining trial issues preserved |
| Dissent | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would reverse the Rule 50 direction and remand | Proportionate-connection extension; jury evidence sufficient; denial of judgment as a matter of law retained; preserved Rule 59/damages issues remain |

## Form and proposition coalitions before assignment

One signed plenary Opinion of the Court is fixed before counting, deciding the particular no-violation application on an express assumption of the asserted interest. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer join that sufficient rationale in full. Scalia and Thomas additionally deny a demonstrated constitutional source for the proposed extension; a separate concurrence performs that distinct work without turning an assumption into recognition. Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg join one dissent extending protection to the bounded deliberate inquiry and seeking vacatur/remand. Stone's separate dissent preserves his different legal sufficiency and remedial position.

No separate disputed threshold component authorizes another assignment: all reach the granted questions, and favorable-evidence review is a common standard within the merits rather than a separate judgment. Five directly support affirmance on the same assumed-right application, so no Marks construction, manufactured plurality or aggregation with the source-objection concurrence is needed. Every Associate commitment remains unchanged; the deliberate follow-up, unnecessary identifiers, limited audience and absent general publication are all accepted at their actual evidentiary status. No circulated revision or new premise calls for another reconciliation stage.

## Assignment statement

O'Connor is the senior participating Associate in the five-Justice affirmance coalition and assigns the sole Court opinion. The Chief opposes that judgment and assigns no portion. Fresh actual-topology counts: Stone-Zsela 6; Stevens 7; O'Connor 9; Scalia 7; Kennedy 7; Souter 8; Thomas 8; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 7. Last counted event: Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party, April 28, 1997; 66 preceding counted signed plenary leads. Receipt: `freeze/OT_1996CHUNK7_79_ASSIGNMENT_RECOUNT.json`.

Eligible whole-opinion authors are O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer. Section 8's Chief-assignment pool does not bind this Associate assignment; for transparent comparison, applying the minimum-plus-one criterion would yield Scalia (7), Kennedy (7), Thomas (8) and Breyer (7). **O'Connor assigns to Kennedy.** Her own Hodge reserved-merits reasoning and Sheets join support distinguishing scope from application without constitutionalizing every confidentiality expectation. Kennedy's frozen ground carries that objective: he accepts sensitivity and deliberate use, rejects a benefits waiver, and separates the confined administrator/physician inquiry from outward or unrelated use while preserving Sheets. Breyer is a strong equally counted alternative, with his Uhlrig distinction among favorable facts, attribution and immunity; Kennedy's exact substantive-use distinction fits the sole merits ground without making the absent immunity issue central. Neither Scalia nor Thomas must abandon their source objection to join the express assumption, and their separate writing preserves it. No historical Supreme Court merits authorship exists to import. The opinion receives one signed lead credit to Kennedy.

| Justice | Reach, judgment and sufficient ground | Own-writing anchors; alternative, objection and join scope |
|---|---|---|
| Stevens | **Vacate and remand on Path B.** The favorable record permits deliberate pursuit of named medical conditions beyond a supported benefits need; the appellate court's generalized cost interest does not justify directing judgment against Doe. No automatic damages reinstatement. | Authored Whalen, especially its distinction between rational safeguarded collection and the express reservation at 605–606; authored Sheets Parts I–II, particularly entrusted purpose, objective intimacy and favorable-evidence review. Whalen's rejection of proven indispensability is the strongest obstacle. He would resolve it by requiring adequate connection, not necessity of every datum: the conscious HIV-identification inquiry and conceded lack of need are evidence, not an automatic least-means test. Will join this medical-use extension and bounded remand; will not join a universal seven-factor test, an assumption that two doctors newly learned the condition, or a rule that emotional distress alone proves constitutional invasion. |
| O'Connor | **Affirm on Path A**, assuming the medical-confidentiality interest and deciding the limited application. | Joined Sheets's expressly confined protection and favorable-review holding; authored Hodge's reserved-merits immunity opinion. Those positions protect genuine intimacy without converting a confidentiality expectation into a universal substantive guarantee. Path B's deliberate-use argument is substantial, but the particular audience, administrative setting and cessation show a limited benefits-review intrusion rather than the unjustified outward dissemination Sheets decided. Will join an assumption-based no-violation holding acknowledging unnecessary identifiers and deliberate review; refuses a categorical rightless zone for government employers, a fabricated immunity ruling or a factual finding that every use was necessary. |
| Scalia | **Affirm on Path C**; also accepts Path A as an independently sufficient assumed-right application if separately stated with its precise facts. | Authored Sheets dissent joined by Thomas: Whalen's reservation and decisional-autonomy decisions did not establish a general informational guarantee; accepted intimate injury and supported facts without an economic-loss rule. Sheets now controls public dissemination, but extending it to administrative access requires an additional constitutional foundation. Path B's analogy is insufficient for him because the source objection is strongest beyond the precedent's actual boundary. Will join Path A expressly on an assumption, but will not join recognition of a general medical-information right or suggest Sheets has been overruled. Prospective separate function: explain why he would reject the new extension rather than resolve its existence. |
| Kennedy | **Affirm on Path A**, assuming the interest and preserving materially different uses. | Joined Sheets Parts I–II and Hodge. His supported prior position protects objective intimate information against insufficiently justified public dissemination while leaving legitimate retention, investigation and distinct government functions intact. Path B correctly rejects waiver from accepting benefits; he does not rely on waiver. But the limited use within the responsible administrative and medical group, followed by cessation, does not establish the constitutional deprivation posited here even with unnecessary identifiers. Will join a rule that evaluates actual use and context; refuses a general permission for diagnostic lists, public disclosure or unrelated employer decisions. |
| Souter | **Vacate and remand on Path B**; do not restore damages automatically. | Joined Sheets's entrusted-purpose, objective-intimacy and favorable-evidence propositions and Hodge's reservation of broader merits. The source of protection cannot turn exclusively on the number of recipients when the government deliberately moves from a financial report to identifying an intimate condition. Path A's ordinary-administration analogy is strong, but must confront the favorable inference about the particular HIV inquiry instead of treating an asserted broad purpose as a conclusive fact. Will join an adequate-connection rule confined to this deliberate medical inquiry, not mandatory encoding for every program or a conclusion that each Westinghouse factor is constitutional law. |
| Thomas | **Affirm on Path C**; accepts Path A's separate assumed-right application. | Joined Scalia's Sheets dissent and Hodge. The supplied prior position demands a demonstrated substantive guarantee while accepting supported intentional conduct and actual intimate injury. Path B extends a bounded precedent rather than merely applying it, and Whalen left the extension unresolved. He will join the assumed-right application without treating it as recognition; will not join categorical economic-loss, public-employment waiver or state-tort-cure theories. A separate source explanation may accompany a full join in an expressly assumed-right application. |
| Ginsburg | **Vacate and remand on Path B**; preserve the appellate damages question and any genuinely remaining trial issue. | Joined both Sheets holdings, including the distinction between independent constitutional judgment and forbidden adverse resolution of supported trial evidence, and joined Hodge. The assertion that this was simply limited cost review cannot overcome the evidence concerning identified HIV inquiry and lack of need at Rule 50. Path A rightly rejects converting every poor practice into a constitutional tort; her confined extension concerns purposeful intimate identification without an adequate program connection. Will join that rule and remand, with no invented wider dissemination, universal strict scrutiny or conclusion that Doe consented by disclosure to trusted coworkers. |
| Breyer | **Affirm on Path A**, assuming the interest. | Joined Sheets and Hodge; authored Uhlrig's careful separation of favorable facts, legal attribution and distinct immunity questions. He accepts the evidence of deliberate study and unnecessary identifiers without treating it as automatic constitutional unlawfulness. The administration of a self-insured plan requires medically informative review; the restricted audience, inquiry into the usefulness of the report, and termination of named reporting make the intrusion insufficient on this record. Path B's concern about diagnosis would prevail on a materially different use detached from benefits administration; this record does not warrant a new universal necessity rule. Will not join an immunity shortcut, imported Fourth Amendment special-needs test or a conclusive finding of no distress. |

## Event

Harbor Tug & Barge Co. v. Papai, No. 95-1621. Argued January 13, 1997; decided May 12, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, September 25, 1995, 67 F.3d 203. The questions concern Jones Act seaman status and whether a final LHWCA award forecloses the seaman claims. Papai injured his knee during a one-day dockside painting assignment aboard Pt. Barrow; the parties dispute the legal significance of his staffing role and prior work. The appeal below also challenged the later vessel-negligence trial judgment, which the Ninth Circuit did not reach.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas throughout; Stone-Zsela in Parts I–II; Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer in Part III | Parts I–II support reversal of status reopening; Part III returns the unresolved vessel appeal | Parts I–II: fleet and status; Part III: independent appeal. Six joins to Parts I–II, eight to Part III. |
| Dissent in part | Stevens | Ginsburg and Breyer | Opposes reversal of status reopening; joins Part III | Triable crew relationship, broader shared-hall alternative and rejection of both award-foreclosure theories for this pending claim. |
