## Opinion form and assignment

Form was determined before counts: one signed lead opinion after plenary review, Parts I–II for fleet and status and Part III for the distinct unresolved vessel appeal; one substantive three-Justice dissent. These divisions preserve different actual components and join scope, and do not manipulate author eligibility. Parts I–II have a six-Justice judgment coalition including the Chief; Part III has all nine in disposition. The Chief assigns the single coordinated opinion. The five Associates who join all Parts can carry the entire opinion; no divided authorship is needed.

Fresh recount: [80 assignment receipt](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK7_80_ASSIGNMENT_RECOUNT.json), drawn from actual opinion tables, including all seven earlier new Court Records. Counts before assignment: Stone-Zsela 6; Stevens 7; O'Connor 9; Scalia 7; Kennedy 8; Souter 8; Thomas 8; Ginsburg 7; Breyer 7. Last counted event: Doe v. SEPTA, May 5, 1997, Kennedy's lead opinion. Eligible whole-opinion authors: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. Stone's judgment-only agreement on Part III excludes him; the dissenters do not join Parts I–II. Minimum eligible count is 7; the pool is Scalia (7), Kennedy (8), Souter (8), Thomas (8).

**Assignment:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns the entire opinion to Souter. Fit governs, with no unresolved formulation barrier requiring expansion. Souter's full Chandris position addresses actual assignment, nature and the factfinding boundary, while his Astoria framework supports explaining why the real final award remains a distinct unnecessary alternative. Scalia is the strongest pool alternative on the common-control boundary; Souter is selected for the joined opinion's combined record/application and reserved-adjudication work. Authorship does not change a vote or depend on the historical target author. Stevens writes the dissent to express the shared actual-crew, labor-market and statutory-accommodation grounds, preserving Ginsburg's different earlier Chandris join. Separate writings do not enter the count.

**Assignment:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns the entire opinion to Souter. Fit governs, with no unresolved formulation barrier requiring expansion. Souter's full Chandris position addresses actual assignment, nature and the factfinding boundary, while his Astoria framework supports explaining why the real final award remains a distinct unnecessary alternative. Scalia is the strongest pool alternative on the common-control boundary; Souter is selected for the joined opinion's combined record/application and reserved-adjudication work. Authorship does not change a vote or depend on the historical target author. Stevens writes the dissent to express the shared actual-crew, labor-market and statutory-accommodation grounds, preserving Ginsburg's different earlier Chandris join. Separate writings do not enter the count.
**Holding and operative rule:** A worker may aggregate service on an identifiable fleet for Jones Act seaman status only when the vessels share ownership or operational control. Obtaining assignments through one union hiring hall does not itself supply that connection, although a hiring-hall worker may establish the required substantial connection to an actual vessel or commonly owned or controlled fleet.
**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a common source of labor does not turn independently directed vessels into one fleet. Chandris requires both contribution to the vessel's work and a connection substantial in nature and duration to a vessel or identifiable fleet. Its actual-assignment inquiry protects an employment relationship, rather than every maritime task a worker performs. Wilander rejects a requirement of traditional navigation duties, but does not eliminate the required vessel connection.
**Controlling explanation:** Chandris directs attention to the actual assignment and preserves factfinding when the properly considered evidence permits competing reasonable conclusions. Here Papai accepted one day of dockside chipping and painting, with no planned sailing or commitment to return. His three or four specifically recalled earlier Pt. Barrow jobs and the immediately preceding Sea Fox job were maintenance. The twelve earlier Harbor Tug engagements do not establish a different continuing sea-service obligation; the Court does not find that every unidentified job was identical.

## Lambrix_v_Singletary_merits_1997-05-12.md

**Case and dockets:** Lambrix v. Singletary; No. 96-5658; OT1996, chunk 7, matter 83.

**Result:** Appellate disposition of the Espinosa claim vacated and remanded; six support setting aside and remanding, with five adopting the binding old-rule instruction and Stone-Zsela retaining a distinct default-first mandate. Three would affirm under Teague.
**Version / lineage:** Initial Canonical Decision Record; supersedes no completed adjudication. Final reconciliation and the subsequent limited mandate-compatibility audit together control; the original freezes remain unchanged. No Git operation or commitment claim is made.

## Event

Lambrix v. Singletary, No. 96-5658. Argued January 15, 1997; decided May 12, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit's January 3, 1996 panel judgment, 72 F.3d 1500. The panel followed en banc Glock; this case was not itself decided en banc. The questions concern Florida's asserted procedural bar and whether the Espinosa claim seeks a new rule under Teague at November 24, 1986 finality. The claim addresses heinous, atrocious or cruel and cold, calculated and premeditated aggravator instructions in the jury's death recommendations; the Court decides no new challenge to the guilt judgments.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court in its respective Parts | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer throughout; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas in I; O'Connor in II and III; Stone-Zsela in III-A | I decides to reach Teague; II rejects the new-rule bar; III-A orders common vacatur/default remand; III-B adds the binding old-rule instruction | Seven direct joins to I; five to II and III-B; six to III-A; no five join every Part. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O'Connor | No other Justice | Supports II–III once Teague is reached; opposes I's sequence | Primary default-first vacatur, affirmative alternative old-rule determination and corresponding binding remand. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins III-A's vacatur/default-remand rule; rejects present Teague adjudication and its binding remand direction | No present old-rule or new-rule position; would consider Teague only if default permits review. Does not join I, II or III-B. |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia and Thomas | Would affirm denial on Teague; also join Part I of the lead | Reasonable prefinality independent-judge distinction; both contemporary exceptions rejected; actual default and merits/cure reserved. |

The controlling portions rest on direct majority agreement, not a combination of narrower noncontrolling rationales.

## Form and coalitions before assignment

One signed lead after plenary review; no consolidation. The legal work divides into Part I, present decision to reach Teague (seven Associates); Part II, old-rule determination (Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer); Part III-A, common operative vacatur/default remand (those five plus Stone); and Part III-B, additional binding old-rule instruction and its remaining-merits directions (the five old-rule Associates). The III-A/III-B division reflects genuine different operative commands: sending the actual default inquiry back and forbidding a future Teague rejection do different legal work. It is not a bare-disposition fragment created for assignment. O'Connor's affirmative alternative joins coexist with her Part I disagreement. Four Associates accept vacatur only with the binding old-rule instruction under the limited audit. Stone's common remand join must not be turned into assent to that instruction. Three substantive separate writings preserve O'Connor's sequence, Stone's distinct unadjudicated-Teague mandate, and the common new-rule dissent. Form, propositions and these component bases were fixed before the recount or author selection.

The Chief assigns Part III-A because he joins its actual six-Justice vacatur and default-remand coalition. Stevens, the senior Associate, assigns Part I from its seven-Associate coalition and Parts II/III-B from their five-Associate old-rule and binding-direction coalition. Stone does not assign a component whose operative instruction he refuses. These are coordinated assignments of one opinion, not separate lead opinions. Because the Chief assigns one real component, the whole coordinated opinion is subject to §8 balance. One author can fully carry every Part; there is no basis to divide authorship.

**Assignment:** The component assigners select Stevens to deliver the entire coordinated opinion: Stone assigns III-A to Stevens, and Stevens assigns I, II and III-B to himself from the respective coalitions he joins. His substantive Stringer and Espinosa positions directly connect guidance to consequential weighing, while his Gray joins distinguish old law from separate claim and remedial predicates. The actual work is to explain why the prefinality state allocation supplies an application of an existing federal protection, candidly preserve the independent judge's role, and keep that conclusion distinct from actual default and cure. Those date-eligible grounds fit all Parts, including the common bounded remand. Breyer is the strongest pool alternative because his functional Gray/Roy approach also preserves actual effect and relief; Ginsburg's Caspari precision likewise fits. Stevens is selected for the particularly direct conjunction of guidance, allocation and separate-remedy grounds he has already adopted. ## Event

Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board, No. 95-1455, and Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, No. 95-1508. Consolidated direct appeals from the three-judge United States District Court for the District of Columbia. Argued December 9, 1996; decided May 12, 1997. The January 8, 1996 judgment granted preclearance to the Board's twelve-district plan. The questions are whether §5 incorporates §2's results standard, whether dilution evidence bears on discriminatory purpose, and whether prohibited purpose is confined to intended retrogression. The Board adopted the Police Jury plan, containing no majority-Black district, and rejected an alternative containing two. The parties stipulated to nonretrogressive effects; §2 liability and the Board's actual purpose were not adjudicated by this Court.

## Camps_Newfound_Owatonna_v_Town_of_Harrison_merits_1997-05-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison; No. 94-1988.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-05-19; October Term 1996, chunk 8.
**Result:** Reverse the residence-condition validation and remand, 6–3; five Justices control the open state-law remedy, while Stone requires extension of the exemption.
**Version / lineage:** Initial decision; supersedes no completed adjudication. Durable file handoff under the user's no-Git instruction; no Git commit or hash verification is claimed.

## Event

Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, No. 94-1988. On review of the Supreme Judicial Court of Maine, 655 A.2d 876, which on April 21, 1995 reversed summary judgment for the camp and sustained the challenged property tax. Argued October 9, 1996; decided May 19, 1997. The question is whether the nonresident-beneficiary condition in Maine's charitable-property exemption discriminates against interstate commerce and whether a subsidy, market-participant or resident-benefit justification sustains it. The Maine nonprofit's 180-acre Christian Science camp recruits nationally; about 95 percent of campers come from outside Maine. Its reported charge is roughly $400 per week and annual property taxes exceed $20,000. It timely pursued state refund and prospective-exemption procedures.

Section 652(1)(A) generally covers real and personal property owned and occupied or used solely for the purposes of benevolent and charitable institutions incorporated by Maine, without loss of exemption merely because of the source of funds or limitations in beneficiary classes, subject to the express nonresident subparagraph. An institution operated principally for nonresidents may exempt no more than $50,000 of current just property value only if the statutory average weekly charge per person does not exceed $30; above that rate it receives no exemption under that subparagraph. The computation divides all tax-year stipends or charges for services, benefits or advantages by all persons receiving them during that year, then divides by the weeks during that tax year when the institution is actually operated principally for nonresidents; §502 defines the tax year. The reported weekly price is not substituted for that statutory computation.

The nonresident subparagraph does not apply to a nonprofit corporation incorporated solely to conduct medical research; that exception does not waive the general exemption's other requirements and is not found applicable to this camp. The paragraph also includes nonprofit nursing, boarding and boarding-care facilities licensed under Title 22, chapter 1665 or its successor; nonprofit community mental-health facilities licensed under Title 34-B, chapter 3; and nonprofit child-care centers incorporated by Maine as benevolent and charitable institutions. Nonprofit means exempt under Internal Revenue Code §501(c)(3). No independent challenge to Maine incorporation, neutral qualification, religious liberty, equal protection or travel rights is decided.

| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins invalidation; would require extension of the exemption under this scheme | Case-specific exemption remedy, with refund and prospective relief subject to governing law |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas and Ginsburg | Would affirm the Maine judgment | Resident-charitable-assistance comparison and alternative justification; no actual-purchase or successful compensatory-tax finding |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm on an additional constitutional ground | Proposed abandonment of the negative Commerce Clause doctrine and tentative Import-Export Clause alternative; Ginsburg does not join |

## Edmond_v_United_States_merits_1997-05-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Edmond v. United States; No. 96-262, including Lazenby, Leaver, Leonard, Nichols and Venable under Rule 12.4.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-05-19; October Term 1996, chunk 8.
**Result:** Affirm every one of the six military appellate judgments, 9–0; eight join the common classification rationale, Souter concurs on a distinct ground.
**Version / lineage:** Initial decision; supersedes no completed adjudication. Durable file handoff under the user's no-Git instruction; no Git commit or hash verification is claimed.

## Event

Edmond v. United States, No. 96-262, including under Rule 12.4 the petitions of Lazenby, Leaver, Leonard, Nichols and Venable. Argued February 24, 1997; decided May 19, 1997. The Court reviews the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces judgments reported at 45 M.J. 19, 44 M.J. 272 and 44 M.J. 273. The questions are whether §323(a) authorized the Secretary of Transportation to appoint civilian judges of the Coast Guard Court of Criminal Appeals and whether these judges are inferior officers under the Appointments Clause. Two civilian judges previously assigned by the Department's General Counsel were formally appointed by the Secretary. The petitioners seek new lawful military appellate review; the Government seeks affirmance.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirms all six judgments | Parts I–II in full |
| Same opinion | Stone-Zsela | Souter | Agrees with all six affirmances | Part I statutory authority; not Part II's sufficient classification rationale |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Affirms all six judgments through a fuller institutional classification inquiry | Joins Part I; requires duties, jurisdiction, tenure and removal analysis in addition to a supervisory superior |

The statutory holding is unanimous. The combined-controls classification rationale has eight direct votes; Souter's distinct sufficient rationale is not an additional join in it.

## Form and assignment

`runtime/OT_1996CHUNK8_88_FORM.md` fixed one signed lead for all six petitioners before counting, with statutory and classification holdings and Souter's separate rationale. No division is needed. The Chief is in the unanimous judgment coalition and assigns.

Fresh receipt `freeze/OT_1996CHUNK8_88_ASSEMBLY_RECOUNT.json` recounts 75 qualifying earlier leads, including all four new Records and same-date alphabetically earlier Camps. Last counted: Camps Newfound/Owatonna, May 19, 1997. Counts: Stone-Zsela 7; Stevens 9; O'Connor 9; Scalia 7; Kennedy 9; Souter 9; Thomas 8; Ginsburg 8; Breyer 9. Eligible complete-lead authors: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; Souter's partial rationale join excludes him. Balance pool: Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg, at eligible minimum 7 or one above.

Stone self-assigns. His approved case-specific formulation supplies the complete separation of statutory authority, constitutional rank and the remedy that follows only from an established defect, with the exact combined hierarchy and reserved one-factor questions the eight-Justice coalition adopts. Personally stating that new classification while preserving the independent Article 66 remedy gives concrete doctrinal-precision value. Scalia is the strongest alternative at the same count and supports the same hierarchy; neither has a balance advantage, and no unresolved objection gives the alternative an expansion advantage. Thomas and Ginsburg also qualify but have higher counts. This choice does not rely on the Chief's office or the case's importance alone and changes no ground to secure authorship. Souter's separate writing does not count.

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 323(a), read with Article 66's provision for civilian military appellate judges, authorizes the Secretary of Transportation as department head to appoint the civilian officers serving on the Coast Guard Court of Criminal Appeals. Article 66 assignment is distinct from appointment, but no additional statute repeating the office's name in an appointment clause is required for these existing statutory offices.
**Controlling explanation:** The Court reads the two enactments together. Section 323(a) expressly permits the Secretary to appoint departmental officers and employees and prescribe their duties and powers. Article 66 establishes the military appellate structure and permits both commissioned officers and qualified civilians to serve as appellate judges. The former supplies appointment authority; the latter supplies the office, assignment and adjudicative responsibilities. The argument that general personnel administration cannot create judicial offices therefore misses both the genuine appointment language and the already established office. The Court infers no power to create unrelated offices or appoint principal officers.
**Holding and operative rule:** These civilian military appellate judges are inferior officers because the combined structure subjects their assignments and administration to the Judge Advocate General, permits removal from judicial assignments and prescription of rules, and places their decisions under superior review by presidentially appointed, Senate-confirmed judges; they lack final authority free of that system of direction and review. Their significant duties and protected adjudicative independence do not make them principal officers, and neither appellate review nor removal alone is held sufficient for every office.
**Controlling explanation:** The Court evaluates the actual relationship of this office to its superiors. The Judge Advocate General organizes the appellate court, prescribes procedural rules and controls judicial assignments and removal from those assignments. The Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces provides superior judicial review within the executive structure. Article 67 review is required for affirmed death sentences and cases the Judge Advocate General sends, and otherwise requires the accused's petition and good cause. Under Article 67(c), it may act only on the findings and sentence approved by the convening authority and affirmed or set aside as incorrect in law by the Court of Criminal Appeals, and takes action only on matters of law. In a Judge Advocate General referral, it need take action only on the issues he raises; on the accused's petition, it need take action only on issues specified in the grant. Those minimum issue obligations do not convert legal review into guaranteed plenary factual rehearing.
**Souter, concurring in part and in the judgment:** Souter joins the statutory authority holding and affirms every petitioner's judgment. He accepts a supervisory superior as necessary here but does not regard administration, removal and higher review alone as a sufficient classification inquiry. Under *Morrison*, the court must also examine the office's duties, jurisdiction, tenure and removal, with appropriate respect for the political branches' judgment in this close classification. The judges perform substantial, independent Article 66 review, but their jurisdiction and assignment-based tenure, the available removal and actual superior relationship, taken together, establish inferior status. His *Weiss* and *Ryder* positions preserve both constitutional appointment and meaningful adjudication; neither makes Article 67 a full factual rehearing or creates a blanket military exception. This fuller ground supports the same result without joining the Court's sufficient combined-controls rationale.
**Event and reach.** No. 96-262, encompassing Edmond, Lazenby, Leaver, Leonard, Nichols and Venable; argued February 24, 1997; consideration May 19, 1997. Each petitioner's judgment remains in the one matter. The challenge concerns the Secretary's actual appointments, not an assumption that the General Counsel's earlier assignments became valid by continued service.
**Coalition and scope.** All eight adopt E1, conclude these judges are inferior officers and affirm all six petitioners' judgments. Seven support the combined-supervision classification rationale stated above. Souter accepts the necessary supervisory condition but requires the fuller duties/jurisdiction/tenure/removal analysis and the stated political-branch consideration before reaching the same classification. Merely calling the common supervision rule institution-specific does not remove that join barrier. His distinct sufficient rationale must remain identifiable unless actual common text meets it and all proposed joins are independently supported in assembly. No ruling validates the earlier General Counsel assignments, expands §323 to principal offices, erases Article 66 fact review or directs fresh review after a lawful appointment.

## Edwards_v_Balisok_merits_1997-05-19.md

**Case and dockets:** Edwards v. Balisok; No. 95-1352.
**Event and date:** Merits decision — 1997-05-19; October Term 1996, chunk 8.
**Result:** Reverse the categorical procedural-claim exemption and remand for claim-specific dismissal and adjudication, 9–0.
**Version / lineage:** Initial decision; supersedes no completed adjudication. Durable file handoff under the user's no-Git instruction; no Git commit or hash verification is claimed.

## Event

Edwards v. Balisok, No. 95-1352. On review of the Ninth Circuit's October 5, 1995 judgment. Argued November 13, 1996; decided May 19, 1997. The question is whether §1983 claims alleging procedural defects in prison discipline may proceed when success necessarily invalidates an outstanding loss of good-time credits. Balisok lost thirty days of earned credits and alleges concealment of exculpatory statements, refusal of requested questions, failure to state supporting facts, deceit and bias. He seeks damages, declarations and prospective date-stamping relief, reserving credit restoration for habeas. The district court stayed the action pending state proceedings; the Ninth Circuit held procedural challenges categorically cognizable. The prospective request was not adjudicated below. The allegations of misconduct are not findings.

## Opinion Topology

## Event

United States v. Thomas. Merits decision, May 19, 1997. Argued January 15, 1997. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit, 74 F.3d 701, Nos. 94-6648 and 94-6649, the consolidated appeals of Robert and Carleen Thomas. The Court reviews whether offense-date 18 U.S.C. §1465 covers the charged interstate electronic image transmissions, whether receiving-community obscenity standards may apply, and whether venue lies in the receiving district. The Sixth Circuit affirmed the convictions. The operators ran a paid California bulletin-board service, screened membership applications containing addresses and telephone numbers, approved a Memphis inspector after contact, provided a password, and authorized downloads of charged images. Videotapes were also shipped to the approved recipient. Robert Thomas was acquitted of the child-pornography charge in Count 11; it is not a conviction under review.

## Event

Ayeni v. Mottola; Supreme Court No. 94-1202, captioned Mottola v. Ayeni on petition; 35 F.3d 680, Second Circuit Nos. 94-6041(L) and 94-6047. Argued and decided May 27, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit's September 12, 1994 judgment affirming denial of an individual federal officer's motion to dismiss on qualified-immunity grounds. The questions concern whether inviting nonassisting media into a home during a warranted search, and their observation and recording, exceed warrant authority and violate the Fourth Amendment; and whether the law was sufficiently clear when the alleged March 5, 1992 search occurred. The complaint alleges that Mottola admitted a television crew that recorded the family and private materials over objection without aiding the evidentiary search. The allegations govern this legal appeal and remain unproved.

## Event

Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque. Merits decision, May 27, 1997. Argued February 18, 1997. On review of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, No. 95-2009, 84 F.3d 1273. The Court reviews whether excluding the proposed religious film and literature from the senior-center program is viewpoint discrimination, whether a genuine subject or activity limit sustains the exclusion, and whether the Establishment Clause justifies it. The Tenth Circuit reversed City summary judgment, enjoined exclusion of the film and literature, awarded appellate fees and remanded for their amount. Albuquerque's six voluntary, nonresidential senior centers admit private lectures, classes and movies, including treatment of the Bible, religion and philosophy. Pastor Don Kimbro requested the film Jesus and voluntary distribution of giant-print New Testaments. The film ends with a conversion invitation and short prayer. The City applied its sectarian-instruction and worship ban; staff screened and sometimes stopped programming without written criteria defining that line.

## Clinton_v_Jones_merits_1997-05-27.md

**Case and dockets:** Clinton v. Jones; No. 95-1853; 520 U.S. 681 (1997).
**Event and date:** Argued merits decision, 1997-05-27; October Term 1996, chunk 8, inventory matter 92.
**Result:** Affirm the Eighth Circuit's judgment, 9–0; return for ordinary federal proceedings with particular accommodations when justified.
**Version / lineage:** Original decision.

## Event

Clinton v. Jones; No. 95-1853; 520 U.S. 681. Argued January 13, 1997; merits decision May 27, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit, reviewing its January 9, 1996 judgment, 72 F.3d 1354. Jones filed this federal and state-law damages action on May 6, 1994, alleging sexual harassment, assault, conspiracy and other injuries beginning in 1991 when Clinton was Governor of Arkansas. The allegations remain disputed. The complaint also includes post-inauguration statements attributed to the presidential press secretary.

The District Court rejected temporary constitutional immunity and permitted discovery against Clinton and Ferguson, but postponed trial until Clinton left office. The Eighth Circuit affirmed denial of immunity and removed the categorical trial stays. Clinton seeks dismissal without prejudice or postponement through the Presidency; Jones seeks ordinary proceedings. The question is whether separation of powers requires dismissal or postponement of this federal damages action based on alleged unofficial pre-presidential conduct, including whether the existing trial stay can stand under ordinary docket-control authority.

Length designation: Extended controlling explanations for Holding II, no temporary immunity for unofficial pre-office federal civil conduct; Holding III, official-act immunity turns on function rather than status; and Holding V, case-specific accommodation rather than a categorical term-long stay: up to 1,000 words each. Stone-Zsela's concurrence and Breyer's concurrence in the judgment are designated separate-writing summaries: up to 600 words each. Other controlling explanations: up to 350 words each.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Affirmance | All five parts, including the linked Ferguson remedy; eight rationale votes. |
| Concurrence | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins the Court and entire judgment | Proposed objective functional guidance and expressly reserved broader views, with no additional controlling rule. |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Entire affirmance | Constitutional protection against significant interference on a reasoned scheduling explanation, with the plaintiff-harm qualification; no formal Court-opinion join. |

## Form fixed before assignment

One signed Opinion of the Court follows plenary argument. The constitutional immunity, official-function boundary, integrated-order review and remedial questions constitute one argued case and one connected opinion. A per curiam disposition would not adequately identify responsibility for this reasoned resolution and its distinct published positions. The Court opinion has five parts fixed on legal grounds: I, confined review; II, temporary immunity; III, official-function boundary and reserved defense; IV, present trial stay and its derivative remedy; V, future accommodation and the post-decision standard. The Ferguson consequence remains a remedial paragraph of Part IV, not an independent immunity adjudication or separately assigned opinion.

The judgment coalition is all nine Justices. The Court-opinion coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg throughout. Breyer concurs in the judgment, substantively agrees with denial of automatic immunity and rejection of the unsupported stay, and preserves his additional Article II qualification without formally joining the Court opinion. His position is not converted into complete assent by the Court's constitutional-respect language.

The eight complete Court-opinion joiners are the eligible authors: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. Breyer's judgment-only relationship excludes him from full lead authorship. No split authorship is warranted because eight authors can carry every part. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela is in every judgment coalition and assigns. This legal form and eligibility were fixed before counts or authorship were considered. The assignment below accounts for all earlier qualifying opinions of this Term.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the constitutional protection of the Presidency concerns the exercise of its assigned powers. Fitzgerald protects official presidential decisionmaking from distortion by personal damages liability. The 1991 conduct alleged here preceded Clinton's Presidency and was no exercise of presidential responsibility. Adjudicating that conduct does not give the Judiciary authority to perform an executive function. Forrester requires attention to the function involved rather than the actor's title and recognizes the President's distinct official-function protection; Antoine likewise insists on a justified connection between immunity and the protected function, without deciding presidential immunity itself.
**Holding and operative rule.** The District Court must give substantial respect to the President's constitutional duties throughout this action; a party seeking protection must identify the particular litigation demand and the conflict or hardship, and any relief must respond to its supported need, duration and alternatives while accounting for the plaintiff's legitimate interests. The court may adjust timing, sequence or manner and may grant a justified, bounded stay, but its discretion cannot authorize significant constitutionally forbidden impairment of another branch's assigned functions.
**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the proper inquiry concerns concrete duties and litigation demands, not a general privilege of office. Articles II and III preserve each branch's assigned work. The Judiciary may adjudicate a private controversy without acquiring authority to obstruct the Executive's constitutional performance. At the same time, the President's title does not establish a particular conflict or shift to the plaintiff the burden to disprove it. The applicant must identify the need for protection. Landis places the clear-showing burden on the applicant when even a fair possibility of harm from a requested stay exists; that conditional burden is retained in its own terms.
### Assignment statement

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns the one signed plenary lead opinion to Justice Scalia. The judgment coalition and the five-part legal form were fixed before the count. No proposition, part or case was added, divided or combined for assignment purposes. All eight complete Court-opinion joiners can deliver the whole opinion; Breyer's judgment-only relationship excludes him from that assignment, and there is no reason to divide authorship.

The opinion-assignment count is recorded in [the assignment receipt](../tmp/Clinton_assignment_recount.json). It includes 79 qualifying opinions delivered before this assignment. Dates control, with same-date case names in alphabetical order. The last counted event is Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, May 27, 1997, delivered by Stone-Zsela. That peer is counted for assignment only; it supplies no intervening substantive law for this case. There are 79 counted opinions before this assignment. Authors come from the actual opinion tables rather than prior assignment totals. Summary and per curiam dispositions, procedural actions, source admissions and separate writings are excluded as individually identified in the receipt. No undelivered opinion is counted.

The eligible minimum is eight, so the pool includes every eligible author with eight or nine counted opinions: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. Fit governs; expansion is unnecessary because the eight share the exact bounded grounds, while the Court does not purport to resolve Breyer's further scheduling objection or secure an additional join from him.

The opinion's work is to identify the protected presidential function, separate that immunity from ordinary stay authority, confine appellate review to the integrated protection order and preserve actual-duty accommodation without inventing a universal procedure. Scalia's Forrester and Antoine joins, Nixon Court join, Plaut dissent and Weiss concurrence support precisely that distinction between constitutional allocation, official function and unsupported procedural enlargement. His commitment expressly accepts the required accommodation and every reservation. He can state the settled common grounds faithfully at the lower count. Thomas is the other eight-opinion candidate; his exact structural method also fits, but Scalia's recorded adoption of Forrester's directly relevant function/identity account supplies the more immediate basis for this opinion's central explanation. This is not a limitation on Thomas's capacity to write it.

O'Connor is the strongest alternative at nine because her Forrester opinion directly develops that functional method. The present coalition already agrees on its application, however, and no formulation obstacle requires her authorship; Scalia can carry that existing method and the same limits at the lower count. Stone's position also separates authority, process and relief, but his distinctive new classification guidance remains outside the Court's adopted rule. Self-assignment offers no necessary accommodation, coordination or precision advantage over Scalia for this narrower opinion. Kennedy's Public Citizen and Reich work likewise supports the distinction but identifies no unresolved objection requiring his higher-count assignment. The case's importance, historical authorship and stylistic reputation play no role. The opinion commands eight complete joins; Stone-Zsela and Breyer file the separate writings stated above.

## CompuServe_Inc_v_Patterson_merits_1997-05-27.md

**Case and dockets:** CompuServe, Inc. v. Patterson; 89 F.3d 1257 (6th Cir. 1996); Sixth Circuit No. 95-3452
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-05-27; October Term 1996, chunk 9.
**Result:** Affirmed, 9–0; the Sixth Circuit's reversal of the personal-jurisdiction dismissal and remand remain operative.
**Version / lineage:** Original decision.

## Event

CompuServe, Inc. v. Patterson; 89 F.3d 1257, Sixth Circuit No. 95-3452. Argued and decided May 27, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Sixth Circuit's July 22, 1996 judgment reversing a personal-jurisdiction dismissal. The questions are whether a continuing commercial relationship with an Ohio computer-network provider constitutes purposeful forum activity, whether physical absence defeats jurisdiction over deliberate electronic commercial dealings, and what connection those dealings must bear to this controversy. Patterson, a Texas individual doing business as FlashPoint Development, repeatedly supplied software to the known Ohio system, used its marketing and fee-collection services, and asserted common-law trademark rights against CompuServe concerning competing names and software. The District Court dismissed on a papers-only Rule 12(b)(2) motion; the Sixth Circuit found a prima-facie showing and remanded.

## Hincapie_Nieto_v_INS_merits_1997-05-27.md

**Case and dockets:** Hincapie-Nieto v. INS; 92 F.3d 27 (2d Cir. 1996); Second Circuit No. 96-4022

## Event

Hincapie-Nieto v. INS; 92 F.3d 27, Second Circuit No. 96-4022. Argued and decided May 27, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit's August 2, 1996 jurisdictional dismissal. The questions concern AEDPA §440(a)'s application to a pending petition, the completed-event limits on that application and the relation between withdrawal of ordinary appellate review and potential collateral protection. Hincapie-Nieto conceded drug and aggravated-felony deportability and received administrative consideration of §212(c) discretionary relief; the BIA affirmed denial on January 18, 1996. His February 16 petition was withdrawn without prejudice and reinstatement sought. The Court assumes the benefit of the original filing date. No surrendered colorable defense or developed separate legal defect in deportability is identified.

## Kinman_v_Omaha_Public_School_District_merits_1997-05-27.md

**Case and dockets:** Kinman v. Omaha Public School District; 94 F.3d 463 (8th Cir. 1996); Eighth Circuit No. 95-2809
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-05-27; October Term 1996, chunk 9.
**Result:** Title IX reversal/remand affirmed with instructions modified, 5–4; individual supervisory §1983 judgments affirmed, 8–1; District §1983 judgment affirmed, 9–0.
**Version / lineage:** Original decision.

## Event

Kinman v. Omaha Public School District; 94 F.3d 463, Eighth Circuit No. 95-2809. Argued and decided May 27, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eighth Circuit's August 26, 1996 judgment affirming the reviewed §1983 summary judgments and reversing summary judgment on Title IX. The questions concern the recipient's responsibility for teacher harassment, the relevance of Title VII agency principles, distinct personal and institutional constitutional responsibility, and same-sex harassment. Kinman attended Bryan High School from 1986 to May 1990 and alleges unwelcome sexual conduct by teacher Sheryl McDougall arising from the educational relationship. Reports, a requested classroom transfer, the timing and quality of investigation, actual knowledge and welcome are material. Later participation and a postgraduation relationship do not settle the initial welcome question. Reported evidence is considered under summary-judgment rules, not found true as a liability determination.

| Opinion announcing the Title IX judgment, Parts IIB–III | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice joins the reasoning; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer agree with the remand judgment on separate grounds | Remand judgment; additional proof and application reasoning lacks a majority | Stone-Zsela's awareness qualifications and recipient-specific further-review application |

## Suitum_v_Tahoe_Regional_Planning_Agency_merits_1997-05-27.md

**Case and dockets:** Suitum v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency; No. 96-243. OT1996, chunk 8, inventory No. 93.

## Event

Suitum v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency, No. 96-243. Merits decision, May 27, 1997. Argued February 26, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 80 F.3d 359. The Ninth Circuit affirmed dismissal of the regulatory-takings claim as unripe because Suitum had not tried to sell transferable development rights. The Agency determined that her Lake Tahoe Basin parcel lay in a stream-environment zone, denied the building permit and completed governing-board review; no further land-use procedure capable of allowing development remains. Transferable rights permit development elsewhere. The question is whether the unsold rights defeat the final-decision component of ripeness despite the fixed prohibition on development of this parcel.

**Case and dockets:** United States v. Hyde; No. 96-667. OT1996, chunk 8, inventory No. 94.

**Result:** Reverse the Ninth Circuit judgment and remand, 9–0.
**Version / lineage:** Original Run adjudication; no completed adjudication superseded. ## Event

United States v. Hyde, No. 96-667. Merits decision, May 27, 1997. Argued April 15, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 92 F.3d 779. After the Rule 11 colloquy, the District Court found Hyde's guilty plea knowing, voluntary and supported by a factual basis, accepted the plea and deferred acceptance of the agreement pending the presentence report. Hyde sought withdrawal a month later, asserting duress and false admissions. After an evidentiary hearing the court rejected duress and any fair and just reason, accepted the agreement, entered judgment on four counts, dismissed four on the Government's motion, and imposed a two-and-a-half-year term. The Ninth Circuit held withdrawal available as of right during the deferral interval. The question is whether accepted pleas remain subject to former Rule 32(e) while agreement acceptance is deferred.

|---|---|---|---|---|

## United_States_v_LaBonte_merits_1997-05-27.md

## Event

United States v. LaBonte, No. 95-1726. Merits decision, May 27, 1997. Argued January 7, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit, 70 F.3d 1396. The question is whether Sentencing Guidelines Amendment 506 conflicts with 28 U.S.C. §994(h) by excluding applicable recidivist increases from the career-offender maximum benchmark. The First Circuit affirmed George LaBonte's reduction from 188 to 151 months; set aside the denial of Alfred Lawrence Hunnewell's request concerning his original 188-month sentence and remanded for possible reduction; and vacated the sentence-related denial for Stephen Dyer, originally sentenced to 262 months, for possible reduction while affirming his separate non-sentencing §2255 dismissal. All three received the required statutory enhancement notices. David Piper was a consolidated lower-court companion, but is not a respondent in this Court and receives no mandate here.

## Vitronics_Corp_v_Conceptronic_Inc_merits_1997-05-27.md

**Case and dockets:** Vitronics Corp. v. Conceptronic, Inc.; 90 F.3d 1576 (Fed. Cir. 1996); Federal Circuit No. 96-1058; Patent 4,654,502, claim 1
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-05-27; October Term 1996, chunk 9.
**Result:** Affirmed, 9–0; the Federal Circuit's reversal and remand for infringement adjudication under the peak-reflow construction remain operative.
**Version / lineage:** Original decision.

## Event

Vitronics Corp. v. Conceptronic, Inc.; 90 F.3d 1576, Federal Circuit No. 96-1058. Argued and decided May 27, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Federal Circuit's July 25, 1996 judgment reversing noninfringement judgment on claim 1 of Patent 4,654,502. The questions concern the role of the patent's claims, specification and prosecution history, the permissible use of outside technical evidence and whether that evidence can displace meaning established by the patent itself. The District Court construed solder reflow temperature as liquidus temperature after hearing trial evidence; Vitronics conceded noninfringement under that construction, and judgment was entered without a jury infringement verdict on the reviewed claim. The Federal Circuit adopted peak reflow temperature and remanded. A separate jury verdict on Patent 4,833,301 was not appealed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Unanimous affirmance | Complete construction, evidentiary-use and remedy opinion |

**Controlling explanation:** Markman assigns legally operative claim meaning to the judge, including genuine construction-related technical and credibility disputes, while retaining ordinary infringement factfinding. The instrument supplies the public boundary; technical help can make its meaning intelligible rather than compete with it as a different source of legal rights. A blanket prohibition on consultation until the judge announces ambiguity would impair that explanatory work. Conversely, generic usage does not control over a different meaning demonstrated by the patent. Prosecution history was apparently not introduced, and its absence establishes no disclaimer or narrowing amendment. The Court prescribes no universal separate construction hearing and decides no general appellate standard for every subsidiary finding.
## Assignment

## Event

Arkansas v. Farm Credit Services of Central Arkansas, No. 95-1918. Argued April 21, 1997; decided June 2, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Eighth Circuit, 76 F.3d 961, which affirmed summary judgment holding four production credit associations immune from the disputed Arkansas income and sales taxes. The granted question is whether 28 U.S.C. §1341 bars their federal action. The associations are federally chartered, borrower-owned cooperative lenders suing in their own right. The United States is not a plaintiff; it appears as an amicus supporting jurisdictional dismissal. The associations expressly concede a plain, speedy and efficient Arkansas remedy.

## Boggs_v_Boggs_merits_1997-06-02.md

**Case and dockets:** Boggs v. Boggs; No. 96-79.
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-06-02; argued 1997-01-15; October Term 1996, chunk 9, inventory No. 102.
**Result:** Fifth Circuit judgment reversed and remanded: survivor-annuity preemption 9–0; pension-receipts, rollover-IRA and distributed-ESOP preemption 5–4.
**Version / lineage:** Initial merits decision completing the stopped event identified by the user as bd27cdc8; no prior Boggs judgment is corrected. Assembly uses the October 18, 2026 approved Version 2 supplement identified by the user as source commit 1ca4f0e9. Those identifiers are user-supplied provenance; no Git verification or repository commit is claimed.

## Event

Boggs v. Boggs, No. 96-79. Merits decision, June 2, 1997; argued January 15, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, 82 F.3d 90. The question is whether ERISA preempts the sons' state-law claims derived from Dorothy Boggs's asserted community interest in pension benefits, including Sandra Boggs's survivor annuity and property distributed to Isaac Boggs.

Dorothy died in 1979. Isaac married Sandra in 1980, retired in 1985 and died in 1989. Dorothy's will left Isaac one-third of her estate and the use and enjoyment during his life of the sons' two-thirds. Sandra brought the federal action for a declaration of preemption; two sons brought the state accounting action. The federal judgment rejected Sandra's position; it did not fix an accounting award or finally allocate each asset. The four claims concern pension installments paid to Isaac, Sandra's survivor annuity, the IRA funded by Isaac's lump-sum distribution, and distributed employee-stock-ownership-plan shares. The sons expressly claim a share of Sandra's annuity, including an accounting or equivalent lump sum; they have not abandoned that claim.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing or portion | Author | Joined by | Relationship and exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court: federal adjudicative route | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Eight direct joins in §1132(a)(1)(B) jurisdiction; Stone-Zsela agrees to reach the controversy without joining this ground |
| Same opinion: survivor-annuity protection | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | All nine join the §1055 holding and reversal of this component |
| Same opinion: pension receipts, IRA and ESOP claims, statutory qualifications and resulting merits mandate | Souter | Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Five direct joins in the antecedent-transfer ground and reversal of these three components |
| Same opinion: ancillary fee disposition | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Eight join the discretionary-fee explanation; Stone-Zsela agrees only with leaving the fee question for proceedings below |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | O'Connor, Ginsburg | Joins the Court's federal-route, survivor and fee portions; would affirm the other three preemption components subject to recipient-accounting limits |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins the Court's survivor portion; would affirm the other three components and preserve an accounting for Dorothy's community one-half; jurisdiction and ancillary fees are disposition-only agreements |

Every controlling proposition has a direct majority at the stated level of generality. No holding rests on combining the separate writings or on a narrowest-grounds rule.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the legal source of the asserted right matters here. Each claim rests on Dorothy's supposed interest in Isaac's undistributed pension benefits and its passage at her death. Describing the sons as heirs under state law rather than voluntary assignees does not resolve whether federal law permits that interest to be enforced. Section 1056(d)'s prohibition, ERISA's identified beneficiaries and its defined domestic-relations exceptions foreclose the asserted route on this record.
**Operative statutory qualifications and limits:** Section 1056(d)(2) excepts a voluntary, revocable assignment of no more than ten percent of a benefit payment and an irrevocable assignment or alienation executed before September 2, 1974, but not an assignment made to defray plan administration costs. A loan to a participant or beneficiary secured by the participant's accrued nonforfeitable benefit is not an assignment if exempt from the prohibited-transaction tax under 26 U.S.C. §4975(d)(1). None describes the transfer asserted here.
### Form, assignment authority and complete recount

Form was fixed before counting: one signed lead opinion resolves the federal route and four claims through separable portions; one Associate partial concurrence/dissent states U; Stone's separate partial concurrence/dissent preserves his distinct approved ownership rule and remedy. A single Justice among the five T adherents can carry every lead proposition, so the lead is not divided among authors. Separate writings do not count.

The Chief is in the judgment coalitions for the federal route, survivor component and consequential fee disposition and assigns those portions. He is not in the reversal coalitions for P/I/E; Stevens, their most senior participating member, assigns those portions and their merits mandate. Both assign to Souter. The Chief exercises no assignment authority over a judgment component he opposes.

The [complete mechanical original-table extract](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK9_102_V2_ASSIGNMENT_EXTRACT.json) and [fresh recount](../freeze/OT_1996CHUNK9_102_V2_ASSIGNMENT_RECOUNT.json) enumerate the source Records rather than importing their reported tallies. Only earlier event dates and alphabetically earlier June 2 cases count. Later-filed Records with qualifying earlier event dates are included; later event dates and later June 2 case names are excluded. The last event counted is Arkansas v. Farm Credit Services of Central Arkansas, June 2, 1997, delivered by Breyer. There are 89 qualifying signed plenary lead opinions and 90 delivering-author credits because Kinman has two delivering Justices. LAVAS, Lynce, Tyler Murray and Humane Society count as signed plenary threshold dispositions; Roy does not count because it is summary. A partial-join row for Stone in Robinette is not authorship. No stopped Boggs assignment counts.

For the survivor portion all nine are eligible; its pool is the same seven, with Stone and O'Connor at eleven outside it. Souter is in every applicable pool. Stone's fallback agreement supplies no eligibility for the route or fee rationale. His disagreement with T excludes him from whole-lead eligibility, not from his Chief assignment authority on the components he joins.

Fit, not expansion, controls. Souter's authored Travelers and Dillingham opinions perform the exact work needed here: distinguishing real statutory interference from ordinary downstream effects without converting either side into a categorical rule. His present T commitment requires that distinction at the difficult boundary between a disallowed prepayment title and an independent later obligation. Stevens separately chooses Souter for the T portions because Souter's own existing actual-conflict opinions and reconciled commitment permit a source-dependent holding that preserves the prior remote-effects boundary, an objective consistent with Stevens's Mackey distinction and current T ground. This models the assigning Associate's concrete legal priorities, not a Chief-imposed policy on the opposing component. The common choice respects the rule against splitting an opinion when one eligible author can deliver all its parts. Souter receives one additional count after this Record is written. ## Event

**Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.**, Seventh Circuit No. 96-1136, 90 F.3d 1237. Plenary merits decision, **June 2, 1997**; merits hearing June 2, 1997. Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254 of the Seventh Circuit's July 23, 1996 judgment reversing dismissal.

The questions concern citizen enforcement of EPCRA reporting deadlines after the reports were filed before suit; informational injury; redress through Treasury penalties and the other requested relief; and Article III jurisdiction before disputed statutory merits. CBE gave notice March 16, 1995. The Company filed the overdue forms before the August 7 complaint, which alleges no continuing or imminent reporting violation.

## De_Buono_v_NYSA_ILA_Medical_and_Clinical_Services_Fund_merits_1997-06-02.md

**Case and dockets:** De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund; No. 95-1594; 74 F.3d 28 (2d Cir. 1996)
**Event and date:** Merits decision, 1997-06-02; October Term 1996, chunk 9.
**Result:** Reversed and remanded, 7–2; ERISA does not preempt the reviewed provider tax, and the injunction is dissolved. Two Justices would first obtain supplemental jurisdictional briefing and argument.
**Version / lineage:** Original decision.

## Event

De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund, No. 95-1594. Argued February 24, 1997; decided June 2, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit, whose January 4, 1996 judgment, 74 F.3d 28, adhered to ERISA preemption after considering Travelers. The question is whether ERISA preempts New York’s Health Facility Assessment as applied to two New York diagnostic and treatment centers owned and operated by a self-insured employee welfare fund. The trustees seek declaration, injunction and repayment; federal tax jurisdiction must be addressed separately.

## Opinion Topology

## Doe_v_District_of_Columbia_merits_1997-06-02.md

**Case and dockets:** Doe v. District of Columbia; D.C. Circuit No. 95-7077; 93 F.3d 861 (1996); no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Event and date:** Plenary merits decision; 1997-06-02; October Term 1996, chunk 10, inventory item 108.
**Result:** Affirm the reviewed federal dismissals: CAPTA claim, 5–4; investigative-procedure due-process claim, 9–0. Preserve local certification and the distinct local-remedy due-process question.
**Version / lineage:** Original event record; supersedes no completed adjudication. Prepared from the frozen chunk-10 handoffs; no repository commit is claimed.

## Event

**Doe v. District of Columbia**, D.C. Circuit No. 95-7077; on review of 93 F.3d 861 (D.C. Cir. 1996). **Merits decision, June 2, 1997. Merits hearing: June 2, 1997.** On granted review of the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit under 28 U.S.C. §1254.

The District Court dismissed federal claims arising from alleged failures to investigate reports that a young child was being neglected before she suffered severe scalding injuries. The Court of Appeals resolved the federal claims and certified unsettled D.C. notice questions to the District of Columbia Court of Appeals. Review of dismissal accepts well-pleaded allegations; it establishes no preinjury notice, breach, causal responsibility, or damages.

The questions are whether CAPTA's report-triggered prompt-investigation command creates an individual right enforceable through §1983; whether the asserted mandatory funding obligation supplies such a right rather than only a public administrative duty; and whether mandatory investigative procedures alone create the claimed protected liberty interest when the injury preceded government custody.

| Partial joins in the Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Support investigative-procedure affirmance; oppose CAPTA affirmance | Investigative-procedure holding and preservation of certification and the distinct local-remedy question only. |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stevens | Stevens, Stone, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Would reverse the CAPTA dismissal and remand; agrees with the remaining disposition | Limited report-triggered statutory right, §1983 channel, and the unresolved elements and defenses on remand; no personal-immunity rule is decided. |

**Version / lineage:** Original decision.

## Event

Saratoga Fishing Co. v. J. M. Martinac & Co., No. 95-1764. Argued February 18, 1997; decided June 2, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, 69 F.3d 1432. The question is whether equipment independently added by an initial user remains other property under maritime products-liability law after resale with the vessel. Martinac supplied the vessel with Marco’s integrated hydraulic system; Madruga outfitted and used it before selling it to the corporation later called Saratoga. The circuit excluded the skiff, net, fuel, spare parts and miscellaneous equipment present at resale from the tort award while preserving other liability and damages rulings.

**Case and dockets:** Bracy v. Gramley; No. 96-6133.

## Event

**Bracy v. Gramley**, No. 96-6133. **Merits decision, June 9, 1997. Argued April 14, 1997.** On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit. The Court of Appeals decided April 12, 1996; certiorari was granted January 13, 1997.

Bracy challenges denial of discovery in his federal habeas proceeding following an Illinois capital conviction before Judge Thomas Maloney. Maloney was later convicted of taking bribes to fix criminal cases. Bracy alleges that Maloney biased unbribed trials to conceal that corruption and identifies connections to his own trial. The question granted is whether these specific allegations and the public corruption record establish good cause under Habeas Corpus Rule 6 to investigate actual bias. The grant does not encompass Collins's judgment or ultimate habeas relief.

## Gilbert_v_Homar_merits_1997-06-09.md

**Case and dockets:** Gilbert v. Homar; No. 96-651.
**Event and date:** Plenary merits decision; 1997-06-09; October Term 1996, chunk 10, inventory item 110.
**Result:** Reverse and remand, 8–1, on the initial unpaid-suspension ruling; all nine require an inquiry into prompt and effective process during the continuing deprivation. Stone would affirm reversal of summary judgment and remand.
**Version / lineage:** Original event record; supersedes no completed adjudication. Prepared from the frozen chunk-10 handoffs; no repository commit is claimed.

## Event

**Gilbert v. Homar**, No. 96-651. **Merits decision, June 9, 1997. Argued March 24, 1997.** On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit, whose decision was entered July 18, 1996.

University officials suspended a tenured police officer without pay immediately after his August 26, 1992 felony arrest. Charges were dismissed September 1. Officials received a supplemental police report September 11 attributing disputed admissions to Homar. A September 18 meeting occurred without disclosure of those attributed admissions or the report's receipt; by a further September 24 meeting Homar had read the report. Later demotion and backpay do not resolve the process questions presented.

The Court reviews the Third Circuit's reversal of summary judgment for the officials and its requirement of an advance opportunity to respond before this unpaid suspension. The questions concern the initial suspension and prompt, effective process during its continuation. For this case the suspension's interference with a protected property interest is assumed without deciding that preliminary issue.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal of the initial-process ruling and the continuing-process remand | Entire opinion. |
| Partial join in the Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone | Supports the continuing-process remand; opposes reversal of the Third Circuit's summary-judgment ruling | Continuing-process holding, including its scope and reservations. |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stone | Stone | Would affirm reversal of summary judgment and remand | Agrees that immediate duty removal is justified and that prompt meaningful later process is required; dissents from approval of initial nonpayment without a separately demonstrated urgency. |

## Johnson_v_Fankell_merits_1997-06-09.md

**Case and dockets:** Johnson v. Fankell; No. 96-292.
**Event and date:** Plenary merits decision; 1997-06-09; October Term 1996, chunk 10, inventory item 111.
**Result:** Affirm, 9–0, Idaho's dismissal of the attempted interlocutory appeal; substantive qualified immunity remains for state-court adjudication and ordinary final review.
**Version / lineage:** Original event record; supersedes no completed adjudication. Prepared from the frozen chunk-10 handoffs; no repository commit is claimed.

## Event

**Johnson v. Fankell**, No. 96-292. **Merits decision, June 9, 1997. Argued February 26, 1997.** On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Idaho.

Fankell, the employee and respondent, brought a §1983 action in state court. Johnson and the other official petitioners sought immediate appeal after denial of qualified immunity. The Idaho Supreme Court dismissed that appeal under its final-judgment rule without adjudicating immunity.

The question is whether §1983, the Supremacy Clause, or federal qualified-immunity doctrine requires a State to supply an interlocutory appeal despite a neutral final-judgment rule that leaves the federal defense available for meaningful adjudication and review. The underlying liability action remains pending.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Unanimous affirmance | Entire opinion. |

## Opinion form, assignment, and final joins

**Form determined before counting:** One signed unanimous plenary Opinion of the Court; no separate writing.

**Assignment countability:** Counted

Signed lead opinion after plenary merits review. The lead counts once for its author; separate writings do not count.

**Assignment basis:** Stone assigns as Chief in the unanimous judgment and rationale coalition. All nine support every governing proposition on the approved or frozen grounds and are eligible. The following assignment supplies the fresh sequential recount and selection.

**Assignment:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela assigns the single unanimous opinion to Scalia. The fresh per-Record counts immediately before assignment are Stone-Zsela 11, Stevens 10, O'Connor 11, Scalia 10, Kennedy 11, Souter 12, Thomas 11, Ginsburg 11 and Breyer 11. Last counted: Gilbert v. Homar, June 9, 1997. All nine are eligible for the complete opinion. The lowest count is 10; the pool is Stone-Zsela (11), Stevens (10), O'Connor (11), Scalia (10), Kennedy (11), Thomas (11), Ginsburg (11) and Breyer (11). Scalia's reconciled source-based position fits the actual work: explaining why the federal collateral-order construction of section 1291 does not become a state appellate command, while preserving substantive immunity and preemption limits. He carries the accepted distinction without broadening it into permission for discriminatory or destructive state procedure. The ground is settled; no new formulation secures a missing join. Stone's approved authority/procedure distinction fits as well, but Scalia can express it with a lower count and no loss of the operative protection. Its express reservation concerning discriminatory or destructive procedure preserves Scalia's, Kennedy's, Ginsburg's, and Stone's limits. Stevens and Breyer can retain their Behrens disagreement about successive federal appeals because this holding concerns whether the state must adopt the federal statutory vehicle. No material non-Stone position changes and no fictional circulation is claimed. Both the judgment and rationale have nine direct joins. No Marks issue arises.
